## A.21 Executive summary (max 750 words, approximately 1 page)
Kigoma is a region with a population of ~3 million people that spans ~37,000 km² in the north-western corner of Tanzania. It currently hosts >60,000 refugees from neighbouring countries, the majority of which are living in refugee camps of Ntida and Nyarugusu. These settlements — which were rapidly established in response to critical humanitarian needs — have added to the population pressures on the surrounding degraded agro-ecological landscapes.

*Statistics received via personal communication with the Kigoma Region Secretariat, March 2020.*

The project will address the urgent need for improved water and sanitation services in these settlements. The proposed intervention is a phased approach of establishing new boreholes and upgrading existing ones to provide safe drinking water to refugees living in camps. The rate of loss in these areas is less than the average rate of loss in the Kigoma region. To isolate the additional effect — or lack

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

Approximately 1% of the host community's population in the areas adjacent to the refugee camps and a substantial number of refugees are engaged in small-scale agriculture. As a result, they rely heavily on the goods and services provided by the ecosystems surrounding their settlements. These include timber for construction and fuelwood as well as rising temperatures and hazards such as erosion; long-term land degradation and the destruction of water resources; and increased vulnerability to pests and diseases. The effects of climate change have been exacerbated by climate change and further highlighted climate change as a significant threat to natural resources and their livelihoods. Other drivers of environmental degradation include population pressure, urbanization, deforestation, and agricultural expansion that is partly attributed to reduced yields from climate factors. Moreover, the enforcement of established land use plans has led to the destruction of forested areas in some villages, which are now being abandoned.

Community consultations highlighted that even for villages where Village Land Use Plans had been developed, village members were not aware of their existence or contents and therefore did not adhere to them. As a result, awareness among community members on land degradation was constrained. These development factors, together with the effects of climate change, have led to increased vulnerability in terms of food security, water availability, and disease, are placing considerable pressure on the region's natural resources. Projected climate change impacts will further exacerbate these pressures.

Historical climate trends for the country show that temperatures are rising and rainfall is becoming more erratic and intense, with Tanzania’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) highlighting the severity of climate-related disasters in the country. The average annual temperature has increased by 0.7°C since 1965, while the number of hot days increased over the long term (an average of +7.8 mm per year). Since 2014, rainfall has become increasingly variable and erratic with a decrease in the number of wet and dry months. The country experienced its worst flooding event in recent years during the 2015/16 rainy season, which was followed by the second-worst flood event in 2017. Both events were associated with extreme rainfall events.

Both host communities and refugee camps reported an increased susceptibility of water resources to contamination from human activities such as deforestation, urbanization, and agricultural expansion. The number of hot days has also increased over time, rising by 7% for the 1100-year flood event. Host communities reported exacerbated flood impacts in areas along the coast where there is a high density of settlements and infrastructure. In addition to flooding, there have been increases in surface water temperatures, which are now up to 38°C during the dry season compared with an average of 25°C. This has led to increased algal blooms and other aquatic pests that can be harmful to livestock.

In all three camps areas the main issue is storm water, not river flooding – causing gully erosion and landslides. The number of hot days in the region has also increased over time, rising by 7% for the 1100-year flood event. Host communities reported exacerbated flood impacts in areas along the coast where there is a high density of settlements and infrastructure.

The Tanzanian government has identified climate change as one of its major challenges. The number of hot days has also increased over the past year, rising by 8% per decade since 2000. This increase in temperature has led to increased evaporation rates from water bodies, which are now up to 38°C during the dry season compared with an average of 25°C.

The above-mentioned changes have exacerbated the existing environmental degradation and had profoundly negative impacts on the livelihoods of communities living around the refugee camps. These include reduced surface water availability, soil erosion and soil fertility. For example, as a result of the impacts of climate variability in the last 30 years, there has been an increase in the number of hot days from 15 to 24 per year.

Projections show that maize yields are expected to decrease by a further 17% because of climate change, with other crops also likely to be affected. The increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have already been associated with poor crop yields, and the increasing unpredictability and unreliability of rains has made planning for future agricultural activities difficult.

The Tanzanian government has identified climate change as one of its major challenges. The number of hot days in the region has also increased over time, rising by 8% per decade since 2000. This increase in temperature has led to increased evaporation rates from water bodies, which are now up to 38°C during the dry season compared with an average of 25°C.

The Tanzanian local communities living within 10-15 km of the camps. For clarity, in this project, the host community includes villages that have been identified as being affected by climate change and environmental degradation. The term host families refer to all those groups including agropastoralists that are not formally settled in villages.

**Page 8**

The proposed project corresponds to the climate change impacts affecting an already vulnerable and degraded landscape while considering the common needs of host communities and refugees in an environment where climate, human security, and development are interlinked. The project will be implemented in the districts of Kilombero, Katavi and Kagera, which host the refugee camps of Ntale, Nyungweju, and Mwanga. It aims to address the climate adaptation needs of host communities and refugees using an integrated landscape-level approach and promoting peacebuilding.

The project will contribute to making the transition from a) addressing villages and host community needs separately and not recognizing that they are part of the same integrated socio-ecological landscape; b) fragmented approaches to development, which do not address the underlying causes of vulnerability and conflict over livelihoods, and financial inclusion without integration; and c) promoting initiatives that are not informed by projected climate change impacts. The project will be implemented in a way that addresses the climate adaptation needs of host communities and refugees through adaptation solutions at the landscape level: 2) integrated interventions across sectors that are designed to address the underlying causes of vulnerability and conflict over livelihoods, land use, and water; and 3) where the people and landscape would be better able to cope with, and adapt to, climate shocks.

Moreover, the project has significant strategic contributions to environmental and social issues in the region by enhancing resilience through integrated landscape-level approaches. It will contribute to poverty reduction and promoting peaceful coexistence, lessening exposure of women and girls to gender-based violence while collecting sufficient data on the impact of climate change on livelihoods and ecosystems services at the landscape level. The project will also contribute to carbon sequestration through sequestration of 762.073 million tCOe and emission reductions of 3.2 million tCOe over 20 years.

All project interventions will be informed by comprehensively assessing and planning for land use, forestry, cooking energy, water resources, and other livelihoods in the landscape. The project will also contribute to climate change actions that complement the estimated US$45 million of development investments in the region that are addressing non-climate related issues.

To address the climate adaptation needs of the targeted beneficiaries, the project builds on bottom-up approaches, anchors to local governance processes, and promotes adaptive management in implementation. These project activities will be implemented through a participatory approach involving all stakeholders at different levels evolving policy environment. Ultimately, the project will contribute to: i) enhancing the resilience of individuals and households; ii) reducing vulnerability and conflict over livelihoods, land use, water resources, and other patterns of degradation of critical ecosystems and the services they provide; The project strategy is to ensure that the landscape-level interventions are designed in a way that addresses the underlying causes of vulnerability for host communities, including agro-pastoral groups that pass through the region as it faces challenges of climate change. This will be achieved by addressing the drivers of conflict over livelihoods and land use, water resources, and ecosystems and ecosystem services in the target host community and refugee areas. It will also have indirect upstream impacts on the broader landscape by supporting increased agro-ecosystem functions and services and policy interventions in Output 4.

The project will be executed by the Government of Tanzania, acting through its Vice President's Office (VPO) and the UNDP Country Office. The project will be implemented with the support of other development partners and agencies, including at the local level. This includes, the District Councils, the President's Office Regional Administration and Local Government (PORALG), the Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism, the Tanzania Forest Service, and other relevant agencies, local government authorities, humanitarian and development partners, and civil society organizations working in the region, promoting an approach that is participatory, gender-responsive and socially inclusive.

B.I. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

# Kigoma Region Context

Kigoma is one of the poorest regions in the country, with more than 32% of its population living below the poverty line, compared to a national average of about 15%. The region has a total land area of 4078 km² and a population density of approximately 6.9 people per km² (as of 2020). Kigoma is divided into three districts: Kigoma district, Mwamba district, and Nkungu district.

The region is characterized by generally low levels of economic development and its economy only contributes 1% to the national GDP. The population growth rate in the region is also higher than the national average (4.8% compared to 2.7%). This underdevelopment is compounded by the region's relatively high population growth rate of 4.8%, significantly higher than that of the rest of Tanzania (4.6 members). Contributing to population pressure in the region is the influx of refugees from neighboring countries, particularly Burundi and Democratic Republic of Congo.

The majority of these refugees are hosted in the Kigoma region, which is home to a population of ~290,000 refugees (as of September 2021). The largest refugee camps in the region include:

- **Makonde** camp in Kigoma district with a population of approximately 35,000.
- **Nkunda** camp in Kigoma district with a population of about 48,000.
- **Kibondo** camp in Kigoma district with a population of around 12,000.

In addition to these camps, there are also smaller refugee settlements and host communities. The presence of these refugees, the rising population of the region, as well as the continued subdivision of land within families have resulted in over-intensive agricultural practices which are placing increasing pressure on limited natural resources. These camps and their host communities are the target of this project (Figure 1).

Ecosystem services and ecosystem services

Kigoma Region is part of the Lake Tanganyika basin, a global biodiversity hotspot. At the east of the project sites are the Moyenzi and Kogas Game Reserves that have floodplain and wetland ecosystems that support large populations of birds and other wildlife. The western edge of the project area is bordered by the Mahale Mountains National Park in the Malagarasi-Moyowosi wetland system, is located towards the south of Moyenzi game reserve. Further south in the region are the Lake Tanganyika Wetlands which form a key component to the conservation programs for Jane Goodall.

The project areas of Kakoma, Kigoma and Kiponda Districts are characterized by mosaic landscapes or land area with patches of land cover types and land uses. In the project areas, these patches range from miombo forests, agricultural land, wetlands, lakes, rivers, and settlements (Figure 1). At the landscape level, multiple ecosystem services are provided including provisioning services such as food for humans and animals; regulating services such as climate regulation, flood control, erosion control, water purification; cultural services such as recreation and recreation; and supporting services such as oxygen production, biogeochemical cycling, and others.

Patches of forest nearest within the mosaic landscapes are mainly composed of miombo woodlands dominated by the genera Brachystegia, Julbernardia, and Pterocarpus. These forests provide a variety of ecosystem services including: as the woodlands provide food and cover for mammals such as elephants, wild dogs, and antelopes. These climax forest are also important in maintaining biodiversity, regulating hydrological cycles, maintain soil fertility, provide timber for tools, erosion control, and other uses. The miombo woodlands are also critical to the livelihoods of communities in East and Southern Africa.

Protection of miombo woodlands through community based forest management and planting of diverse native species can help to restore these ecosystems. However, when they have been degraded by human activities such as uncontrolled burning, overgrazing, or unsustainable land use practices, these interventions, these ecosystems are more vulnerable to unsustainability bushland farming, agricultural conversion, deforestation, and other forms of degradation. These changes in the landscape can lead to a loss of ecosystem services that are expected to be intensified by climate change. Degraded landscapes are more vulnerable to forest succession or reversion to grasslands. The miombo woodlands are also critical for maintaining biodiversity as per the IUCN/CCU also refer to observed transition of miombo woodlands into closed woodlands and evergreen forests.

Enhancing ecosystem services in mosaic landscapes are important in supporting resilience of both environment and people. For example, miombo woodlands provide a variety of ecosystem services including: food for humans and animals; for example, has the potential to provide bee pollination services, particularly from Miombo woodlands, and support natural pest control by providing habitat for insect predators such as birds and bats. Heterogeneous land cover help enhance resilience by maintaining high diversity of different functional groups and patches of native vegetation may enhance dispersal of beneficial organisms such as pollinating birds and insects by

# Agricultural practices and land use change

In Kigoma region, agriculture is the primary economic activity and accounts for ~40% of the total economic output. Agriculture in Kigoma is predominantly practiced by small-scale, subsistence farmers practicing low-input farming, with average farm sizes ranging between 0.2 and 2 ha. Uptake of agricultural technology in Kigoma is low, with 62% of all crops being produced using traditional methods. These include the use of animal manure as a fertilizer source, which limit agricultural efficiency and result in low yields. Seed quality is generally also poor and there is limited access to improved seeds. The main staple crop grown by small-scale farmers in Kigoma region is maize, which is produced because they are generally not affordable and input distribution networks are not well developed. Crop growth in Kigoma is also affected by the high levels of soil erosion due to heavy rainfall and poor land management practices. The main crops produced in the district include peas, coffee, sugar cane, cotton and tobacco. Agriculture is the primary livelihood activity in the region with 80% of households engaged in farming activities. The main areas where agriculture takes place are in the project target area. The main crops produced in the district are maize, cassava, sorghum, cabbage, beans and tomatoes [1]. In the project target area, most farmers grow maize as a staple food crop for their families. Most of these farmers also plant wheat due to its high demand in the region. Other crops grown include bananas, potatoes, onions, yams, sweet potatoes and cassava. Planting was observed, mainly involving banana trees, some small scale flue-cured plantations on farms, and natural and planted rubber trees.

Besides the small-scale agriculture practiced by host communities, agro-pastoral Sukuma people are also present in the project target area. They pass through the Kigoma region from the north in search of grazing land. Most of the Sukuma pastoralists are semi-nomadic herders who move their livestock to different areas within Tanzania as a result in environmental degradation such as land clearing for farming including in village and national forest reserves and overgrazing. The agro-pastoral Sukuma people have traditionally been excluded from participating in the community land use planning and enforcement undertaken by local authorities to date is the exclusion of (legal) pastoral communities from the project area. This has led to a lack of understanding between pastoralists and farmers on grazing processes due their transient lifestyle and not being part of the local communities’ socio fabric.

Farm activities are also taking place within the camps. Refugees grow crops (many vegetables) individually around their shelters, in community gardens or through the provision of food parcels from international agencies (IFD) and community gardens. The harvested crops contribute to meeting household food and nutrition needs and complement the cash income generated by refugees selling produce at local markets. In some cases, refugees sell surplus produce to meet basic household needs. Sales place directly among households on or the small scattered markets in the camps. In other cases, refugees share their produce with other families within the same camp through informal sharing arrangements. Under these types of arrangements, refugees may pay some money or provide another form of compensation for the produce they receive from others. In some cases, refugees sell their produce to host communities, refugee work on host community gardens and in return are given access to some land to cultivate. These arrangements are not formalized by any written agreement but are based on mutual understanding between parties. In other cases, host community landowners and their idle land to refugees to cultivate for free. In addition, Burundian refugees are allowed to grow crops within the camp areas in exchange of a small fee paid to the local authorities. In some cases, host communities and Congolese refugees is. It is important to underline that these arrangements are informal and thus do not conform with any legal framework.

Food security is a growing concern in Tanzania and while Kigoma currently has a surplus food supply, demand is increasing for food in other regions within the country as well as in neighbouring countries. Total grain equivalent for maize production in 2016 was estimated at 354,987 metric tons [2]. The total grain equivalent of cassava production was estimated at 1,000,000 metric tons [3], resulting in a total food surplus of nearly 950,000 metric tons [4]. However, future climate change conditions — in conjunction with demographic pressures such as high population growth — will erode any food situation Kigoma in the future.

The resilience and sustainability of the agricultural sector in Kigoma is also being affected by changes in food prices, which are increasing at a faster rate than wages. As a result of this, many small-scale farmers in the region and other countries decreases their agricultural productivity and results in food shortages, which are often augmented with supplies from Tanzania. Agro-pastoralism is the agricultural livelihood of western Tanzania, shortages in neighbouring

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 10

countries are experiencing demand for commodities from Kigoma. For example, conflict in Burundi has resulted in an annual food deficit in the country of approximately 350,000–450,000 tonnes, most of which is being augmented by increased imports of maize and wheat. In addition to these direct impacts on food security, climate change is also affecting these existing socio-political factors; climate change is resulting in reduced crop yields in South and East Africa. Additionally, the region has experienced two consecutive droughts in 2015–2016 and 2017–2018, which have affected maize production and impacting food security in the region[39][40]. For example, the occurrence of two consecutive droughts in Southern Africa during 2015–2016 resulted in a significant reduction in the supply of white maize from the region[41]. Consequently, global stocks of maize declined by more than 7% between January and June 2018. In Tanzania, the price of maize increased by over 30%, with wholesale maize price rose to about 32% above the five-year average – 10% higher than in June of the same year[42].

Increases in the price of crops result in reduced purchasing power of low-income households, inhibiting their ability to purchase food. In addition, the increased prices of staple foods have led to a reduction in the consumption of these foods by vulnerable groups such as children and pregnant women. Although there is a regional food surplus, these excess crops are not well distributed and are not nutritionally diverse. Moreover, the poor quality of storage facilities has resulted in the deterioration of some of this food stock. The combination of high food prices – in combination with the reduced agricultural productivity of soils in the region – result in the increased vulnerability of households to food insecurity.

Expansion of agriculture has been cited as among the leading causes of degradation in the region[43]. This is observed as farm productivity declines, particularly for maize. Vegetable production, for example, has expanded into valleys that were previously used for grazing livestock and growing crops. The expansion of agriculture into these areas results in communities competing for productive land and natural resources. The increasing reliance on these resources to meet the needs of a rapidly expanding population is leading to increased competition over these resources[44]. This will accelerate under future climate change conditions if crop productivity within farms does not improve. Conversion of land from grazing or forested areas into agricultural use has also led to soil erosion and loss of biodiversity, which in turn affects farmers. To supplement incomes, farmers and livestock keepers may also resort to selling charcoal and fuelwood in times of drought.

As regional prices rise, those within refugee camps are also directly affected. In refugee camps, food supply is influenced by market conditions outside of the camp and prices within the camps are always higher than in surrounding areas[45]. This is because refugees have limited access to markets due to their lack of income and purchasing power. In market districts within the camps, most refugees do not produce their own food and rely on food aid and trade with local vendors for their food needs. The prices paid by refugees are often higher than those in surrounding areas[46]. This further places pressure on aid agencies tasked with the provision of food aid in the camp. Refugees agencies rely heavily on international organizations such as WFP to provide food assistance, but these organizations have limited budgets from these organisations[47]. As prices rise in the region, the purchasing power of refugees is reduced more significantly than in surrounding areas, leading to an even greater reliance on food aid[48]. ## Pests and diseases affecting agricultural yields and human health

Several agricultural pests and diseases are periodically recorded within Kigoma. Examples of the most common crop pests and diseases as well as their occurrence severity are presented below in Table 1.

| Crop | Name of pest or disease | Occurrence severity |
|---|---|---|
| Maize | American bollworm | Average |
| | Bacterial leaf spot | Average |
| | Earworm | Small |
| Beans | Aphids | Average |
| | Ants | Small |
| | Down mildew | Average |
| Cassava | Angular leaf spot | Small |
| | Mosaic disease | Average |
| | Rust disease | Small |
| | Wilts | Average |
| Banana | Banana Xanthomonas | Average |

To date, evidence suggests that crop pests and diseases within Kigoma do not currently pose a considerable threat to the nutrition and food security of local communities.### Moreover, there have been no recent major outbreaks in the region. However, climate change is expected to increase the occurrence of these pests and diseases over the next fifty years or so. Climate change, however, has the potential to increase the occurrence of agricultural pests and diseases within Kigoma. The occurrence of these pests and diseases is influenced by climatic conditions, which is more pronounced under changing climatic conditions.

### A notable increase in stork blisters (Catalpa gigantea - known locally as Mipio) as a result of warmer temperatures.

An increase in the use of synthetic pesticides or insecticides in the control and suppression of pests and diseases poses a risk to human health. The use of these chemicals has been associated with increased cases of pesticide poisoning, especially among children. These cases have resulted in hospitalization and death. In addition, there is an increased risk of contamination of food crops such as maize, beans, millet, and vegetables and presenting a major public health concern.

### Inhabitants of the region are exposed to these chemicals through direct application of pesticides on commercial farms or domestic gardens. Consequently, these workers are more likely to suffer from acute intoxication and long-term adverse health conditions. Diseases and health conditions in farm workers have been associated with exposure to pesticides include dermatitis, neurological disorders such as Alzheimer's disease,

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 12

### Forest resources and deforestation

Western Tanzania, where Kigoma is located, is in densest forest than other parts of the country, with forest and woodland covering about 45% of its area. Across Tanzania, ~23% of land area is occupied by production forests used for, inter alia, charcoal production — and ~7% is classified as shifting cultivation, which contains forests in various stages of degradation. The rate of deforestation has been increasing over the past two decades. In Kigoma District, deforestation in Tanzania has increased from ~130,000 ha to 500,000 ha per year of forest loss per year between 1988 and 2007. The rate of deforestation in Kigoma District was estimated at 4% annually during the period 1988–2007 but has diminished only to 5.6% in 2010 from 23.2% in 1990, while cultivation increased to 17% in 2014 from 2% in 1990.

Most of the refuge and host communities in Kigoma live within or near forests (Figure 3), and while lower than the national average, the proportion of households using firewood as their primary cooking fuel is high. According to the Household Budget Survey 2017-2018 indicates that in Kigoma 97.5% of the population indicated to use fuelwood as the main source of energy for cooking and heating (compared with national average of 64%). The proportion of households using firewood is higher than the national average by about 32.8%. Use of solar for cooking was relatively developed in the region, with some 4.3% of households using solar as their main source of energy for cooking and heating (compared to a national average of 0.5%). The communities identified deforestation in the village and communal areas as a major environmental issue compromising livelihoods.

### Human disease epidemics

Human disease epidemics are a major concern affecting communities within Kigoma. Vector-borne and waterborne diseases are the most important climate-sensitive diseases reported in Tanzania. These include malaria, Rift Valley fever (RVF), dengue fever, leishmaniasis, schistosomiasis, and lymphatic filariasis. In Nyungweyasi the community reported the prevalence of waterborne diseases including diarrhea, malaria, Rift Valley fever, and schistosomiasis. The community also reported that there has been an increase in the incidence of cholera and urinary tract infections; the community believes are a result of deforestation and increased use of firewood. In Kigoma District, the prevalence of waterborne diseases is amplified during periods of severe weather, including floods and droughts. In recent years, the region had several outbreaks of malaria, RVF, dengue fever, and schistosomiasis. The community reported that in 2015 there were 4,528 RBM refugees communities**. The Kigoma region is additionally impacted by zoonotic* and vector-borne diseases, including HAT (HIV/AIDS), malaria, RVF, dengue fever, schistosomiasis, leishmaniasis, and lymphatic filariasis. Sickness is a potentially fatal disease transmitted by the tsetse fly, with most cases in Kigoma being contracted when infected individuals consume raw or undercooked meat from animals that have been infected with trypanosomes such as cattle, sheep, goats, and pigs. The communities also reported that there has been an increase in the incidence of cholera and urinary tract infections; the community believes are a result of deforestation and increased use of firewood. In Kigoma District, the prevalence of waterborne diseases is amplified during periods of severe weather, including floods and droughts. In recent years, the region had several outbreaks of malaria, RVF, dengue fever, and schistosomiasis. The community reported that in 2015 there were 4,528 RBM refugees communities**.

average daily consumption of wood fuel is ~1.8 kg per person**. Deforestation within the camps themselves is an acute problem that has left some areas “denuded” in Banyimbo and firewood shortages. Mendilli former camp has less deforestation than other camps, but still shows signs of degradation. This degradation is caused by deforestation, including the reduced provisioning of ecosystem services such as water regulation, food production, soil fertility and stabilisation, which further directly impact agricultural systems and threaten ecosystem-based livelihoods.

Forests within Tanzania are also increasingly becoming degraded by wildfires, with consequent changes in vegetation composition and structure. The most recent wildfire occurred on 20th December 2015 in the district ranking of area burned: Kilombero ranks 6th and Kaskazi 14th among the surveyed sites**. Most of the fires were caused by human activities, such as slash-and-burn agriculture, charcoal production, and grazing. The vegetation is starting to lose moisture and is more prone to combustion. However, as Tanzania’s climate becomes drier, wildfires are expected to become more frequent and intense in the future.

The impact of deforestation on ecosystem service provision by, inter alia, reducing soil organic matter which affects soil aggregation — one of the most important properties for maintaining soil structure and fertility — has a direct negative effect on agricultural sustainability. Moreover, fire-related impacts on Tanzanian ecosystems are expected to increase under climate change scenarios, with increased frequency and intensity of vegetation combustion, which will result in a shift in species composition because of greater fire intensity**.

As traditional agriculture becomes less sustainable and natural vegetation becomes less suitable for agricultural purposes, the need for wood fuel increases. This has led to an expansion of deforestation, resulting in reduced agricultural yield and fewer biomass stocks. To meet this growing productivity gap, host community and camp residents have been forced to expand their land use into areas that are not only more vulnerable to fire but also increasingly less suited to their needs, leading to significant deforestation throughout the Kilombero region.

The additional burden of the growing population, including through refugee influx, exacerbated by land clearing to remove vegetation for agriculture, has led to a rapid increase in forest loss. This is further compounded by the large and dispersed immigrant forest cover. During UNHCR and FAO field surveys, it was observed that wood collection and charcoal production are major drivers of deforestation within the Kilombero refugee camps**.

Between 2011 and 2014, Kilombero lost ~108,000 ha of tree cover (~4.3% decrease). Several studies have pointed to the rapid loss of forest cover in Kilombero as a result of deforestation, which is mainly driven by firewood collection and charcoal production. This has also created a complex heat island effect within the refugee camps, with temperatures up to 10°C higher than surrounding areas.

The majority of the additional forest loss after 2015 around the three refugee camps, however, occurred within the camp boundaries. It is likely that this initial acceleration of forest loss is attributable to the reestablishment and expansion of firewood collection and charcoal production in these areas following the closure of the Mitabila refugee camp in the mid-1990s for Mendilli and Nolubi, initially by the Government of Tanzania for the emergency settlement of refugees in 2015. The rapid deforestation within the camps is also attributable to the lack of alternative livelihood opportunities topographically and ecologically. As described below in Section B.3., the initial rapid settlement at these sites could not continue due to a lack of land availability, which has led to an expansion into areas that are more vulnerable to fire practices, which have led to maladaptation as a result. Since the camp began regrowing in 2015/2016, the forested area within ~3 km of six sites is still severely deforested, particularly around Nolubi and Kanyamwaji; in these areas, the soil has been eroded by fire.

### Effects of forest degradation

The effects of these pressures on the forest cover is a degraded landscape with low soil stability, reduced soil nutrient content and poor water retention capacity. These pressures have been exacerbated by the lack of investment in land use departments - involving forest guards patrol forest areas - have had limited success owing to limited resources and support. This has led to increased pressure from livestock grazing and agricultural activities which have further supported agricultural and other livelihood activities, less able to retain resources required for these livelihoods and even more vulnerable to climate change impacts.

### Vulnerabilities in the region

The following zones are most vulnerable in the region (Table 2) and aim to address these demand-side developmental drivers of degradation, whilst others provide support to the communities:

- Zone F. G. H.
- Zones 4, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11 and 13 in Nyangweso; and
- Zones 10, 12 and 15 in Manda.

### Surface and groundwater

Kilolo and Kibokono districts are part of the Lake Tanganyika Basin which is experiencing impacts of climate change on water resources through various ways, including a decline in the flow of major rivers and drying of the Kalukuba, Kiloko and Katisho wetlands in Kigoma Region. The national averages of Potential Evapotranspiration (PET) and Actual Evapotranspiration (AET) are 1800 mm/yr and 950 mm/yr respectively compared to 2300 mm/yr and 1460 mm/yr in Kilolo District, respectively. As a result, catchments are characterised by lower run off and higher evapotranspiration due to increased temperatures.

There is limited data available on actual flow rates of the rivers surrounding the camps and in the areas within. However, a reduction in surface and groundwater resources has been observed according to anecdotal evidence. For example, the levels of the rivers surrounding Ndutu, Ngorongoro and Mendelir former camp have fallen noticeably over time. In 2015, the average annual rainfall at Kigoma District Council (KDC) headquarters was 398 mm, showing a reduction of -7.5% from the historical climatological mean. Over this period, there has also been a statistically significant increase in the number of days with no rain and an increase in the frequency of dry spells affecting water resources. These climatological factors may have been responsible for some of the streams in the districts having become unreliable sources of water. The reduction in rainfall over time has also led to increased pressure on unsustainable pressure on these rivers and streams. As a result, in Kigoma district as whole, there is a heavy dependence on boreholes for domestic use. In 2015, the average annual rainfall at Kigoma District Council headquarters was considered very low with, medium, with only -20-100 mm annual recharge. Groundwater is, consequently, not a reliable source of water in the district and there are no major rivers or streams that can be relied upon for irrigation purposes. There are 3 boreholes yielding water. Moreover, there are now two 'formal' irrigation schemes (commissioned by the District and funded by the World Bank) serving Kigoma Districts.

### Soil erosion and soil fertility

Land degradation has been ranked as the major environmental challenge in Tanzania for more than 60 years [1]. Of particular concern is soil erosion, which has occurred over more than 61% of Tanzania's total land area over this period. The most significant areas affected by soil erosion are the Kigoma region and the western Arusha region, increasing to 224 tonnes/ha in both the 1980s and 1990s [3]. Erosion mostly occurs at the beginning of the rainy season when the ground is still wet from the previous rains. This is because the soil has not yet dried out sufficiently for cultivation, but because cultivated and natural vegetation are sparse after the dry season, resulting in reduced water infiltration capacity and increased runoff. The most significant areas affected by soil erosion are the Kigoma region and the western Arusha region, related to alterations in rainfall intensity as well as increases in the length of drought periods that decrease vegetation cover.

Although the refugee camps are topographically relatively well situated, Tanzanian authorities and camp planners were likely unable to take long-term development pressures and climate change impacts into account when designing [4] the sites. The high population densities within these camps have led to a number of environmental problems.

Within refugee camps in the Kigoma region, the high population densities in a small area and the associated human activities have resulted in soil erosion being one of the most significant environmental problems. Most houses are constructed with mud bricks that are excavated from pits within the camp. These pits promote erosion by creating bare areas on which wind and water can erode the soil. The construction of these houses also creates a physical hazard to the camp inhabitants and have the potential to increase the prevalence of water- and vector-borne diseases in the camps, as well as increasing the risk of landslides. In addition, the digging of pits for latrines has led to the development of gullies – which have the potential to cause considerable injury to members of the refugee community. Further land degradation is likely to occur within these camps, along with increases in the intensity of rainfall events, is expected to aggravate these impacts for refugees.

Outside of refugee camps, farming practices that lead to vegetation degradation contribute to increased soil erosion. This is because most farms are located on former forest lands which have been cleared and converted into farmland previously covered by forests [5]. Soils on these farms become shallower with a larger proportion of stone, which reduces their ability to retain water and nutrients. The use of traditional farming tools such as hoes and hand sickles and other tools. These tools do not allow farmers to plough deep enough, consequently leading to the formation of a horizon over the topsoil that is rich in organic matter. This horizon can be easily eroded by wind or water. In addition, these practices contribute to soil erosion because they cause the exposure of the soil to abrasion from water and wind [6]. These two environmental problems are likely to increase as climate change leads to more frequent and intense rainfall events.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

altering the structure of the soil and reducing its stability. Increasing temperatures and more frequent higher intensity rainfall events are likely to further reduce the stability of soils on agricultural lands, resulting in soil and nutrient loss through runoff.

Through the removal of topsoils in agricultural areas or localities where vegetation has been removed, soil erosion affects soil fertility levels, a factor that is already limiting in certain areas of Kigoma. Soil fertility is a product of the balance between nutrients added to the soil by natural processes (e.g., weathering) and those lost through runoff. Soil erosion related changes in climate will therefore increase nutrient depletion and further contribute to soil infertility in such areas.

Soil infertility resulting from nutrient depletion can be attributed to the breakdown of traditional farming practices and the low priority given by governments to agriculture in rural areas [1]. Over decades, small-scale farmers have removed topsoils through cultivation and other land use activities. This has resulted in soils with reduced organic matter content, insufficient amounts of manure or fertilisers. The community of Kalamunguhas noted the increase in land becoming unsuitable for crop production due to soil infertility.

The lack of nutrients in the soil also results from the application of low quality and quantity of fertilizers compared with neighboring countries using 8 kg ha^-1 instead of the 32 kg/ha or 29 kg/ha used by Tanzania.

In addition to loss of matters from erosion, irrigated cultivated lands which are poorly drained are impacted by increased salinity as a result of naturally high evaporation rates. This results in 27% of irrigated lands in Tanzania becoming unsuitable for crop production due to soil infertility and salinity [1]. The increase in the demand for water for irrigation demands for irrigation because rising temperature leading to higher salinity levels on cultivated land and further reducing its fertility.

Poor land use planning and degraded ecosystems in the areas surrounding the region's refugee camps are also interacting to increase the negative impacts of increasingly frequent floods. Flooding causes, inter alia, roads to become impassable, destroying infrastructure such as schools and health facilities, leading to increased child mortality rates and health and sanitation problems [1]. Floods also worsen soil erosion that reduces yields in kitchen gardens and community plantations. The increase in the demand for water for irrigation because rising temperature leading to higher salinity levels on cultivated land and further reducing its fertility.

The host community population in the project areas are composed of farmers and livestock keepers. Around 70% of the population in the region are engaged in small-scale farming, with others involved in livestock keeping. The current lack of access to water for irrigation is a major constraint to increasing agricultural productivity [1]. The lack of land assigned for grazing could also lead to encroachment of villages and natural forest reserves outside camps and villages.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

The recently concluded community consultations in September 2020 have given more insights on the interactions between host communities and refugees. Both communities generally remarked that relations between the communities are improving, but there is still a need to address some of the existing challenges related to land tenure arrangements with host communities as agriculture laborers or through land lease/land use arrangements.

Despite good relations in general, there are some conflicts between host communities and refugees. The typology of local conflicts within host community groups and between refugee groups, host communities and other beneficiary groups are not uniform across the region. Some of the most common conflict types that have been reported include tensions around:

- benefit sharing on agricultural production on land informally leased to refugees;
- concerns of responsibility for protection by host communities in upland areas and benefit and pollution from livestock grazing;
- competition over access to water sources; and
- gender-based violence experience during foodcollection.

Existing mechanisms to manage conflict include peaceful co-existence meetings that are known to work in resolving inter-communal conflicts.

## Market conditions and available financial services

The climate-vulnerability of smallholder and refugee farmers in Kigoma is determined by their strong reliance on rain-fed agricultural systems which, under anticipated precipitation changes, are expected to become further exposed. For example, the projected increase in temperature will lead to a decrease in soil moisture content, thus limiting climate-smart agricultural practices and the subsequent application of supplemental modes of production. These farmers also face limited access to financial services that could help them diversify their livelihoods or provide alternative income opportunities to counteract the potential loss of crop production.

The limited availability of financial services in the region has exacerbated the difficulties regarding the uptake of climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies. Smallholder farmers, particularly those communities in the Kibondo, Kakumio and Kasulu Districts are reliant on Savings Associations (SAs) to manage their finances, in the absence of formal banking services. However, these SAs have been unable to provide adequate financial services due to limited resources available for the financial contributions and gains generated from SA's. Women tended to be better represented in SA's in host communities compared with refugee groups. The lack of access to financial services has also led to a low demand to provide saving and loan facilities. Levels of capital in single SAs are generally extremely low, and 25–30 SAs may not have any savings at all. This is further compounded by the fact that most SAs are unable to provide loans to communities as part of the Kigoma Joint Programme. There is significant potential for SA's to be used to scale-up climate-smart agricultural practices in host and refugee communities, particularly through the provision of credit facilities and the uptake of water-efficient technology. For more information on the role of SAs in the region – as well as an overview of the Kigoma Joint Programme project delivery structure – please refer to Section 3.1.2 of the Feasibility Study (referred to as "The Savings Associations").

## Historic and Projected Climate Change

Tanzania's climate is highly variable, but climate trend data already indicates that temperatures are rising. Year-on-year rainfall variability has also increased with the rate of pre-potential evaporation increasing. The sections below discuss these historical changes and projections in the project sites in detail.

### Observed temperature and rainfall change and impacts

Historical climate trends for the country indicate that temperatures have risen from 1960–1990. The long-term trend shows a significant increase of approximately 0.5°C, with the highest maximum temperatures as well as the highest maximum temperatures. This increase is –0.18°C per decade from 1983 to 2017. The average annual rainfall has also increased by about 4% over this period. However, the 90th percentile maximum temperature has increased from the 1960s by more than 6%. Analysis of both the day-

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 19

### Historical climate trends for the country indicate that rainfall is becoming more erratic. Over the past three decades, Tanzania has experienced a general decrease in precipitation and an increase in temperature. This has been accompanied by abnormally lower precipitation for several years. Annual rainfall in Tanzania has decreased at an average rate of 3.3% per decade since 1960 (Figure 5). In addition to this overall trend towards decreasing precipitation, there are noted general decreases in precipitation elsewhere in the country, precipitation in the regions of Nduwa and Pongweyabo have decreased by approximately 27% over the past three decades. The average annual rainfall has increased by ~7.5 mm/decade since 1901. The most prominent observed climate change trend has been a tendency towards lower rainfall volume during the early growing seasons (October to December) and increases later in the season.

The increase in precipitation volumes is occurring in the presence of increasing temperatures. This has the effect of enhancing evapotranspiration, which results in an overall decrease in SPEI over time. The Standardised Precipitation-Exapotranspiration Index (SPEI) drought index classifies years from < -3 representing extremely wet and > 2.5 representing extremely dry conditions; with values between ~0.5 < 0.5 representing near normal wet and dry respectively. There is a general decrease in SPEI over time (Figure 1). While there are some exceptions to this trend, the overall pattern indicates that for most years in the future, the long-term trend indicates a general decrease and leaning towards the more dry categories as a result of increasing temperatures and decreasing precipitation volumes. This will have an impact on both the SPEI wet and dry years. This will result in wet years that are more moderate and dry years that are more severe.

### Figure 5. SPEI 48 month rolling variability magnitude over time (left), simplified SPEI wet and dry magnitude.

The historical changes in the climate (increased temperature and more intense precipitation) have already had noted impacts on communities, as evidenced by the Tanzania Climate Vulnerability Profile (TCVP) and the UNDP/UNCRS database records events and their impact on local populations. While these impacts are not spatially explicit, they do demonstrate an increasing trend of communities being affected by rainfall-related hazards (Figure 6).

Observational climate change control site To compare the rainfall patterns themselves — of the refugees on the area surrounding the camps, a control site at ~85km south of Nyungwe was selected for comparative purposes. The town of Uwinka and its surrounds have similar rainfall patterns to those experienced by the refugee camps in the north of Nyungwe. Both areas are located within the same climate zone as the camps in this area. The control area has experienced a similar change in observational climatic, with notable increases in both daily minimum temperature and average monthly maximum temperatures over time. The control area has also experienced an increase in total precipitation over time, as well as a strong decrease in SPI magnitude as a result of increasing rainfall variability.

Despite these similar physical and climatic conditions, the forest loss around the control site is significantly higher than that surrounding the refugee camps. This loss was, on average, ~850 ha per year between 2001 and 2018, although this rate has slowed in recent years. The most significant difference in forest loss rates can be seen to occur in the areas surrounding the control site. The cumulative NDVI surrounding the control villages shows losses similar to those experienced by the refugee camps. However, close to Meru, the forests of Nyungwe and Ngoro-Ngoro have had even less forest loss.

Although the drivers of deforestation are a challenge to attribute with certainty, given the similar climatic factors and basic topography between the two areas, the results from this study suggest that climate change does not appear to have resulted in greater degree of deforestation compared with the control site of Uwinka. This reinforces the findings of the land cover changes for the refugee camps themselves.

Projected temperature and rainfall change

Average maximum temperatures (currently ranging from 27–31°C at Kigoma region) Figure [2] are likely to increase under both RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 scenarios, with increases under both RCPs of ~0.9°C for the average maximum temperature and an increase of ≥2.5–4.9°C in the hottest month of the year from 2031 onwards.

Average minimum temperatures at 14°C for the region will increase by ~1.1–1.7°C by 2050 and by 1.3–2.3°C by 2070 for RCP4.5 and 8.5, respectively. These increases will with both day- and night-time temperature profiles away from cooler temperatures in the future.

The frequency of extreme rainfall events is expected to increase under both scenarios. The number of days when daily precipitation exceeds 10 mm per day is likely to increase by ~2–3 days per year for RCP4.5 and 8.5 by 2050, with an increase to ≥39–67 days per year from 2031 onwards.

The number of days when daily precipitation exceeds 50 mm per day is likely to increase by ~1–2 days per year for RCP4.5 and 8.5 by 2050, with an increase to ≥29–32 days per year from 2031 onwards.

By the 2050s, annual rainfall is projected to increase in Kigoma by -7...9%. Annual projected precipitation changes in this region show general increases for both 2040-2060 RCP4.5 (Figure 8, bottom left) and 2060-2080 RCP8.5 scenarios. The former shows an increase of 13 mm annually over the entire Kigoma area with a maximum increase of 79 mm for Mendi and Ntuba areas by 2060. The latter have greater capacity for cloud formation and chance of rainfall in the future, leading to increases of up to 45 mm annually across the region. These increases are more pronounced under RCP8.5 with increases in the Kipasi area of 43 mm and 49-64 mm annually in the Kakonko and Kobondo areas. These increases will be accompanied by a decrease in the length of dry spell duration because of the anticipated increase in total annual precipitation becoming more focused within a shorter period, leading to an increase in the frequency of heavy rainfall events that can lead to flooding. Hourly peak precipitation intensity** will likely increase by up to 9% and 13% under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 respectively, with a shift towards a more frequent occurrence of a higher profile, resulting in heavy and fast-occurring rainfall which is likely to increase the frequency and intensity of floods.

While the projected change in total annual rainfall by the 2050s is small, seasonal fluctuations are larger, with increased variability in the early onset of rainfall events from October to December (-9% under RCP4.5 and 12% under RCP8.5 for the period March to May) and a shift towards a more frequent occurrence of a higher profile.

Higher average and extreme temperatures will enhance evaporation and evapotranspiration from the earth's surface, leading to increased water demand in agriculture and other sectors. This will likely lead to an increase in dry spells and extreme rainfall events that can lead to flooding. Hourly peak precipitation intensity** will likely increase by up to 9% and 13% under RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 respectively, with a shift towards a more frequent occurrence of a higher profile, resulting in heavy and fast-occurring rainfall which is likely to increase the frequency and intensity of floods.

#### Climate impacts
Climate change—in combination with non-climate drivers such as agricultural practices and land use changes—has already altered ecosystem functioning and affected livelihoods in the Kigoma region. The local-level impacts of climate change are expected to be amplified by feedback mechanisms over the long term, interacting on socio-ecological systems, creating feedback loops that amplify climate hazards and increase vulnerability.

Temperature and precipitation changes interact to amplify impacts. The potential failure of early season rainfall increases the likelihood of severe and prolonged droughts. At the same time, floods are expected to increase in frequency and intensity due to more frequent heavy rainfalls and increased runoff from land use change and urbanization.

### Increase with global climate variability. A climate risks remain unabated, aggregate models estimate that the associated economic costs may be equivalent to as much as 2% of GDP per year by 2050.

The impacts of climate change on livelihoods are not only limited to the direct effects of climate change is an impact on agricultural productivity and consequently food security. In addition to diminishing the critical ecosystem services provided by forests, wetlands, and other natural resources, climate change also has a significant indirect effect on agriculture. The impacts of climate change on livelihoods in general decline in agricultural productivity and agro-diversity. Indeed, climate variability is already having an impact on crop yields and livestock production. This is due to the increased incidence of pests and diseases. Other crops have also been in decline. For example, a 50% decrease in productivity of bananas and plantains has been observed in some areas. The increase in temperature and rainfall patterns are conducive to the spread of pests and disease, but also because of insufficient rotation of these crops resulting in inadequate return of nutrients to the soil. This is leading to a decline in yields and an increased use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides which in turn puts greater pressure on land used to farm these crops. The impacts of our intensive agricultural practices have been further exacerbated by climate change. In addition, the increase in temperature has led to an increase in evaporation rates, a factor directly related to population growth. This is resulting in arable land being exposed for marginal areas. These are areas that were previously not used due to lack of water and other resources. As the demand for food continues to grow, this change is becoming more prevalent and will become increasingly severe under future climate change scenarios.

### To address these challenges, it is essential to adopt a multi-faceted approach which includes improving agricultural systems and diversify the variety of crops grown to meet the nutritional requirements of the growing population. To achieve this, it is necessary to improve the resilience of livelihoods by adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices. This will reduce the vulnerability of these systems to the impacts of climate change by adapting climate-resilient agricultural practices.

Climate change is particularly likely to affect resource poor households such as smallholder local farmers and rur-geese farming communities in the region. These groups are more vulnerable because they have limited access to resources, information, and technology. They will likely transfer this pressure and cause greater reliance on the local ecosystem. Further accelerating degradation of natural resources and increasing vulnerability to climate change.

### The impacts of climate change on livelihoods can be seen in the refugee camps in Burundi. These increases in precipitation intensity will further exacerbate these impacts. Densely populated flood-prone areas are already vulnerable to flooding, but the frequency and intensity of such events is expected to increase as a result of climate change. This has occurred on slopes as observed in the project area. High intensity rainfall events following long dry spells will further compound this problem. The increased precipitation also increases the risk of landslides which can destroy infrastructure and the landscape will be soil-saturated, reducing soil nutrient availability and reduced soil moisture. These impacts compound with other factors such as deforestation, overgrazing, and poor land management practices. This results in a decline in crop yields and other livestock activities, less able to retain water required for these livelihoods, and are more susceptible to flooding.

The provision of the landscape to continue to provide ecosystem services (such as flood attenuation) under climate change conditions will also be affected by other climate hazards, including fires. Although increased incidences of fire in the region have been observed over recent years, there is no evidence that this has been caused by climate change. However, with the climate becoming hotter and drier, the frequency and intensity of forest fires are expected to increase. This will result in a decline in ecosystem services such as flood attenuation and water retention.

In 2015, cholera affected refugees and host populations in four of Kigoma's districts, including Ujiji, Kimata, and Katete. In total, there were 346 cases with 8 deaths reported from these districts. The majority of the cases occurred among persons and 4,528 in the refugee communities. Of the total cholera cases in 2015, 61 resulted in deaths. From consultation with LWF/UNHCR staff, it is believed that the high number of deaths was due to delayed treatment.

Cattle in Kigoma are susceptible to enteric nematodes, particularly populations that are near the Mafinga forest reserve and the Lake Tanganyika shoreline where there is a high prevalence of these parasites. In 2015, 34% of cattle sampled from the refugee camps were infected with enteric nematodes. The prevalence was even higher in cattle sampled from surrounding Kigoma's refugee camps.

In addition to socioeconomic conditions in the area, the lack of financial services and credit facilities is a major constraint on smallholder farmers that may have a reduced ability to mitigate, cope with, and recover from climate shocks and to sustain their livelihoods.

Other climate change impacts include flood impacts, heat stress, reduced water availability and function of crops to agro-ecological zones and ecosystems. These climate impacts interact with structural development challenges, such that the region is vulnerable to both climate change and structural development pressures (e.g., increasing food demands) into the region. Many of these structural development challenges are being addressed through comprehensive policy and institutional reforms.

## Investments in the Project Area

This project aims to build upon the ongoing development and humanitarian work that has, and is, being conducted in Kigoma to address the outstanding challenges through an integrated approach based on land use planning – namely, a "no-regrets" approach. The no-regrets approach is one of the most important strategies for climate change adaptation and maximise co-benefits -a "no regrets" approach has been applied in the project design. Through this approach, measures are designed to be robust enough to withstand future changes in climate and other environmental pressures while maintaining the maximum potential for on-going adaptation. This strategy is integrated into all of the project's investments and interventions. The no-regrets approach will also help to reduce vulnerability by reducing dependence on the surrounding ecosystems. Globally the no regrets approach has shown to increase resilience and improve livelihoods, particularly in vulnerable communities. The no-regrets approach is one that ensures that what it works at the integrated landscape or "whole system" level. Although the parallel financing and other projects have been designed with a no-regrets approach, this project will be the first to implement such an approach on a large scale. The project will contribute to addressing this through the integrated land use planning in Output 1, which will further be scaled up by the KIP and other partners. This will ensure that all investments and supporting mainstreaming to address more systemic coordinated issues across sectors and institutions.

In addition to committing co-financing to the project through its natural resources and shared environment programme, UNICEF is also committed to providing technical assistance in the next few years in the largest refugee hosting area that will complement the GCF project activities, especially in addressing structural drivers of environmental degradation. The KIP has been designed with a phased approach, which allows for implementation at different stages and are therefore indicative of the interventions the project will coordinate with. These investments are in addition to the national, regional and district allocations towards energy and environment interventions within the refugee hosting areas.

Section 2.5 of the GCF provides detail on the current use of fuel wood in Kigoma that the project aims to address. Presently, the main sources of fuel wood are from forests and shrublands, with some contribution from charcoal and other options. Currently, fuel wood extraction accounts for 60% of forest resource usage. The proposed project will be implemented through a phased approach, which allows for implementation at different stages and is designed to additionally provide important biodiversity and ecosystem services – towards faster growing species planted on degraded land. This project will also focus on environmental and forest protection, addressing some of the supply side issues highlighted above.

Several initiatives and projects are underway or planned to provide longer-term solutions and interventions to address demand-side problems of fuelwood extraction for heating and lighting in refugee camps and host communities. These include the implementation of solar cookers and improved stoves, as well as the promotion of alternative energy sources such as UNHCR initiatives that aim to reduce fuelwood demand. A new project funded by Denmark and to be implemented by the KIP will focus on the supply side of fuelwood extraction in refugee camps and host communities. The project will also focus on environmental and forest protection, addressing some of the supply-side issues highlighted above.

The project is expected to have significant impacts on agricultural production and livelihood systems in Western Tanzania. As noted, some of the above investments and projects are still in discussion and negotiation stages and are only indicative of interventions the project will potentially contribute with. However, the table clearly illustrates that baseline data on forest cover, land use change, soil erosion, water availability, and other environmental indicators (e.g., climate change) are being addressed by development and humanitarian financing. As environmental degradation in relation to these indicators is a major concern for the region, it is expected that additional investments will be initiated by the Government with other funding sources during the course of the GCF project implementation.

Table 2 does not include development financing allocated through district, regional, and national government programs and projects. The proposed project interventions will build on government policies and actions on environment and forest management and will closely complement and reinforce government expenditures, plans, and priorities.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### Problem Statement

Kigoma is experiencing rising temperatures as well as increasing rainfall intensity and variability, which will contribute to an increase in the frequency and severity of floods, droughts, heat stress and fires. Observed changes in weather patterns are expected to have a significant impact on agriculture, livestock production, water availability, and forest management activities. These changes will also affect agricultural productivity by reducing crop yields through increased pest and disease outbreaks, reduced soil fertility, and increased surface- and groundwater salinity. The projected increase in temperatures is likely to exacerbate these impacts. These changes are threatening local ruralfied and ecosystem-reliant livelihoods in the region.

Climate hazards interact to produce impacts on ecosystems and agriculture that include: (i) soil erosion – and subsequent loss of topsoil, reduced water infiltration capacity, and increased runoff; (ii) reduced water availability; (iii) proliferation of pests and diseases; and (iv) geographical shifts of agro-ecological zones and ecosystems. These hazards are likely to exacerbate the impacts on livelihoods in Kigoma District, which is a semi-arid and pressure landscape. These hazards and impacts are pulling local livelihoods at risk and underpinning the capacity of households to adapt to climate change.

The effects of projected climate changes on the agro-ecological system compound existing stresses from other drivers such as population growth, urbanization, land use change, and market-driven demand for agricultural products. These drivers themselves, Moreover, development (non-climate) impacts on ecosystems – which are likely to be exacerbated by climate change – will further reduce ecosystem services that are critical for food security, water supply, and host communities and refugees’ limited capacity to access financing, technology and information for adaptation.

11) This estimate is based on consultations with the Kigoma District Environmental Officer and Tanzania Forest Service that indicate that the Abanda district annual environmental operating budget covering reforestation, soil conservation, solid waste management and similar activities normally stands at TSh 300,000.00 or around USD 125.43 per year. For this budget has been allocated to the project as a baseline estimate of government contributions. The actual amount is likely to be higher in Kigoma, whereas for Kasaui, the TBF budget is estimated to be slightly higher at TSh 300,000,000 per year. These figures were also used by the Government of Tanzania as a baseline estimate and discussions are ongoing with regional authorities to provide more accurate estimates of projected government contributions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 31

B

Insufficient access to natural resources and diminishing ecosystem services contribute to conflict both within and across refugee and host communities. Accessing resources and inputs for adaptation is critical for maintaining livelihoods and improving food security in the face of climate change. However, this is often constrained by inadequate land use planning and livelihood activities in refugee camps and host communities. In the absence of the intervention, the vulnerability of these populations to climate change will increase, leading to further conflict over resources and contribute to — the impacts of climate change on the agro-ecological system. Given the target districts' reliance on agriculture as a primary source of income and livelihoods, any reduction in agricultural productivity by limitations on their livelihood activities, this will threaten the livelihoods and lives of most people in the project area.

While other activities in region are addressing discrete elements of these problems, there is limited landscape-level consideration for how climate change impacts will be addressed. This proposal addresses these concerns having limited consideration for the long-term impacts of climate changes or have insufficient scale to trigger a positive feedback loop.

## Preferred Solution

The theory of change for this project (Figure 10 below) has the following goal statement:

IF ecosystem-based adaptation is integrated into land use planning and development and humanitarian policies, key ecosystems restored/sustained managed and resilient livelihoods practiced. THEN climate-resilient sustainable development will be achieved in the target districts because of enabling policy and planning processes, healthy ecosystems and improvements to food security.

The project interventions aim to disrupt the abovementioned feedback loops by strengthening community capacity and improving access to resources and inputs for adaptation. This will enable communities to adapt to ongoing and future climate change impacts and build resilience to shocks and stresses. The project will also strengthen institutional systems will be strengthened and ecosystems and livelihoods resilience will improve among host communities and refugees in the target districts. In addition, the project will ensure that local communities have the resources and knowledge to undertake adaptation activities, and local capacity will be created to accommodate new climate-resilient livelihoods.

In order to complete participatory climate resilient land use planning and related environmental management, the project will strengthen district and community planning and management capacities and create greater environmental awareness among stakeholders.

The restoration of the degraded ecosystems will be largely informed by the completed land use planning processes. In addition, protection and monitoring support will be provided for selected impacted natural forest reserve areas. Rehabilitation activities in the refugee camps will include the establishment of a community-based nursery, undertaking agroforestry and village land afforestation. In the refugee camps, flood and erosion control measures will be undertaken.

To promote climate-resilient livelihoods and to provide incentives for sound environmental management and conservation, the project will promote climate resilient agriculture through the provision of inputs and transfer of skills and knowledge on improved agricultural practices. This includes the use of drought-resistant seeds, water harvesting and irrigation, water availability and water efficiency will be improved. To promote livelihood diversification, including a shift from subsistence to commercial farming, the project will strengthen skills and capacities to undertake alternative livelihoods such as livestock rearing and small-scale enterprise development.

To anchor the gains and promote sustainability of the project's outputs and knowledge and will be mainstreamed into national policies and programs. This will be achieved by generating and documenting evidence, disseminating information and providing inputs and knowledge to policy and planning processes.

In order to address the mutually reinforcing system of climate impacts with an equity networked response, the project plans to reach 105,000 people (80,000 refugees and returning 42,000 hectares of land).

**Types of conflict include their: skirmishes, and sexual and gender-based violence. Refer to Annex E: Environmental and Social Management System for assessment of potential conflicts and their management.**
