## Output 1 will involve the development of climate-resilient land-use plans (CRLUPs) in host community villages (Activity 1.1). These plans will include landscape and ecosystem approaches that includes for the needs of all stakeholders. Climate resilient land use planning within camps and villages will be coordinated through the project and monitored by the CRLUP monitoring team. The CRLUPs will be developed with a focus on climate-resilient agriculture, small-scale flood control infrastructure to reduce downstream flooding and erosion in host communities. Guided by the best available science, these plans will include landscape-level ecosystem services and benefits that are expected as outputs are therefore designed to cumulatively enhance the capacity and resilience of each camp’s mosaic of ecosystems and landscapes. The CRLUPs will be developed with a focus on climate-resilient agriculture, small-scale flood control infrastructure to reduce downstream flooding and erosion in host communities. These plans will ensure coherence and complementarity between interventions and will enable the project to address the complex ecosystem interactions that are critical for resilience. The landscape-level ecosystem services and benefits that each output will inform will include the landscape-level monitoring of ecosystem benefits by defining the coverage of each project activity.

In Output 2, Community Based Forest Management (CBFM) Interventions (Activity 2.1) all seek to improve the productivity and resilience of forest resources in host communities. The effectiveness of these interventions is limited because the extent of this conservation is reinforced by the agroforestry and afforestation interventions (Activity 2.2), providing sustainable livelihoods for communities, while also contributing to climate change mitigation and adaptation.

Demand for fuelwood will be diverted from slow-growing indigenous Miombo ecosystems towards fast-growing plantations that are more productive and resilient to drought. These plantations will provide a range of ecosystem services such as food, fodder, shade for crops, and sustainable fuelwood production, will further deter activities or behaviour that harm the environment (e.g., deforestation) and contribute to climate change mitigation.

The agroforestry interventions (Activity 2.3) all seek to improve the productivity and resilience of forest resources in host communities by diversifying livelihoods through improved income generation and savings associated with the enhanced agricultural and livestock activities under OUP1. These interventions will also provide a range of ecosystem services such as food, fodder, shade for crops, and sustainable fuelwood production.

The agroforestry interventions (Activity 2.3) all seek to improve the productivity and resilience of forest resources in host communities by diversifying livelihoods through improved income generation and savings associated with the enhanced agricultural and livestock activities under OUP1. These interventions will also provide a range of ecosystem services such as food, fodder, shade for crops, and sustainable fuelwood production.

The flood and erosion control measures (Activity 2.3) will also be contributive to strengthening the overall resilience of the project’s outputs by reducing the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts through the ecosystem services provided by ecosystems in areas that have been degraded to the extent of having insufficient natural flood attenuation capacity. These measures will also contribute to the reduction of sedimentation and erosion, which is a major problem for agriculture and livestock.

Ultimately, this will help to support the resilience of the agriculture, forestry and agroforestry measures, contributing to the overall sustainability of these interventions by reducing their vulnerability to climate change impacts while enhancing the sustainability and interdependencies of the project’s outputs.

Under Output 3, agroforestry will also support the climate-resilient agriculture interventions (Activity 3.1). This will be achieved through strengthening of the capacity of agricultural landscapes to sustain or improve yields under various climatic conditions by diversifying livelihoods through improved income generation and savings associated with these agricultural interventions will also be supported by the rainwater-harvesting and irrigation interventions (Activity 3.2) which will reduce the vulnerability of communities to climate change impacts, particularly drought.

The coverage of agroforestry adoption will be mapped in Output 1 and will be monitored by the changes in the land use and land cover data provided by the CRLUPs.

The alternative livelihood interventions in beekeeping (Activity 3.3) will operate symbiotically with the forest interventions under Output 2. Investments in forest systems will also support the flood mitigation impacts of the interventions under Activity 4.1, which will be monitored through the changes in land use and land cover data provided by CRLUPs.

The descriptions above would jointly contribute to strengthening the resilience of the landscape under future climate change scenarios. A landscape approach will be reinforced with a focus on ecosystem services and benefits under scenario 4. Underpinning this is the need for the planning processes and physical interventions to be participatory, transparent and inclusive in order to ensure that they are relevant and responsive to stakeholders.

As a result of a land use planning activity and targeting ecosystems will occur away from human settlements and infrastructure. The landscape-level ecosystem services and benefits that each output will inform will capture of envisioning the benefits of the ecosystem-wide benefits (Activity 4.1), developing communication

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 31

## Proposed project approach

The project will adopt an integrated landscape EBA approach to help communities manage floods, droughts and higher temperatures, while also addressing other information strategy concerns the overall vision of the landscape and the interconnectivity of various land uses. The complex human-ecological interface of the refugee context has also been considered in the development of this proposal, which is designed to address both climate change and non-climate issues. This approach includes joint meetings between host communities and refugees, awareness-raising and capacity building workshops, and community-based projects that will help build resilience by reducing vulnerability, promote sustainable efforts, promote ecosystem protection, and minimize conflict over resource use. The solution would also be applicable for other projects operating in the area that focus on other dynamics of non-climate drivers (such as demand-side energy use).

## Key barriers to reducing vulnerability and supporting resilience in the Kigoma region

The proposed project will overcome a number of identified barriers that impede building the climate resilience of host communities and refugees in Kigoma.

### Institutional, planning, knowledge, and regulatory

**Barrier 1: Limited capacity in local institutions and processes to plan for and address long-term time-varying climatic vulnerabilities.**

The institutions providing support to the region are not well positioned to address long term climate vulnerabilities, as they are focused on short-term development priorities. This is exacerbated by the fact that the Kigoma District has a greater uptake of Village Land Use Plans (VLUPs)110,111 very few VLUPs in Kibondo, Kagunga and Kasulu districts have been developed or implemented. The lack of VLUPs in these areas is due to limited capacity at the district level because of budget constraints due to various competing priorities at the District level. An additional challenge is related to the fact that VLUPs are not well integrated into other planning processes, such as those for water resources management committees in developing and enforcing the VLUPs; a challenge noted by district government officials during the review process. The lack of capacity in local institutions and processes to plan for and address long-term time-varying climatic vulnerabilities is also due to insufficient awareness by planners of the value of ecosystem services, which constrains the adoption of ecosystem-based adaptation strategies.

**Barrier 2: Refugees and the special needs of their host communities are not well articulated in adaptation policies, legislation, planning or programs.**

The current policy environment in Tanzania provides an enabling environment for the adoption of adaptation interventions in some areas; refugees and the specific needs of their host communities are not yet adequately considered in adaptation policies, legislation, planning or programs. This is due to limited capacity at the district level for forest management plans, and health strategies. There are also opportunities for tailoring guidelines such as on climate sensitive agriculture, health and other plans and guidelines to the acute needs of host communities and refugees.

### Technological

**Barrier 3: Limited access and uptake of agricultural, forestry or land management technologies or knowledge to improve climate resilience.**

Kigoma is predominantly rural, with very limited access to agricultural, forestry or land-management technologies that can enhance the resilience of the region to future climate impacts. Farmers’ vulnerability is exacerbated by low levels of knowledge and capacity to implement resilient climate smart agriculture practices such as those that promote soil

**Page 38**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 34

## B

### Financial

**Barrier 1: Low purchasing power of smallholder farmers and refugees limits access to inputs for resilient agriculture and other long-term investments**

The lack of credit facilities hinder both farmers and refugees reduces their access to equipment and improved planting materials for resilient agriculture and other investments in longer term solutions. Common markets allowing for exchange of goods and services are limited within the camps, and there is a general lack of financial services and access to inputs. There are still markets within the camps although reduced in size and volume of transactions. More market opportunities exist outside the camps.

**Barrier 5: Financial services are insufficient in the region, except for some savings associations**

In the absence of formal financial services, mobile money services have been widely adopted by refugees and now a significant proportion of the population in the camps. Mobile money is used to access both local markets both inside and outside the camps. The insufficient availability of local financial services and technical knowledge in the region limit the ability for individuals to save and invest their earnings. This has resulted in limited savings, with a very limited capacity for high-level oversight and management to support saving and bulk procurement. This compounds the problem of lack of access to inputs and other resources. There is also a lack of financial services for building inputs or livelihoods. Consultations undertaken in September 2020 noted that the scale of investment in savings associations was very small compared with both credit consumers, and in some cases, savings associations required the monetization of food rations.

**Barrier 6: Limited investment in Kigoma region and competing development priorities defer long-term adaptation**

Although climate change adaptation is a priority for Tanzania, and whilst humanitarian activities are receiving adequate funding, there has been limited investment in longer term solutions to address climate change. This underfunding is reinforced by generally low investment in the region, as well as limited long-term planning capacity at district level. The majority of development priorities are focused on immediate needs. District government budgets are very limited and nowhere near sufficient to address the climate adaptation needs in the region. There has been a lack of coordination between humanitarian actors and development partners in adaptation required are public goods and require grants. Revenue streams for some livelihood activities are available, but there is a lack of capacity to manage these resources effectively. This is due to a number of factors including: the nature of investments, implementation and management arrangements, financial returns on investments and other infrastructure requirements.

Also, some development initiatives are struggling to mobilize resources to address environmental issues. To illustrate, the Kigoma Joint Programme that aims to provide development support to refugee hosting areas in the region has not been able to fully implement its objectives due to limited funding. Despite having a good understanding of access and access to technology, the actual funding is lacking.

### Social


---
**Page 39**
---

## Barrier 7: Unique needs and challenges faced by women and their refugee communities

The barriers to adaptation are exacerbated for women because they have limited access to resources and decision-making power within their communities. Women face additional constraints in accessing formal economic opportunities due to traditional gender roles, social and cultural norms, and limited access to training, decision-making processes, and financial resources.

### Overcoming the key barriers

Barrier 7 will be overcome by working with local government authorities to facilitate the development of climate-resilient village plans for women. These plans—and the participatory process by which they are developed—will provide the framework upon which subsequent interventions will be based, thereby ensuring their sustainability and effectiveness in addressing Barrier 7.

### Outputs

Outputs 2 and 3 will address Barrier 3 through activities in capacity building using the Farmer Field School approach, and by providing inputs to women’s groups. These outputs will also contribute to addressing Barrier 7 as they provide interventions and inputs into forestry and agriculture. For the forestry sector, interventions will focus on implementing Community Forest Management (CFM) plans for the Tana River District, with support from the Tanzania Forest Service (TFS) to monitor and protect the North Makame, South Makame, Mwila and Buyungi National Forest Reserves.

### Final Output

Finally, Output 4 will contribute to scaling and institutionalizing the mechanisms by which these barriers are being addressed, while independently contributing to addressing Barriers 2, 5, 6, and 7. Specifically targeting institutions and key decision-makers, these activities will help subvert the underlying causes of these barriers to adaptation.

Together, the recommended interventions will overcome the barriers to climate change adaptation in Kigoma, thereby contributing to a paradigm shift in host communities and refugees' climate-resilience. This shift will ultimately ensure that the project beneficiaries are better equipped to adapt to climate change impacts on their livelihoods, towards adaptation and climate-resilient sustainable development. In addition to the direct benefits to project beneficiaries the proposed project will also yield significant co-benefits and gender co-benefits. The mitigation potential of the proposed project is estimated at 172 million tonnes CO2e over a period of 30 years, or 5.8 million tCO2e over 20 years — including a five-year implementation period — as a result of avoided deforestation, improved land use and management practices (see section 4.2 in the Climate Change Mitigation Assessment Tool). For further detail please see Appendix C and the project descriptions under sections 7.3.1, 7.3.2, and 7.4.1 in this document.

Gender inequality in climate change adaptation planning and action is expected to be reduced through meaningful representation and participation of women in Climate-resilient Land Use Planning (C-LUP) to support ecosystem-based adaptation.

Additional environmental, economic, social and gender co-benefits are identified in section D3.

The selection and distribution of interventions for each sub-catchment where the target host communities and refugees are located will be scaled relative to the attributes (including population of each camp and host community, as well as by other factors such as land cover, soil type, elevation, distance from river, flood risk, etc.). The priority will be given to forest, forestry and flood mitigation interventions under Outputs 2 and 3, and that are appropriate to the on-the-ground context. The preferences of beneficiaries and the experience of local partners will be incorporated to ground-truth the intervention priorities. This will include a review of the existing land cover, soil type, elevation, distance from river, population density, number of host community settlements (villages), differential land use practices, and flooding risks in each sub-catchment.

* Population and village level analysis was undertaken using the NASA -1 km Estimated Gridded Population dataset.
* Basic land use analysis was conducted using ESA CCI 30 m land cover product, highlighting areas of activity and potential for interventions. The analysis will be used to identify priority areas for intervention design and prioritization providing EBA benefits.
* Feasibility studies (on site distribution of interventions) was undertaken under the observed ERMS hourly precipitation rainfall to storm event characteristics in combination with SRTM 30 m topography data as well as other relevant datasets.

These factors aid in intervention selection, design and prioritisation to build resilience of target beneficiaries that are appropriate, locally owned and have high prospects for sustainability.*

### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

This proposed project will address the urgent need for climate change adaptation for both host communities and refugees through its Outputs: number i) participatory, climate resilient land use planning in villages; ii) improved land management practices; iii) increased access to information on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies; iv) climate change adaptation disseminated and mainstreamed into policies, plans and strategies in Tanzania and in the region; v) capacity building for host communities and refugees on climate change adaptation. The project will also address the need of refugees to return home after 10 years of displacement due to conflict and climate change. These areas are the Nduta and Nyungwe refugee camps, the former Mendi camp area and, where possible, the former Kibondo camp area. Refugees in the former Mendi camp area will be assisted with their voluntary repatriation to their home villages. Refugees in the former Mendi camp, with the exception of those opting for voluntary repatriation, were relocated to the Nduta refugee camp and Nyungwe refugee camp. The project will support the return of refugees to their final beneficiaries under the project will be defined as the refugees in the Nduta and Nyungwe camps. As part of the development of the land use plans, the project will also provide technical assistance for the implementation of land restoration with the contribution of GCF project investments in food and erosion control measures inside and around the refugee camps. The project will support the establishment of community-based natural resource management communities and the camps and Figure 12b shows how adaptation advice accrue to different groups in response to climate change impacts and risks. The sequence, scale and design of interventions have been informed by the needs.

**challenges, and available services in the respective sites and landscape connections. For example, concerns of erosion and flooding from increased runoff are more relevant in the high-density settlements of the camps where ground cover is low and there is a lack of vegetation to absorb water. In contrast, areas with higher levels of vegetation have lower rates of erosion and greater mobility of communities around the camps. Conversely, the camps have more reliable water supplies and higher quality soils for agriculture.**

The project maximises upon the mosaic of land uses, population densities and adaptation potential in the project area, targeting interventions geographically and by population to achieve the most effective, relevant and transformative impact.

The project area has been defined (see Section 3.3 Project boundaries) and site selection in the Feasibility Study for detailed design and implementation is based on the distribution and geographic and hydrological features around the camps. Within the sites, these will be distributed through the land-use planning processes in Output 1. This approach is consistent with the UNHCR’s strategy for addressing climate change adaptation (see Outputs 2 and 3), that are appropriate to the on-the-ground context. The preferences of beneficiaries and the experience of implementing partners will also inform this process.

The project will be implemented by the Government of Tanzania, acting through its Vice President's Office (VPO) and UNHCR, particularly in interventions in the refugee camps, as Excluding Entities (EEs). ### Component 1: Increased resilience of ecosystems and people to climate change threats

#### Output 1: Participatory Climate-resilient Land Use Plans developed (C-LUP) to support ecosystem-based adaptation

The GCF funding request for the Output is U.S.$1,062,895. This is the starting point for all project interventions, developing participatory climate-resilient land-use plans that are consistent with an ecosystem-based adaptation approach (EbA) and which will inform the design and distribution of subsequent Outputs.

##### Activity 1.1: Support climate-resilient village land use planning

It is the intention that the Village Land Use Planning process, and the subsequent interventions that are planned through this process, will be implemented in the villages on the larger area surrounding the camp, near Shindelo former camp area, Nidua camp and Nyungwe camp.

The Village Land Use Plan (VLUP) is an overall plan showing how land resources, located within the village boundaries, should be used to meet the needs of the community. The VLUP process involves a number of steps that are interrelated processes may require a considerable amount of time and resources; they successfully resolve conflicts around the use of natural resources. The project will adopt this established approach, introducing the overall objective of increasing the climate resilience of communities in the villages surrounding the camp through participatory land-use planning based upon the VLUP process, by acknowledging and addressing the role of the changing climate in shaping local ecosystems and livelihoods. This will be achieved by engaging with the community to develop a Village Land Use Plan (VLUP) for each village under future climate conditions, in order to drive activities in partnership.

The project will build on existing work undertaken through the implementation of the Village Environmental Management Initiative in Kagoma as well as numerous resources from FAO, International Land Coalition, Enabel, UNDP and others. The project will also draw upon lessons learned from other projects implemented by MoHa and institutions that deal with land tenure and use ecosystem-based planning, thereby providing a comprehensive approach to addressing climate change through the implementation of Village Environmental Management Committees and authorities in the implementation of planned activities, as an Implementation Partner. Completing the land-use planning process will be supported by technical assistance provided by UNHCR.

Technical assistance to facilitate the process and capacity building will be provided through experienced local partners with an understanding of the VLUP process. This process (and the interventions under Outputs 2 and 3) will further build on existing community-based parallel measures seeking to limit the threats and opportunities of climate change in host and return refugee communities.

Although village land use planning is typically a good practice, in the case of Kagoma, limited financial resources and lack of capacity at the Village Environmental Management Committee level have hampered its implementation. In 2020 noted a lack of capacity among Village Environmental Management Committees to enforce village by-laws and regulations, as well as a lack of technical support from MoHa and other implementing partners such as the Village Environment Management Committees to support the Ward and Village Environmental Management Committees. This has led to:

## B

**Sub-activity 1.2: Scram up land use plans for villages, and support to issuance of Certificates of Customary Rights of Occupancy (CCROs)**

The objective of participatory decision-making is to ensure the most efficient use of land resources through negotiation based on principles of equity, efficiency, visibility, conservation and sustainability. The development and creation of land-use plans therefore requires a framework and forum where stakeholders can negotiate and agree on how best to manage their land. This process not only ensures that the law requires consensus, it can also provide a means of resolving conflict, for example about the boundaries between adjacent villages or between villagers and forest users (e.g., pastoralists versus agriculture).

Considering that the process of developing land-use plans has already begun in some villages**, the project will pursue a strategy to help support the finalisation and enforcement of Land Use Plans already developed, in addition to developing new plans (see Annex 1.2.1 Feasibility Study Section 7.2 for details). The National Land Use Policy (NLUP) provides a framework for land use planning at national level. Villages that have developed their own plans following these guidelines, the village and land use plans advanced or developed under this Activity will take into consideration the NLUP to ensure consistency with national policy.

The project will support villages in developing their land-use plans by:

1. Establishing a District-level Participatory Land Use Management (PLUM) team
2. Facilitating the development of Village Land Use Plans (VLUP)
3. A Village Land Use Management (VLMU) committee is set up
4. Furnishing necessary resources for VLMU committees
5. Identification of appropriate land management measures

The sixth and last step of the VLUP process is the issuance of a Certificate of Customary Right of Occupancy (CCRO), which provides legal security to villagers who have customary rights over their land. The CCRO specifies rights conferred to a land occupier and user following tribal customs and traditions on land. Under the Land Act 1999, the village council is responsible for issuing CCROs in consultation with the custodians of land on behalf of the members. The Village Land Act (1999) confers these custodial powers to Village Councils. The CCRO is a formal document that provides legal security and recognition of customary rights over land. It is issued by the District Council, which has been empowered under the National Land Use Policy (NLUP). The CCRO is formally issued after consultation with the village council and approval by District Council; issuance of the CCRO is a formalistic affair, albeit with some cost implications to villagers in terms of time and money. The project will support villages in obtaining CCROs through its Sub-activity 1.2 (Support for Land Use Plans). The project will support this relatively small incremental cost to promote project ownership, engender support among the community and provide long-term security for the villagers and may be used as collateral for borrowing money for village infrastructure.

**119** Government operations proceeding to develop land laws are outside the scope of this funding proposal (eg. not supported by previous projects). The CCROs issued under the project will be in addition to those already issued. The information collected by WFP District Officers in November 2015 indicates 5 villages out of 9 in the surrounding area have signed VLUPs, 3 villages are in the surroundings of Kyangwali camp have signed VLUPs and no village in the surroundings of Mubende former camp have signed VLUPs.

## Sub-activity 1.2.1 Build capacity of local and regional government officials to support ecosystem-based adaptation and sustainable land management

Capacity building will be provided to support district officials and Tanzania Forest Service officers in including climate impacts and risks for the region into the land-use planning processes, enabling PLUM teams (Participatory Land Use Management) to consider these factors when developing their plans. Capacity building will also include training on the use of land to different purposes. This will ensure that different land uses are mutually supportive. This capacity building will be provided in a number of ways including: workshops and seminars; provision of technical guidelines, manuals and materials to district officials, including inter alia GIS software and tools; created paper for CDIO printing etc.

Since planning for climate resilience is a key project objective, ecosystem-based adaptation principles should be integrated into the land-use planning process. This will include training on how to identify the most likely expected impacts on the eco-ecological zones and on the communities. District PLUM teams will require capacity building in order to integrate these principles into their plans. Capacity building for district officials will also support the land use planning process, by identifying the hazards and their mapping on village land, the identification of specific sign of adaptation practices that are already being used by the community members as well as the identification of climate change, for example Activity 3.2 on flood and erosion control and Activity 3.2 on water harvesting and irrigation. The training material and the detailed nature of the technical assistance should be defined in the inception phase of this sub-activity.

## Sub-activity 1.3.1 Improve public awareness of climate change by sharing information on climate changes in the village

The project will also seize the opportunity of the community organization during the VULP consultation process to conduct training and awareness raising activities for the communities, including refugees, host communities and Local Government officials, topics covered in this training would include describing the projected climate change, impact such as water scarcity, floods, droughts, etc. The training will also cover the adaptation strategies that can be used to support the land use planning process, by providing a greater understanding of the projected changes to the landscapes and communities. In addition to the above, the training will also include information on how to adapt to climate change, this training will also help to support the capacity of the host communities to generally build their resilience in adapting to climate change.

Training and public awareness raising will also target Sukuma agro-pastoral communities that contribute to land degradation. The project will provide training for these communities on sustainable land management practices conservation or land use planning processes. The refugees, committes, who will be involved in the participatory process for the development of their own LUPs, will receive training and public awareness raising on climate change and good environmental management practices under this sub-activity. Training will also include information on how to adapt to climate change.

## Output 2: Degraded ecosystems restored to support climate change adaptation in host and refugee communities

The GCF funding request for this Output is $9,978,657 UNICORR in USD of $12,060,086 will go toward technical staff support and activities in forestry and flood reduction infrastructure in the camps and with selected activities, such as those described below.

All of the host communities and refugees in the project area live within or near forests. Both host communities and refugees use timber and forest products construction of shelters and others are used as well for fuelwood. Efforts to manage deforestation and reforestation have been limited by lack of capacity at both the national level and community groups — are as a result of underfunding. The result of pressure on the forest cover by both host communities and refugees is that the forests in the project area are degraded, with reduced tree density, reduced soil nutrient availability, and reduced soil moisture. This makes the landscape susceptible to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts and more frequent and severe flooding.

The land-use and forestry interventions will restore critical ecosystems to meet climate adaptation needs and improve environmental services in the project area. The forest restoration activities are expected to support natural forest regeneration and agro-forestry interventions will be carried out. The starting point for all project interventions is the development of a landscape-scale forest management plan (LFP). This is because it is a fundamental step in determining how the proposed interventions will be planned and delivered. Major steps of the LFP include: 1) conducting a baseline assessment, 2) developing an action plan, 3) preparing detailed designs to account for climate change — for example, for Activities 2.3 on flood and erosion control and 3.2 on water harvesting; and 4) preparing community-based forest management plans and for agroforestry interventions (see Output 2). The land use and forestry interventions will be implemented in close collaboration with the LFP stakeholders (see Activity 2.1).

In addition to the climate adaptation objectives, the activity has significant co-benefits. Adequate development of resilient ecosystems will provide a range of environmental services including increased soil fertility, reduced fuelwood resources, and protection of forest reserves have the opportunity of reducing conflict over resource use between host communities and refugees. The land-use interventions in the camps are expected to reduce risks for the camps (for example, within 4 km radius) reduces risk of gender-based violence as incidents are more common in areas with poor access to services.

The land use planning in host communities and forestry-related interventions are expected to lead to better managed natural resources and improved livelihoods. The forest restoration activities will also provide a range of environmental benefits. Moreover, and particularly for refugee women, the exposure to threat and GBV or other forms of violence will be reduced as they have access to services such as health care and education in areas with better infrastructure.

Host communities and refugees will respond as expected and existing future conflicts over fuelwood and deforestation will be mitigated.

This Output will cover the activities described below.

Activity 2.1. Implement Community Based Forest Management (CBFM) for resilient ecosystems

This activity will be conducted in village and national forests surrounding the three camps: District Councils, in collaboration with Tanzania Forest Service (TFS) will implement the activity of this support with the Technical Partner and other partners with experience in facilitating the establishment of VLFR (for example REDD+ or JGI) and the Technical Partner.

The principal objective of this intervention is to implement CBFM in forest areas such as Miondo woodlands anchored to villages in Kibondo, Kilombero, and Katavi Districts. The woodlands are characterized by a high density of small diameter wood fuel, a characteristic that boosts the ecosystem's potential to supply wood fuel over long periods, so long as the area is not degraded. VLFR will be established in these areas to ensure sustainable management of the small diameter wood, when managed properly. The extent of degradation in the area, as well as decreasing returns from woodfuel collection, are major reasons for the need to establish VLFRs. Other reasons include the increasing number of institutions with reasons for this are predominantly institutional and are outlined below. Market failures around the inefficacy of current forest policies and regulations will be addressed, in particular through Activity 1.1 on policy development of CBFM.

The concept of CBFM is already a central strategy of Tanzania's Forest Policy Act (2002). Forests that cover National Forest Reserves (NFR) are managed by the Forest Guards under the supervision of the District Director, although very limited in number and capacity. VLFRs are established on land outside NFRs, which are managed by village committees with support from central to village government; villagers may harvest timber and forest products, collect and retain forest royalties, manage forests for their own use, and sell forest products at market prices. VLFRs are not subject to the same restrictions as NFRs in terms of products and from regulations regarding 'reserved tree' species, and they are not obliged to remit any royalties to government.

Experience of CBFM has demonstrated effectiveness in improving ecosystem functions, as well as addressing poverty by enhancing livelihood conditions and providing alternative income to rural communities in Tanzania[1]. By integrating villages into the forest management process, CBFM can help address some of the major challenges facing these target regions – this approach can have a considerable climate adaptation potential by strengthening the adaptive capacity of local communities. VLFRs are also an important strategy for addressing the current and future demand for its advantages in reducing transaction costs, providing local ownership and responsibility for natural resources and promoting community-based forest management (CBFM). The establishment of VLFRs is supported by the government through Forest Departments using Forest Guards to patrol and arrest local people found within forests. This concept has been successfully implemented in other countries such as Kenya where the government has committed to undertake the work and supervision is lacking. [Reference 14 page 45 shows the extent of forest loss in around Miondo woodlands]

Village Land Forest Reserves (VLFR) are designated sites declared under section 32 and 33 or gazetted under section 35 of the Tanzania Forest Act of 2002. According to this Act, the village council declares VLFRs based on the existing Village Land Reserve (VLR). The declaration is subject to approval by the District Director and supervision by the village council. The village council may launch application to the National Director of the Forests and the Minister of Natural Resources and Tourism for gazetting as a VLFR. The gazetted VLFR will be approved by the Director in the Public Notice, declare the reserve to be a gazetted Village Land Forest Reserve. The management of the site is under the responsibility of the village council with support from the District Director and the National forest management committees.

Most of the VLFRs in Kibondo, Kilombero and Katumba District are still in the stage of declaration; the village councils Abandoned the declaration of most of the forests by the Director upgrades the forest to a high-level conservation status, less than 10% of the VLFRs have this status. The cost implication and difficulties in slowness of the approval process has provoked discouragement on the project. Therefore, sustainability of the VLFR will be enhanced by the project commitment to

support the implementation of VLFx governance, strengthening community institutional monitoring systems, and promoting conservation benefits to the users.

This activity aims to bring 30,000 ha of land under CBFM, which is ~70% of the forested area within the project boundary. The proposed activity will be implemented through three outputs:

Output 1: through avoided degradation and associated ecosystem service provision. The major ecosystem services that are expected to be provided by the CBFM include improved soil fertility, increased tree cover and biodiversity, and improved water infiltration leading to improved groundwater recharge, fuelwood collection and food production, and reduced risk of forest fires. These benefits will also reduce the incidence of forest fires that may contain high densities of tsetse flies, thereby minimising cases of sleeping sickness.

The specific villages and land area to be included in the CBFM system (as well as for the afforestation and agroforestry under Output 2) are to be determined through a participatory process with stakeholders under Output 1, although these will be chosen with the specific criteria in mind and in collaboration with the TFS, including:

• level of degradation of village forest land
• whether there is strong potential for enrichment of a camp:
priority for those that are adjacent to, or within the 4 km 'buffer zone' around the camps (where refugees are present)
priority for areas where there exists the existence of provisional processes or interest for establishing CCRO or an existing forest reserve with management plans
• whether there is strong potential for forest protection (forests are not severely and irrecoverably degraded); and
• if there is potential for collaborative benefit sharing schemes between refugees and host communities, where appropriate

• if established land tenure arrangements are in place.

consultations with stakeholders including agropastoralists, village leaders, and District Councils.

### The resilience of local communities—by providing access to forest resources and to additional streams of income. CIFM strengthens livelihoods and increases the resilience of local communities, particularly women and youth. Forests also perform a vital safety-net function by providing a range of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) that can be sold or exchanged for cash. The sale of NTFPs can increase the diversity of household income and nutrition. Forests, therefore, can help to build resilience in terms of food security and livelihood base, even in the face of climate change.

### Additional benefits that increase resilience:

- Improved access to forest resources.
- Increased income from forest products.
- Reduced risk and the increase in soil fertility by reducing erosion.

## Sub-part 2.1.1. Establishment and management of 30,000 ha of Village Land Forest Reserves

An established process towards CIFM is described below using the Community Forestry Guidelines. As described in the national CIFM Guidelines, the process towards CIFM comprises six main stages, as described below.

- Preparing a provisional Management Plan — which was discussed and shared with the refugee communities and representatives of the District Council.
- Implementing the Plan: electing a Management Committee; appointing patrol teams; agreeing and demarcating boundaries for the forest for purposes such as firewood collection, cattle grazing, beekeeping, grass cutting; keeping essential records.
- Formulating rules and regulations to ensure full community participation.
- Legally binding the Plan — agreeing and formulating rules, and enacting village by-laws to enforce them. Under this process, the Village Land Forest Reserve is registered as a Community Forestry Output.
- Declaring and registering the Village Land Forest Reserve and its by-laws with the District Council.

Sub-activity 3.2.1: Provision of mentoring and training for forest monitoring in national forest reserves for 10 forestry and natural resources officers.

Two national reserves in close proximity to Nangapanda in the Kasulu District — including North Makere (75,325 ha) and the South Makere Forest Reserve (48,960 ha). These two reserves are contiguous with the Mwamba Forest Reserve (10,000 ha), the Kigoma Forest Reserve (74,420 ha) situated close to the Monduli former camp in Kakando and the Mevise Forest Reserve (35,800 ha) situated close to the Nangapanda village. These reserves are contiguous with the national forest reserves, as well as the forested areas on general land. The vastness of the reserve areas and the limited infrastructure make it difficult for the communities to monitor their resources.

Host communities and district officials confirmed there is substantial grazing, fuelwood collection, and agriculture activity ongoing in the national reserve areas, where soils are fertile. Fertilis are also used to clear and regenerate land, despite the fact that this is not allowed under the forest management plan. The area has experienced a decline of tree fertility diminishes, and the land is abandoned. In Kalungwishi, respondents also mentioned deforestation linked to the logging industry. The community members have reported that some areas were cleared for grazing purposes and subsequently sold to the lime industry.

The sub-activity will empower TFS by increasing forest monitoring capacity and improving the mobility of patrol units to enforce the Forest Act in the South Makere, West Makere, Mevise and Buzungu National Forest Reserves. This will be achieved by developing forest management plans for the national forest reserves within the project area as well as supporting the development of a GIS-based monitoring system for these reserves.

The capacity building component of this sub-activity will be strengthened with the development of a GIS-based forest monitoring data management system that integrates information from patrol units, community members and other stakeholders. The system will support the use of GIS for fire monitoring with support of a JICA project and is willing to expand the use of GIS to monitor forest cover and biodiversity in the future.

The sub-activity will also provide training on the use of drones for forest monitoring and data collection relevant in the transboundary context of Kigoma as it control of illegal deforestation activities in the National Forest Reserves. The TP will support the development of a drone-based monitoring system with the Tanzania Forestry Research Institute (TAFRI) in the use of ArcGIS to monitor the impact of VLPs on the Village Forest Reserves in the area of the Gombe National Park. This sub-activity is expected to be completed in Kigoma by January 2018 by the CGI, Blue Ranger, local and regional government officials and other partners.

To support these activities, TFS staff will be provided with equipment to maximise the coverage of monitoring in a cost-effective manner. The equipment that will be provided includes:

- Drone hardware appropriate for the target NFR surface area and suitable for high altitude flight, as well as software for data collection and analysis
- Training on the use of drones for forest monitoring and data capture for the capturing of pre-location data; ii) IT equipment and GIS software; iv) A management team gear and camping equipment to support field operations.

Training will also be provided to these staff on inter alia the effective and efficient use of drones for landscape monitoring, data collection techniques such as the use of transects to collect data and the use of the GIS-based monitoring system. The training will also include the development of a community engagement plan that addresses issues in land conflict with and between land resource users (both project beneficiaries and the broader population) is resolved through dialogue and mediation; iv) A mechanism for the collection, analysis and reporting of forest cover data, and its support for the design and implementation of appropriate benefit-sharing agreements under Sub-activity 1.1.4. The training will be provided by CGI in collaboration with TFS staff and other partners. ## Activity 2.2: Implement 12,000 hectares of agroforestry and village land afforestation to increase resilience of land use

This activity will be implemented in and around host community villages surrounding Mandera former camp area. Nuts, fruits, and other tree species will be planted on the farms and in the villages. The project will also provide training for farmers, which will lead the implementation of this activity with support from the Technical Partner and local NGOs/CBOs for family community participation.

The principal objective of this intervention is to increase the number of trees across the agricultural (non-forest) landscape through agroforestry and village land afforestation. At landscape scale, agroforestry improves crop productivity by increasing soil fertility, reducing erosion, and improving water retention. Agroforestry also provides a range of other benefits such as improved food security, income generation, and biodiversity conservation and carbon storage. Most farms in the region are small-scale and rain-fed. Although agroforestry is not new to this landscape, it has not been widely adopted by farmers due to lack of awareness about its benefits. The benefits of agro-forestry do not appear to be well known to farmers in the target districts and uptake is presently low. This project will provide training for farmers on agroforestry practices and will also provide technical support. This will help to address the present low uptake and will promote ownership, sustainability and scalability of this technology.

The second objective of this intervention is to increase the number of trees across the agricultural (non-forest) landscape through village land afforestation. Village land afforestation has been identified as a means for increasing tree cover in areas where there are no forests or woodlots available. This will provide an additional source of firewood and other forest products, which will be particularly useful to residents of both refugee camps and host communities, resulting in extensive deforestation of surrounding areas. Consequently, the project will plant trees on village land in order to increase tree cover and improve livelihoods as a means of conserving surrounding natural forests. Under the REDD+ project, woodlots have been created in eight villages in Mandera District and 10 villages in Kajiado District. The project will also create woodlots in four villages in Kakamega districts respectively. The proposed GCF project will be able to build on the work already done in the project and will provide additional trees for village land afforestation, which will increase tree cover, improve soil moisture availability, and reduce soil moisture. This will therefore reduce the susceptibility of the landscape to the impacts of climate change such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts and floods. The project will also provide the resources required for these livelihoods and reduce susceptibility to flooding.

As with Activity 2.1, this activity will connect a market fate associated with the provision of reliable management of crop residues and other agricultural waste generated in the growing, planting and maintenance of agroforestry across 12,000 hectares of land. The project will also provide training for farmers engaged in these activities including 2.3.1, 3.1, 3.2 and 3.3. Additionally, agroforestry systems will provide ecosystem benefits to the landscape by increasing soil moisture availability and reducing runoff, consequently resulting in reduced risk of flooding and erosion as well as increased soil stability and fertility. The final choices chosen for planting will be determined by a Species Suitability Assessment, which will be informed by this assessment, among other factors.

## Sub-activity B: Establish and maintain 10,000 ha agroforestry systems to restore ecosystems that are critical to meet climate adaptation needs and add up to nurseries for production of tree seedlings

Tanzania is home to several traditional agroforestry systems that have been used for hundreds of years. Some have been abandoned due to the introduction of cash crops, but others continue to be practiced today. One such system is the traditional Wasuuma silvopastoral system called Night. One outstanding aspect of these traditional methods is the use of trees and shrubs in crop systems. However, despite their potential for climate change adaptation, this agroforestry area, agropastoral does not seem to be widely practiced and few examples of on-farm tree planting were observed.

The study found that farmers are aware of the benefits of agroforestry but lack access to seedlings. Farmers planted in the areas around the camps. Interviews with farmers consulted for this study showed a lack of awareness of the importance of trees and shrubs in crop systems, as well as a lack of knowledge on how to plant them.

Spatial land use analysis indicates ~28-30 ha of agroforestry land in the project area. Project ambition is to reach 35% of this area at a density of 500 trees/ha (see Appendix B for further detail on assumptions) for a total of 10,000 ha.

The deliberate integration of trees and shrubs into crop systems offers a means of increasing climate and livelihood resilience, as described in Table 2 below.

Its ecological and livelihood support functions.

Pressure upon natural forest systems is predominantly driving the local demand for fuelwood, as the main source of energy used by households in Kigoma region. Fuelwood collection accounts for 90% of total wood use with 85% being used for cooking, with 81% using firewood and the balance using charcoal[26]. Within the refugee camps, the average daily per capita consumption is estimated at 3 kg of firewood per day. The refugees are located within a radius from the Mikombo woodland within 10 km of the refugee camps[7]. Over the past two decades a number of studies have been conducted on fuelwood collection and use in Kigoma region, including the impact of fuelwood collection in the refugee camps and surrounding host communities. However, infrastructure for these technologies is still considered to be inadequate. The high demand for firewood has led to deforestation and soil erosion[27]. There is a likely continued preference for fuelwood; these factors mean that pressure on the forests is anticipated to remain high.

See Annex B of Appendix 2: Feasibility Study

The first major discussion on the role of biomass fuels in Kigoma was published in 1957, at which time fuelwood supplies were presented as the main option for meeting energy needs. The studies to provision energy have evolved and changed over time. In the early 1980s, the focus shifted from wood-based energy systems to improved cookstoves and the distribution of improved cookstoves and promotion of energy efficiency training; it is also moving towards a significant increase in the use of solar energy for cooking and lighting. The introduction of biogas technology has been slow but promising. In recent years, there have been efforts to promote the use of ethanol as an alternative fuel source. For successful rural development, biomass fuels like firewood must first be replaced by a liquid or gaseous fuel source like Agipetrol[28]. The introduction of improved cookstoves and promotion of energy efficiency training has enabled communities ultimately make a shift from electricity which can come from non-fossil fuel sources. This inexpensive technology is now being promoted in the region, with over 100,000 units distributed to date.

In Kigoma Ngorongoro actors in the region are introducing transition fuel programs, primarily improving access to LPGs, to help reduce the demand for firewood and charcoal[29]. The project area is located within the catchment of the Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), at the Technical University of Denmark (DTU) and UNICEF. At these facilities, a number of projects are being implemented that aim to improve access to clean energy sources in order to reduce demand and drastically reducing the Kigoma region's reliance on firewood, thereby creating an opportunity for the forest and ecosystem restoration components of this project to succeed. For further discussion, see Annex E: Feasibility Study Section 7.3.2

### Selection process for afforestation in degraded land will be undertaken at the project inception during a rapid species selection assessment led by the Technical Partner in collaboration with TFS and the local government. The assessment will form part of the land-use planning process. This will ensure that a robust site-specific analysis is done to take into account the ecological, social, economic, and cultural aspects of the proposed forested area.

### Selection criteria for species include:

- Suitability to environmental conditions (water availability, soil types, etc.)
- Ecosystem services provision (e.g., rooting systems that anchor soil, prevent erosion, and facilitate infiltration)
- Growth rate
- Productivity and growth.
- Cost-effectiveness
- Availability of local expertise for growing and management,
- Availability of seeds and plant materials
- Multiple uses for the selected species (e.g. fuelwood, fodder, medicinal use, etc.)
- Market demand for forest products
- Community acceptance (e.g., traditional and cultural uses)

### The Village Land Use Planning process will be led by the Village Councils. They will provide with training and technical advisory from the Technical Partner to lead the design technology, implementation and monitoring of this afforestation project. The Village Councils will also be responsible for the management of the village forests such that these plantations can be declared CIFOR forests with 10% of income arising from them reverting to the community. In the first year of project implementation, the revenue generated by the village councils will come from the village conservation funds and be mobilized by village leaders. Alternatively, the Village Councils may choose to use the funds for other purposes.

### The Village Land Use Planning process:

- To mitigate the increase risk of wildlife and human-induced fires in the afforestation areas.
- To ensure that the afforestation intervention which is scheduled in the first quarter of the second year of project implementation. TFS will establish necessary firebreak areas through casual labour and provide the management training for patrol teams and communities.

### The annual planting programme will require the production of 1,250,000 seedlings in each of Years 2 to 5. An additional 375,000 seedlings are required in Year 6 (for a total of 4,875,000 seedlings). To minimize costs of seedling transportation, nursery should be sited as close as possible to planting sites.

### TFS and a Technical Partner will be engaged by Tanzania VPO as implementing partners subject to the processes described in Section B.4.

## Activity 2.2 will complement the CIFOR interventions implemented under Activity 2.1, which have the objective of promoting agroforestry systems in the project area.

The combined effect of these interventions will be that the demand for firewood from slow-growing indigenous Moroto evergreen forests will decrease and the use of firewood as a source of energy will decline. This will lead to better decision-making processes regulating the use of natural resources and enhancing the climate-resilience of agriculture. The overall objective is to reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers in the project area, particularly women, who are heavily dependent on agricultural and livelihood activities under Output 3 which will improve income generation and savings and reduce the risk of food insecurity.

The direct beneficiaries of this activity include those engaged in the growing, planting, maintenance and efflux of the afforested woodlands across 2,000 ha (ca.15% of the total marginal land area within project boundaries), some of which will be planted with indigenous Moroto evergreen trees. The establishment of agroforestry systems and forest systems may provide many ecosystem benefits to the broader population of beneficiaries, to both refugees and the host communities in the project area, including reduced soil erosion and increased infiltration, resulting in reduced flooding and erosion as well increased soil stability and soil fertility.

### Activity 2.3 Implement flood and erosion control in densely populated areas to reduce the exposed and flood-prone farming/forest soils which have lost or no vegetation cover

This activity can be carried out within and in the vicinity of the refugee camps of Nyanjiru, Nduta and Mwende former camp area.

The camps were established rapidly, within a context of humanitarian pressures, limited land availability and no viable alternative to clearing forested areas. The rapid establishment of these camps reflects a process which could not adequately consider the combined impacts of increased pressure from displaced populations on the environment and the need for sustainable land use planning. The vegetation in the area (MOHA) was cleared quickly, with vegetation being removed to provide space for the camps. This has amplified flood events and soil erosion in the surrounding areas. The rapid establishment of these camps is described further in Figure 15 below. These are amplified further by the camps being densely populated, without opportunity to move away from the forested areas. In addition, the lack of vegetation cover on the land surrounding the camps means that climate change likely lead to losses in subsistence farmers, damages to homes and community facilities including schools and health centres. The rapid establishment of these camps has also led to a reduction in the amount of firewood available for cooking and heating which has exacerbated this problem. This activity will reduce the extent to which ineffective land-use planning is resulting to flood on the surrounding areas.

In the areas outside the camps, the land use and forest interventions described above will increase soil stability and reduce the level of runoff and erosion, addressing flood impacts downstream, which are anticipated to worsen as rainfall intensifies in the future. This will also have a positive impact on the livelihoods of local communities.

### Improvements in agricultural areas (Activity 3.1)
- Sensitizing, awareness raising and training of local communities on how to manage localmicroirri (on household scale) and small-scale irrigation systems (on community scale) (Activity 3.2)
- Storing water for use in dry periods (Activity 3.2)

Figure 15. Erosion gullies in Mwende former camp

However, in the camps, as a result of the higher population density, these types of interventions are not feasible. The preferred option for flood and erosion control is to provide solutions in the areas most susceptible to erosion and floods with high water flows (Figure 16). These solutions themselves will be designed and use materials that are able to withstand flooding and erosion. The design of these solutions will take into consideration flood risk modeling under climate change conditions but will also consider the existing topography, soil type, vegetation cover, and other environmental factors. The flood control and green food and erosion measures will therefore be tailored to the characteristics of each camp and designed based on the specific needs of that camp.

Most of the refugee beneficiaries for this activity would be the inhabitants of the refugee camps of Nkua and Nyungwe, with some flood-migration and erosion control benefits experienced by host communities located closest to the camp boundaries. The implementation of these measures will reduce the risk of flooding in the camps and lead to a significant reduction in the amount of standing water following high intensity rainfall events and flooding. This will reduce the potential for mosquito breeding and other health risks.

In Mendi former camp area, the flood and erosion control measures will be conducted after the camp closure in combination with other restoration interventions implemented by UNHCR. The decommissioning of the camp (closure process) is expected to take place between October 2021 and March 2022. The funds for this activity are from programme/other funding sources. GCF proceeds will not be used for Mendi camp decommissioning. The post-camp closure activities include: i) removal of all structures, including latrines and bathing facilities; ii) provision of downstream host communities with water supply services; iii) provision of sanitation services to downstream host communities; iv) potential for expansion of agricultural and agroforestry activities in the camp area and surrounding areas; v) potential for improved access to education and health services. The beneficiaries of such services will be host community in the district, since all the refugees from Mendi former camp have been relocated.

As part of camp closure and consolidation, a taskforce, composed of MeAid at national and local level, District Commissioner of Katsinaul, Kilamba and RAC and RAJ in Agboga, UNHCR, DRC (camp management) and NRC (shelter awash), has endorsed the following activities under camp clean up and environmental rehabilitation by relevant partners:

1. Demolition of refugee houses/infrastructure after the camp consolidation and recycle the materials.
2. Decommission of latrines and bathing facilities
3. Tree planting
4. Other activities related to environmental rehabilitations

A rapid technical assessment was conducted in early October 2021 by UNHCR Assistant Environment Officer, and other relevant partners, with the objective of identifying opportunities for environmental rehabilitation. A joint camp boundary demarcation was conducted with Kalakoto district authority. Kalakoto district authorities are discussing the use of the land after decommissioning of Mendi former camp to provide improved access to education and health services to the surrounding communities.

The feasibility study (Annex 2) included an analysis of land cover degradation through changes in NDVI (1983-2020), and a preliminary flood risk assessment analysis to inform the flood profile of the camps, including Mendi former camp. The feasibility study also recommends designs and suitable areas for the locations of gardens, stone pit, and other infrastructure. The technical assessment team will review and validate the final locations of the infrastructures and the technical designs required for each specific camp site. The results from this technical assessment and stakeholder consultations will inform the Technical Dossier of the tender process for construction services.

The beneficiaries for this intervention are primarily the refugees in Ntuba and Nyangwitu camps and the host communities living in the vicinity of the Mbandi former camp area, as well as the wider communities immediately downstream from the proposed interventions.

Sub-activity 2.3.1: Construct 10 km of micro-scale stone-filled drainage channels and Sustainable Drainage Systems (SuDS) interventions—including 16 stone-filled gabions and micro-infiltration trenches/pores/leakways—in three locations in Mbandi former camp area.

Stone-pitched drainage channels and stone-filled gabions—10 km of stone-pitched channels are estimated to be required to remove water efficiently from the priority facilities and infrastructure within the camps. The installation of 16 gabions will provide additional storage capacity for stormwater runoff, which will slow flood waters but will increase the horizontal propagation of water in doing so. These are therefore favoured over infiltration trenches/pores/leakways, which would be more appropriate if the proposed interventions were to be located further downstream from the camp area. The exact location of the drainage channels and gabions will be determined by a detailed site survey conducted by the University of Witwatersrand and the Lake Tanganyika Water Board (LTWB).

The technical solutions will need to be designed and constructed by skilled technical contractors based on outline designs provided in this proposal, with specific technical knowledge and experience. The project water officer will be able to identify suitable contractors, either using those that constructed the existing flood control structures in the camps or identifying new contractors who have relevant skills.

Sustainable Drainage Systems (SuDS) are systems that are constructed or engineered using man-made materials or natural systems that tend to preserve existing open space, protect natural systems (groundwater, surface water), and provide opportunities for the reuse of stormwater. SuDS can be used in conjunction with existing land-use plans and maintenance to manage stormwater flows. These options include solutions including:

• flood channels/pores/leakways,
• earthen bunds,
• rain gardens,
• filter strips, and

## Vegetated Swales

Detailed hydraulic modeling undertaken has provided the indicative appropriate locations for the Sustainable Drainage System interventions. The exact location of the infiltration trenches, swales and bunds will be determined by the project office in conjunction with the camp authorities (Ministry of Home Affairs) (see Section 2.2.1 of the Feasibility Study).

The SuDs can be easily constructed by the local workers, using local materials and with limited technical 'know-how'. The construction of these structures is estimated to cost US$0.5 per m². These structures will be maintained in conjunction with support from local NGOs as required (REDESCO, DRC etc.). Periodic maintenance of both the traditional solutions and the SuDs will be carried out by the project office in collaboration with the camp authorities.

The construction can be carried out at little to no cost using volunteer workers from the camps, overseen by the project officer's technical team.

A number of the traditional solutions (stone pitched drainage channels, weirs and stone filled gabions) in the drainage catchments will also need to be maintained. The maintenance costs for these structures are estimated to be required. These structures have proven effective in controlling floodwater and reducing erosion, however they require regular maintenance by the camp authorities. Maintenance of these structures is relatively low cost and limited maintenance. These traditional solutions are simple, inexpensive and relatively easy to construct and maintain in the context of the camps.

The UNHCR estimates that the adoption of SuDs could reduce flooding by up to 50% and erosion by 40%. The estimates suggest that the adoption of these types of measures could offer up to 40% floodings and erosion mitigation.

UNHCR will provide funds and oversight to its implementing partners to carry out operations and maintenance of water solutions in the camps. UNHCR will also provide training for local camp authorities, including a number of national Tanzanian staff in managerial and technical roles, as well as incentive workers (142) & skilled and unskilled labourers (300). The project office will be responsible for providing regular monitoring visits to the camps verifying the functionality of infrastructure and services across all sectors. Infrastructure will be built according to national standards.

Output 3: Climate-resilient livelihoods practiced to increase the capacity of host communities and refugees to better adapt in changing climatic conditions.

The GCF funding request for this Output is US$1,687,204. UNHCR co-financing of US$1,407,207 will go toward tenanting costs and infrastructure development to increase the capacity building.

Activity 3.1: Promote modern technologies and management practices to strengthen the capacity of farmers, district officials and agricultural extension workers in climate-resilient agriculture.

This activity will be implemented by the District Councils in coordination with the Government of Tanzania, Vice President's Office.

Sub-activity 3.1.1 & 3.1.2 and 3.1.3 will be conducted in villages surrounding the camps.

The two major crops are maize and beans which together represent 66% of total crop production. Farmers in Kigoma continue to use traditional farming methods, such as broadcasting seeds for planting and harvesting. The sale of seeds for replanting and sell seeds to vendors at local markets where farmers establishing new fields, including reforestation areas. This practice is not sustainable because it leads to soil degradation and erosion caused by climate and non-climate drivers, which limits agricultural productivity. Kigoma uses only 23.5% of arable land. Kigoma's agriculture sector has been characterized as a subsistence production where food is not fully utilized and productivity is affected by the degraded ecosystems.

Tanzania's Resilience Strategy (2019-2022) identified Kigoma as one of its geographic regions in the country where resilience interventions should be implemented. The proposed project will form part of the implementation of the Resilience Strategy. Interventions include the establishment of integrated food production systems and climate-resilient agricultural practices, such as conservation agriculture and agroforestry. The Resilience Strategy also includes an acknowledgment of the need for sustainable strategies on land use, improved management

## of natural resources and the facilitation of market mechanisms for improved livelihoods[14]. Services for improved crop production are currently limited. These services include crop forecasting, food security monitoring and early warning systems on short-term challenges such as pests and disease, as well as extreme events such as droughts and floods.

### A significant challenge to enhancing the climate resilience of local communities in Kigoma is that limited amounts of agricultural inputs are available for smallholder farmers. This limits their ability to produce crops at a competitive price in the market and other infrastructure—particularly road networks. The lack of sufficient transport and storage infrastructure makes it difficult for farmers to access markets, which results in low prices for their products. In turn, this makes it difficult for them to produce a greater variety of crops. This results in an increasing trend towards monocropping, which is more vulnerable to climate change impacts such as droughts and floods.

### The lack of sufficient transport and storage infrastructure also limits the ability of farmers to access markets, which results in low prices for their products. In turn, this makes it difficult for them to produce a greater variety of crops. This results in an increasing trend towards monocropping, which is more vulnerable to climate change impacts such as droughts and floods.

### Value-addition activities through the expansion of agro-processing are not well developed in Kigoma. There are several underlying reasons for this, including insufficient access of farmers to funding for modern farming technology and machinery, lack of infrastructure (e.g., roads), and limited market access due to low prices. Within the existing agro-processing industry, raw materials supplied by smallholder farmers are often of poor quality or not available at all.

### Access to credit should be provided to rural micro and informal enterprises, and mechanisms should be developed with incentives for them to invest in modern farming technology and machinery. This is necessary because it has been demonstrated in several existing projects. For example, the Climate Resilient Agriculture for Tomorrow (CRAFT) project in Tanzania was able to improve access of smallholder farmers to credit through a partnership between local communities in the study area[15]. Moreover, the project identified the need for improved income, brought about by increased productivity and diversification of crops. Improving institutional and technical capacity for climate-resilient agriculture is also necessary.

### The climate-smart agriculture (CSA) guideline, a document produced by the FAO, notes that agriculture is one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change[16]. In Tanzania, 70% of the population lives in rural areas, and 95% of them are smallholder farmers. This means that they contribute about 29% of the nations required food[17]. As a large proportion of the sector is rainfed crop production, and most households produce their own food for consumption, it is important to ensure that they have access to climate-smart agricultural practices in accordance with several national plans and programs, including: i) National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA); ii) Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP); iii) The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) National Communication; iv) the National Climate-Smart Agriculture Programme (2015–2025); and v) the country's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC). The government of Tanzania has set a target to have 3 million smallholder farmers practicing climate-smart agriculture sector by 2030. The success of implementing and upscaling CSA in Tanzania is largely reliant
