On institutions and policies and a behavioral shift of the population[148]. The CSA guideline identifies several key areas where capacity building interventions are required, which align with the interventions to be implemented under this project. These include: (i) improved access to information on climate-smart agricultural practices through training in climate-smart farming techniques, training tailored to the needs of specific groups of stakeholders, easier access to climate information services and advisory services; (ii) increased adoption of CSA technologies and practices by smallholder farmers with a focus on capacity building for pro-poor economic development in the region as there is a high incidence of poverty in this population group. The project will seek to improve linkages between these two areas.

The Sustainable Agriculture in Kigoma Regional Project (SARKOPI) 2015-2020, also example[149]. The project demonstrated the need for enhanced climate resilience in agriculture and specifically the development of climate change adaptation strategies. In this regard, the project implemented a number of interventions to build capacity building farmers through Farmers Field Schools (FFSs) is coordinated manner as well as the provision of extension services.

The interventions proposed under this activity will focus on promoting the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural technologies and management practices, as well as the procurement and distribution of agricultural inputs. The climate resilience strategies include: (i) improved access to information on climate-smart agricultural practices through training in climate-smart farming techniques, training tailored to the needs of specific groups of stakeholders, easier access to climate information services and advisory services; (ii) increased adoption of CSA technologies and practices by smallholder farmers with a focus on capacity building for pro-poor economic development in the region as there is a high incidence of poverty in this population group. The project will seek to improve linkages between these two areas.

Project budget: Field farmer schools (FFSs) in the villages will provide training to farmers and government extension officers on climate-smart agricultural practices, including improved crop management techniques that increase yield efficiency and reduced erosion, as well as training in adaptive alternatives to crops such as the production of honey and other non-timber forest products. The project will also support the procurement and distribution of inputs materials, among which at least 60% will be distributed to farmers through Savings Associations, prioritising resilient climate change adaptation strategies. This will include seeds for improved varieties of maize, beans, sorghum, millet grown in the region and new varieties of cassava which bring disease resistance. The capital available in the Savings Associations will also be used to purchase inputs such as fertilizers, pesticides, herbicides, insecticides, and seedlings nurseries for agroforestry seedlings. Through this, farmers will receive access to inputs such as drought-resistant seeds and improved varieties of crops. This is further explained under section 7.4.1 of the feasibility study.

In the host communities, improved climate resilience strategies include: (i) increased access on bears and honey, greater access to clean and enhanced water and soil management as supported by the project; (ii) increased agricultural production and reduce farmers' vulnerability to climate shocks and stresses. By improving agriculture production, food security will be bolstered in the host communities. This will also lead to improved income generation activities which will enable them from agriculture thereby improving their access to food by enabling them to purchase more, other diverse, food items.

A similar logic applies to the alternative livelihood activities related to beekeeping and mushroom production, which is particularly important for women who are often responsible for these activities. In Kigoma region, honey bees and mushrooms, often used a meat substitute (as indicated during community consultations) brings diversity to local diets and provides an additional source of income. The project will support the establishment of co-operatives enabling them to buy more and move diverse food items. In Kigoma Region, preferred mushroom species is Pleurotus floridus. Some of these mushrooms are also used as a meat substitute in the region. The production of honey and mushrooms by the host community will serve to ensure sustainability in the near-to-medium term whilst also ensuring livelihoods and

Extension services will encourage farmers' learning through demonstration plots where they will have time to start up seeds and relevant crop husbandry training in pre-harvest, production and post-harvest practices. With the aim of scaling up these activities, a Savings Association Model Office (SAMO) has been established at each District level as used for a strategy to strengthen group cohesion, disseminate knowledge and ultimately create stronger farmers' groups. The SAMOs will be responsible for training and mentoring Savings Associations in the FFS process (to be determined through the VLUP process), with two demonstration plots established per FFS. Starter kits for the FFS will be provided by the District Agricultural Officer (DAO) to each Savings Association, which will then conduct a participatory view with a view to have equal representation of women and men as well as to select members of a monitoring committee.

Climate resilient agricultural practices and techniques have been tested and adopted in several regions in Tanzania. For example, conservation agriculture practices are widely promoted as part of sustainable soil management and climate-resilient agricultural practices. These include the use of organic manure, intercropping with legumes such as pigeon pea, cowpea, soybean, sunflower, sorghum, maize, millet, groundnut, beans, as well as increased soil fertility, carbon content and water retention capacity. As part of FAO Mitigation of Climate Change (MCC) project in Tanzania, the use of conservation agriculture practices has been promoted through various approaches have been used and FFS have demonstrated to be very successful in Kagera and Morogoro. Training sessions on conservation agriculture were also conducted by the same project.

Sub-activity 3.1.2 Provide 50kg of maize seeds and 1 kg at least 60% to be procured by SAMOs and distributed through Savings Associations, to each of 15,000 farmers for climate-resilient agriculture including seeds, tools and inputs as well as training.

Once the training activities have been delivered and the capacities of extension officers have been enhanced, conservation agriculture practices will be promoted in the villages. This will include the provision of seeds and other total inputs necessary for implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices. At the village level, procurement and distribution of seeds and other inputs will be done through Savings Associations. The procurement activities will be undertaken through the saving associations, who will receive requests from the farmers' groups on the types and quantities of seeds needed to plant in their fields. These requests will then be submitted to the local market by the District Councils and directly granted to smaller farmer beneficiaries (5000 households). In year two, the SAMOs will provide 1 kg of maize seeds per household for climate-resilient agriculture. Farmers will have access to agricultural inputs worth BSLUSD, which may include agricultural tools, cassava cuttings, beans, sorghum and millet seeds, as well as other seeds and inputs. This will be provided in the form of: a) 100 cassava cuttings required per hectare at an average of 40TSC/ha(bunch) or 1 hecctare of beans in pure stand (50 kg to 70 kg per ha), b) 200 sorghum seeds per hectare, c) 300 millet seeds per hectare and d) 600 maize seeds per hectare at an average of 3.000 TSC/kg. Detail provided in Annex B. Budget: detailed assumptions.

The project will promote two crops – cassava and beans – and will focus on increasing their productivity. The selection of these crops is based on the following criteria: i) availability of seeds, ii) the potential for market demand; iii) the availability of cuttings and iv) the preferences of potential beneficiaries expressed during focus group discussions (FGDs). Cassava is a preferred crop because it has high nutritional value, can be stored easily, requires less inputs than maize, and provides income. Beans are also a preferred crop as they provide income, contribute to food security, and have good market demand.

Activity 3.2: Increase water availability and use-efficiently through water harvesting and efficient irrigation interventions.

Activity 3.2 will be done in villages surrounding the camps. UNHCR will lead the procurement of rainwater harvesting and efficient irrigation interventions, including the construction of small-scale water harvesting structures (e.g., cisterns) under the supervision of the Water Office (WOP). The District Councils will host the implementation capacity building and awareness raising on water resources management.

Under increasing climate change, water scarcity is expected to become an ever more limiting factor for activities such as agriculture, agroforestry and afforestation. To ensure that the contribution that these activities make to livelihoods is not compromised by a lack of water, it will be necessary to ensure that there is sufficient availability of water. The availability of water can be considered a mixed good, given that it does not fully align with either the definitions of a private or a public good. It is therefore important to consider how best to manage and distribute this resource. This activity will ensure increased and continued availability of water for livelihoods.

Interventions under this activity will ensure that water is available in close proximity to agricultural areas, as well as for the agroforestry and afforestation activities. The interventions themselves will be designed and use materials that are able to withstand changing weather extremes such as drought or flooding. Water harvesting structures will be constructed using locally sourced materials and through the C-LUP processes, based on sample technical designs (see Section 7.4 of the Feasibility Study).

As this activity intends to complement and support the agricultural and forestry activities in Outcome 3.1 and 2 Output 2.2, it is important that the interventions are designed with a view to ensuring that they will be sustainable from the agricultural training and inputs provided in Activity 3.1 and the agroforestry, afforestation and flood erosion control measures as well as the maintenance of the water harvesting and irrigation systems.

The project will support the maintenance of the water harvesting and irrigation systems, as well as those maintain the agroforestry and forestry activities. This will be done through training and capacity building for those responsible for maintaining these systems during the project period.

## Sub-activity 3.2.1: Construct micro-rainwater harvesting systems - 94 check dams - 30 run-off harvesting micro dams (500m³) - 120 untied water gares and - 110 tied micro ponds in host communities in Kiboko, Ilololo and Kakototo.

Water flows as sheet flow over the surface without stagnation or accumulation in some areas. In other areas, water is temporarily accumulating to flow as a stream. These areas are suitable for installation of inter-alia small check dams or charco dams.

Charco dams are off-channel permanent water reservoirs with anticipated sizes of 500m³/ha. These would either use the runoff from the catchment area or be fed by perennial streams and rivers, depending on the rainfall pattern and flood events. This may have things depending on the underlying soils, resulting in reduced water loss to the soil. During the dry season, these dams will provide a reliable source of water for livestock watering and other domestic uses within agricultural areas. It is unlikely that these dams will be full throughout the year as the water will be used by the communities during the dry season. The charco dams are also expected to reduce flooding in the rainy season due to the dual effect of slowing water flow reducing erosion and increasing the height of the water table in the agricultural areas.

The dams and in stream structures require significant technical knowledge and experience to construct. This will require experienced local contractors who have been trained by the Tanzania Water Resources Management Authority (TWRA) in Tanzania in accordance with applicable UN policies, rules and regulations. Basic labor could be supplied by the target communities as part of their contribution towards the project.

The hydraulic modeling of current and projected storm analysis indicates that in order to reach the majority of agricultural areas and host communities, 20 large dams and 94 check dams could be built for water harvesting purposes. The remaining structures will be constructed on streams and rivers where there is a need for storage and/or by pumping systems from the charco dams. The length of size, number of gabions and locations will be determined by the results of the field surveys to be conducted in November 2018. The Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MoWI) will be subject to change based on these surveys and community priorities.

These interventions are expected to have minimal impacts on water availability and flood downstream. For Nyerugusu camp, there is a need for further investigation and analysis on the impact that these structures would have at the entrance of the Mwanga-Miyowi-Myosari RAMSAR site and concluded to be very minimal.

The check dams will be constructed in areas where they are expected to provide maximum benefits. The check dams will act to slow down water flow and lessen erosion. The maximum volume of water stored by the structures is unlikely to exceed 50m³/ha, which would not have any significant impact on downstream flows or aquatic life.

The charco dams will be sited in areas feasible for natural reftling and being close to agriculturally important areas. The check dams, in the flow channel will also be constructed in the potential intervention location. Where possible these structures will be built using the existing channels and/or by excavating a new channel. In other cases they may need to be built using the soil and treadle pumps for water distribution. The propagation of this will be reliant on an in-situ topographic survey, which is expected to be completed in November 2018. The check dams will be constructed with the treadle pumped to them. They will therefore have more flexibility in their location, allowing for them being placed in closer proximity to the communities.

Figure 17 shows the sublocates for rainwater harvesting (RWH) interventions around Mendi former camp area, Nuba and Nyerugusu camps and the surrounding host communities.

## Construction and installation of in-situ water ponds and sustainable irrigation systems techniques require local technical know-how and can be undertaken by the farmers themselves using local materials, with supervision from the team of experts. The ponds will be constructed on land that is not suitable for crop production but where it is possible to use the area in the future. These will act as the local water storage for farm usage. The sited of these ponds will be determined during the feasibility study.

### Fencing or other physical structures to prevent encroachment would likely not be viable due to the cost of installation and maintenance. Some of the areas may also be marily so that fences would not work well. Community ownership of the water storage facilities is a key factor in ensuring their sustainability, as it will ensure that the ponds can be planted around chaco dams. These would act as wind breaks, lowering evaporation. Covers may be effective at reducing evaporation but they are expensive and difficult to maintain.

### Where possible, chaco dams will be constructed deeper and have a lower surface area. Check dams would not require protection from encroachment or erosion. The construction of the check dams will be done in such a way as to reduce sediments ending into the dams. Maintenance activities would all be done in the dry season when the dams are empty, so that they can be cleaned and maintained as these allow for overflooding during flood events. Additional cleaning, if required, would be done in the dry season.

### Training will be provided as part of Activity 3.1 (Resilient Agriculture). The specific off-road systems to use in each area will be determined by the feasibility study. These systems will be used to harvest water from the dams where it can be harvested, the type of soil and its properties, the groundwater level; geological information about the catchment, and the hydrological characteristics.

#### Sub-activity 3.2.2: Establish water pumping and irrigation through ~10 micro solar and ~172 treadle pumps that will draw water from the rainwater harvesting locations to where it is needed in host communities in Kasauli, Kibbero, and Kullu districts.

- Water can be drawn from natural water courses and engineered rainwater harvesting locations (Activity 3.2.1) as where it is needed for irrigation. As well as the host community farmers, water pumps and irrigation systems are also proposed to provide water for livestock watering and drinking.
- For those farming activities located close or near/below elevation to water sources (max height 7.5 m), a collection of farmers will be provided with treadle pumps to deliver water to their gardens. This water will be stored in tanks or small irrigation ponds, which can then be used for drip irrigation. A treadle pump can also provide sprinkler irrigation.
- A treadle pump system would include:
  - main distribution hose
  - main distribution hose
  - main distribution hose
  - main distribution hose
  - water tank
  - control head (valve), a filter and the drip lines
  - spray/sprinkler head.

- Solar pumps will be provided for communities whose cultivated land is located further away from the water storage locations. The solar pump design is unable to propagate water to the needed locations (max height 150m).
- The solar pump system would include:
  - solar panel
  - solar power system
  - solar controller
  - solar charging household/central room
  - irrigation kit (soil moisture testing kit, water tank, control head valves), a filter and the drip lines

The beneficiary profile and technology selection will be undertaken in a participatory manner that accounts for technical factors such as soil type, climate, topography, etc. The systems to be provided are designed to irrigate up to 500m³ or less. The technology is used for both high value crops (vegetables) and fruit trees. The irrigation kits will also include a filter to remove sediment from the water. This technology is available in the country and is part of the Climate Smart Agriculture guidelines, climate-smart agriculture activities in the GCF project. It is expected that this technology will reduce the volume of water approaching the consumptive use/crop water requirements of the plant thereby minimising losses.

Given the extent of the physical and administrative interventions planned in the project, an extensive programme of training and awareness is proposed. Project activities will only be sustainable in the long term if they are supported by

The community has the necessary skills to use and maintain the rainwater harvesting, storage and pumping as well as irrigation equipment.

As noted above, the C-LUP program will seek to gain consensus among the host communities around how water resources are managed in their areas. This will be achieved through a series of workshops and training sessions. Specific training will then be provided to ensure that this management plan can be implemented. Details of activities included in the training are described below.

### Water Resources Management and Protection

- The advantages of protecting water sources and understand the watershed/catchment
- Understanding how human activities such as agriculture, livestock rearing etc., will impact source lands over time
- The problems with and consequences of farming in the river floodplains
- The benefits of good land use practices in terms of sources of water, better farming methods, water conservation practices, and boosting soil fertility.
- The importance of trees and the tree lines planted along the 60m mark

#### RWIM: Storage and distribution

- Advantages of RWIM for domestic and agricultural uses
- How to construct a rainwater harvesting system
- How to install and operate a rainwater harvesting system
- Operation, maintenance and repair of RWIMs (e.g. place like the ponds/dams, the solar pumping stations, and the basic irrigation systems [design and installation] and basic irrigation agronomy)
- Capacity building for community members on construction of proper vegetative swales as drainage systems for their homes/villages and farms.

This training programme will enable community members to continue maintaining activities introduced by the project in terms of water harvesting, storage and distribution. The training will be conducted on the importance of good land use practices and water conservation methods in their farms, and harvested water for irrigation and domestic use. Training will be done by a local trainer and an international trainer (during the first two months of the project) and then continued as needed by the projects water experts.

### Sub-activity 3.2: Support the establishment of watershed extension Water Users Associations (WUA) through capacity building

Although there have been considerable efforts by the host community to protect the major water sources within the project area, the enforcement of village environmental bylaws has been a challenge as there is still some agricultural and livestock rearing activities taking place in these areas. The establishment of WUAs in the Nyanguruwa camp — has resulted in an increase in waterborne diseases such as urinary tract infections and cholera. In addition, the lack of proper water management systems within the project area has led to conflicts between communities. The establishment of WUAs has the potential to address challenges regarding water management, the enforcement of water conservation laws and the protection of major water sources.

WUA — alongside staff from the Tanzania Forest Service (TFS) and EMC members — will be trained on conflict resolution mechanisms and how to enforce village environmental bylaws. This training will be conducted in line with the binding sharing agreements established under Sub-activity 1.1.4. Moreover, strong linkages between the WUAs and the TFS and EMC will be strengthened through this activity.

If conflicts cannot be easily resolved, it will be managed through the grievance redress mechanism as outlined in Annex 6. Enforcement of village environmental bylaws is a critical component for ensuring that water resources are maintained at sufficient quality and quantity for host and refugee communities than what is predicted to be negatively affected by climate change.

Currently, the Tanganyika Lake Basin Water Board has supported the establishment of a WUA in the area; however additional support and the establishment of more WUAs is required. The proposed project will strengthen the capacity of the Tanganyika Lake Basin Water Board to provide technical assistance for the management of water resources working and coordinating closely with the Tanganyika Lake Basin Water Board. It will additionally enhance linkages between the Tanganyika Lake Basin Water Board and other stakeholders such as TFS, EMC and WUAs in order to ensure that they have the potential to play a major role in supporting other sub-activities under Activity 3.2 including awareness raising on water conservation practices.

## Activity 3.3: Promote climate-resilient livelihood diversification to strengthen food security and nutrition; provide alternative income as a safety net, and to sustain the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural and forest management practices in the Kogelo region.

The District Council, in cooperation with the Government of Tanzania and Vice President's Office (VP), will implement the livelihood interventions in the host communities. UNHCR through the project co-funding will provide livelihood interventions for displaced persons living within the project area, which will be coordinated and agreed with Mkuu.

But as noted above, households in the Kogelo region have a limited suite of livelihood options available to them and are therefore vulnerable to climate-related hazards such as drought. Further to this, high levels of maturation among maize crops in the region make them particularly susceptible to climate-related shocks. In addition to the main sources of livelihood in the region, other activities such as beekeeping and mushroom growing are also practiced in the host and displaced communities. These activities are more resilient than agriculture because they require less water (no irrigation), no fertile soils (and water), but are more resilient than agriculture to projected climate variability. However, there are several barriers that need to be addressed for these livelihood diversification options to be fully realized. Some of these barriers will be addressed through the strengthening of Savings Associations in host communities to distribute inputs and reinvest returns on savings. Other barriers will be addressed by providing training and support services.

The project team has found a strong level of support from focus groups for the strengthening of SAD. Additionally, this activity will provide considerable co-benefits as it is also expected that the SADs will be able to provide inputs for these activities. This support would ideally be provided through public services including development initiatives. These services are currently not available in the region and therefore need to be provided by the project.

The primary objective of the livelihood diversification activities is to strengthen food security and nutrition in the event of hazards such as droughts or floods — which could reduce crop yields — provide alternative income as a safety net in times of crisis, and sustain the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural and forest management practices. The prioritisation of livelihood diversification activities has been discussed with potential beneficiaries in the target communities.

### Sub-activity 3.3.1: Promote beekeeping to incentivize forest conservation in the forests surrounding the camps and villages by providing equipment and training to 800 people

Beekeeping and honey production are vulnerable to climate change and climate variability, which has negative impacts on both activities. In addition to these direct impacts, two non-climatic drivers of change have been linked to losses in honey production in Kibondo and Kakame: the arrival of refugees and consequent demarcation of forest areas for their use; and the expansion of agriculture into forests. These factors have led to increased deforestation and loss of vines.

This project will tackle these challenges through participatory village land use planning with communities, which will provide a framework for sustainable forest management and consequently allow beekeepers to implement their activities on selected locations within the village forest reserves.


---
**Page 75**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 71

The activity will be carried out by the District Councils and the Tanzania Forest Service in collaboration with the Kibondo Beekeepers Cooperative Society. It aims to provide technical support and capacity building for beekeeping and sustainable forest conservation value. The Kibondo Beekeepers Cooperative Society was founded in 1992. The cooperative provides training, equipment, and extension services to its members. The cooperative has established contracts with local companies (for example, Jamiiwe Bee and Upenyo Honey). There are four beekeepers' cooperatives in the region, but Kibondo is the only one that has a formal structure. All of these cooperatives are represented by a cooperative office in Kibondo. A number of studies [1] have found that beekeeping in the country has been hampered by lack of access to markets and extension services, poor quality equipment, low productivity, reliance on traditional hives and limited access to finance. When linked to community-based natural resource management, beekeeping has been proven as an effective strategy from an economic and environmental perspective [2].

Under this activity, beekeeping will be promoted as an alternative livelihood activity that contributes to forest conservation and sustainable use of forest resources in the Kibondo District. The project activities will be implemented in the forests surrounding the camps and host community villages. Equipment and inputs will be distributed through the cooperative office.

The promotion of climate-resilient beekeeping activities will involve the distribution of modern hives, which protect colonies from heavy rains. Modern beekeeping equipment and to be owned by farmers will allow them to improve their productivity and income. The project will also provide training on good beekeeping practices that include knowledge of climate change and its impact, enabling farmers to select the most appropriate beekeeping activities in response to changing climatic conditions [3]. Climate-resilient beekeeping activity will therefore involve this type of training to both farmers and extension officers. The extension services provided by the project will be critical for the success of these activities.

Inputs include:
- Identification of areas for beekeeping activities.
- Provision of modern beekeeping equipment; and
- Capacity building and ongoing support to beekeepers and extension services.

Demand for honey is established in the region and likely sufficient to support the business model. The target area, and particularly Kibondo district, leads as major producer of honey and beeswax in the region [4], with an annual production of 10 million kg of honey and 250 tons of beeswax.

## Sub-activity 3.3.2: Promote mushroom cultivation for commercialized persons with training and mushroom growing facilities by providing equipment and training to 500 people

This activity aims at supporting livelihoods diversification for both host communities and refugees, by establishing mushroom growing facilities in the refugee camps and surrounding areas of Tanga District. The project will be implemented over a period of three years.

### Mushroom cultivation is well-established practice in the region, particularly during rainy seasons.
- Mushrooms are short-cycle, high-yield crops, and offer an alternative to currently cultivated crops both in terms of potential income opportunities and higher nutritional value.
- With continuous income from climate-friendly business activities, refugees can improve their livelihoods and reduce vulnerability.

### Existing studies and projects in the country have demonstrated that communities who harvest mushrooms have a higher standard of living than those who do not. However, existing facilities are often located far away from refugee camps, and land conditions are not anticipated to present any risk to establishing growing facilities because of controlled humidity and temperature.
- Small-scale mushroom farming has been used as a strategy for diversifying livelihoods in the region (e.g., Tanga District). The success or failure of this strategy over time (period of time) has affected communities. In Tanzania, communities reliant on forests have been supported to diversify their livelihoods by establishing mushroom growing facilities and training them to manage forest resources.
- Other programs in different regions of Tanzania have promoted mushrooms growing as livelihood diversification strategies.

### Mushrooms are already a food crop in the region, and they are also used for medicinal purposes. The project will focus on improving the sustainability of farming systems through the recycling of organic matter, which can be used as a growing medium.
- Mushroom cultivation is a good livelihood option for families with or without available land. Existing pilots from REDESO served as starting points to establish mushroom growing facilities in refugee camps and surrounding areas. The base is constituted by hotels and agencies in the region. While host communities and refugees will be targeted, an emphasis on training and providing equipment will be placed on refugees who are closer to where they are produced, to reduce the amount spent during transportation.
- As there is a larger market for fresh mushrooms than dried ones, this project represents a significant constraint to expansion. The lack of reliable methods to transport mushrooms to markets also inhibits farmers from expanding their operations. However, dry mushrooms for food and medicine are still in demand.

### Activity 4.1: Generate evidence of the economic benefits of ecosystem-based adaptation to host and refugee populations for use in policy-making

To prioritize an ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) approach, decision-makers need to understand and appreciate the relevant interlinked ecological processes and how they directly produce economic benefits. The analysis would consider the direct and indirect benefits that are produced by ecosystems, including those that are not easily quantified as reduced food security or decreased water quality; and its replacement costs – the implication of implementing ‘grey’ alternatives (e.g., infrastructure) to replace ecosystem services in order to achieve similar environmental outcomes.

The tangible services provided by local ecosystems include food provision, provisioning biomasses used for fuelwood, fodder, and other non-food products. The intangible benefits are those that are harder to quantify but have long-term benefits of these services are carbon sequestration, soil nutrient cycling, water decomposition and numerous cultural values. These ecosystem services provide a range of economic benefits including reduced health care costs, as well as a reduced disease burden from exposure to faeces tills and risk of contracting sleeping sickness.

By recognizing the positive environmental, economic and social co-benefits to communities of these ecosystems, this sub-activity will help decision-makers understand how ecosystem-based adaptation can be used to enhance natural resources. The sub-activities under this activity will achieve this by determining the economic value of the ecosystem services provided by the ecosystems in the project sites, as well as the costs associated with their provision and benefits, based on livelihoods and primary resource dependence.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.1: Identify benefit and cost streams for valuing ecosystem services under different climate scenarios

Project-delivered benefits and costs to be evaluated will be identified, to measure and value the ecosystem services provided by the ecosystems in the project sites (e.g., water provision, soil nutrient cycling, carbon sequestration) under increasing (rising tide and decreasing food peach), changes to water quality and quantity, enhanced soil health and reduced disease burden. The benefits of these services will be valued using a range of methods including avoided implementation expenses, cost institutional and enabling costs, opportunity costs, and unintended social and economic impacts.

#### Sub-activity 4.1.2: Identify data streams and modelling methodology for valuing ecosystem services

This sub-activity will determine the data required to conduct these valuations, as well as the modelling methodology for determining:

(i) avoided costs under hydrologic impact mitigation; and (ii) replacement costs to replace benefits of affected ecosystems.

The identification and valuation of ecosystem services provided by the ecosystems referenced above will be conducted under different climate change projections and a sensitivity analysis will be carried out determining critical degradation

## Sub-activity 4.1.3 Gather primary and secondary data for project interventions.

Following the identification of the methodology in Sub-Activity 4.1.2, primary and secondary data will be gathered to understand the baseline situation and identify key drivers of change. This will include qualitative data with dimensions such as spatial information, land-and-land use data, socio-economic population data, livelihoods analysis, surveys/research, health statistics, market assessments, institutional analysis, stakeholder mapping and soil and vegetation surveys.

## Sub-activity 4.1.4 Carry out valuation modelling exercises of benefit and cost streams identified under 4.1.1

Based on the methodology determined under Sub-Acivities 4.1.1-4.1.2 and the data collected under Sub-Activity 4.1.3, a baseline will be established to carry out valuation modeling exercises of benefits and costs.

## Sub-activity 4.1.5 Consider temporal, spatial and sectoral impacts and opportunities arising from the costs and benefits evaluated in sub-activity 4.1.4

This sub-activity would consider the cost at which habitat recovery (afforestation, agroforestry, flood interventions) represents a net benefit, as well as the timing of other benefit and cost streams, as identified in Sub-Activity 4.1.1. This will consider gains and losses for up- and downstream refugee and host communities. Additionally, this would provide an opportunity to identify opportunities for cross-sectoral collaboration between different stakeholder groups (gender, urban-rural, refugee-host community, youth etc.). In addition to considering the impacts this sub-activity would also consider how these interventions can be integrated into existing national development plans, as well as synergy finance solutions to optimise benefit streams (such as payment for ecosystem services (PES) or redirecting remittances towards conservation activities).

## Sub-activity 4.1.6 Prepare full and summary reports

The analysis and findings of the economic assessment will be compiled into full and summary reports — highlighting the interdependencies and sensitivities based on the redundancy hypothesis of the ecosystem — to be submitted to stakeholders, policy makers and UNHCR. The summary report will also include a brief description of how the results of communicating the evidence of benefits of EDA provide specific policy recommendations and looking at opportunities for cross-sectoral collaboration between different stakeholder groups (gender, urban-rural, refugee-host community, youth etc.). This sub-activity would also be used as an opportunity to disseminate lessons learned from the project. The findings will also be shared more broadly with stakeholders involved in the National Adaptation Planning, NDC development, and stakeholders involved in the implementation of the Tanzania Climate Change Action Plan.

## Sub-activity 4.2 Develop communication products to disseminate project results

To scale the impacts of the proposed interventions, it is necessary to disseminate the lessons and lessons of the project to a wider audience. This will be done through a series of workshops for policy makers in Tanzania and UNHCR’s humanitarian programmes to mainstream climate resilience into village land use planning implemented under Activity 1.1, policy briefs will be compiled and distributed by key stakeholders including the Village Land Use Committee (VLUC) for internal review and revision. Stakeholder engagement and distribution will include representatives from district and regional government, host villages, as well as refugees and camp authorities. These products will also be used to disseminate to other similar contexts within regions of Tanzania large as well as in other refugee sites globally.

### Sub-activity 4.2.1 Develop policy briefs on mainstreaming climate resilience in village land-use planning

Capturing the lessons learned from the climate-resilient village land-use planning implemented under Activity 1.1, policy briefs will be compiled and distributed by key stakeholders including the Village Land Use Committee (VLUC) for internal review and revision. Stakeholder engagement and distribution will include representatives from district and regional government, host villages, as well as refugees and camp authorities. These products will also be used to disseminate to other similar contexts within regions of Tanzania large as well as in other refugee sites globally.

These policy options and the guidelines developed under Sub-Activity 4.2.3 will improve upon existing land-use planning by addressing the gaps in mainstreaming climate change adaptation, planning integrated landscape approaches and incorporating gender considerations into land-use planning processes. Topics for these briefs may include:

Sub-activity 4.2.3: Develop a publication paper on mainstreaming climate resilience in village land-use planning

This sub-activity is an output of the policy briefs developed under Sub-Acivity 4.2.1; guidelines will be developed and distributed to inform decision-makers, planners and authorities on how to mainstream climate resilience into the design, implementation and monitoring of village land-use plans. The publication paper will provide guidance in different contexts and institutional responsibilities for enforcement, monitoring and implementation. In addition, the guidelines will include:

- defining the climate problem threatening the camp villages, including its impacts on women, men and vulnerable groups
- best practices and effective strategies in consensus building and negotiation in fair and equitable allocation of land use planning resources
- selecting intervention sites and assessing impact scenarios with and without climate-resilient land-use planning
- identifying how to integrate mitigation targets into village land-use planning
- incorporating agricultural and other Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures into sustainable village planning and management
- addressing costs and benefits of climate-resilient land-use planning;
- implementing and updating of climate-resilient land-use planning, including identifying appropriate sustainable livelihoods options for refugees
- monitoring and evaluating climate-resilient land-use planning.

Sub-activity 4.2.3: Produce lessons learned and audience paper on the use of Savings Associations at scale in climate change adaptation

Capturing the lessons from the use of Savings Associations (SA) in host communities to upscale climate-resilient agricultural practices demonstrated under Activity 3.1 findings will be compiled and distributed to key stakeholders to inform their decision-making processes for scaling up these practices. The lessons learned will be disseminated at various levels, from SAs themselves to locally operating NGOs, high-level district planners and multi-lateral development agencies.

In order to support the implementation of these findings, guidelines will be developed and distributed to inform users, decision-makers and other stakeholders on how to upscale climate-resilient agricultural practices. The process to develop these guidelines would include a review of how SAs in various countries have been used for scaling up climate-resilient agricultural practices. The lessons learned from the Tanzania model will be benchmarked against other contexts before, and after implementation of project activities.

The guidelines will include options for adapting this model in different contexts (in Tanzania, and globally) as well as best practices on how to upscale these practices through Savings Associations. These guidelines will also provide guidance or adapt the Operational Manual Outline for the Savings Association Management Offices included in Appendix C. In addition, the guidelines will offer detailed guidance on the processes of:

Activity 4.3 Draft revisions to key plans and policies and support their integration into national and district government planning processes to promote up-scaling of the EBA model

This activity will focus on the development-humanitarian-climate nexus (Figure 18), to bridge the gaps and discordance between these three sectors, which are often seen as separate. The project will work with key stakeholders—overseeing the various elements of these intersecting clusters, with divergent priorities, timelines and target beneficiaries—to ensure that the different actors involved in development, humanitarian aid and climate change and inclusive development in Kigoma. This sub-activity will review the relevant laws and policies that govern or are relevant to adaptation in Kigoma region, including those related to land use planning, disaster risk reduction, and conflict resolution. These include district development plans, forest management plans, livelihood improvement plans, disaster risk reduction plans, conflict resolution plans, etc., as well as the extent to which UNHCR, the Ministry of Home Affairs and other entities operating within the camps and humanitarian concerns, are embedded in district- and region-level development and spatial planning. B.4 Implementation arrangements (max: 1000 words, approximately | pages plus diagrams)

The proposed project will be implemented over a five-year period. The United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) will be the Lead Entity (LE), the Government of Tanzania (GoT) as the Subsidiary Authority through its Vice President's Office (VPO) and the United Nations High Commission for Refugees (UNHCR) will function as the Executing Entity (EE). The collaboration between UNEP, GoT and UNHCR will leverage capacity at the national, regional and district-level and climate change adaptation expertise and UNHCR’s unique capacity and experience in refugee protection, assistance and solutions.

### Accredited Entity

As the Accredited Entity, UNEP will carry out field visits and safeguards oversight and provide the necessary scientific and technical support to the project formulation, start-up, implementation, evaluations and closure.

#### Experience and track record of the AE

UNEP has a broad global portfolio on climate change projects funded through, inter alia, the Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF), Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF), Adaptation Fund (AF) and bilateral funds such as the European Commission’s Climate Change and Forests Facility (CCFF). UNEP has extensive experience in: i) supporting countries to adapt to climate change. UNEP's knowledge base is derived from completed and ongoing projects supporting ii) building capacity of national institutions, iii) enhancing climate resilience by restoring vulnerable ecosystems that underpin community livelihoods; and iv) improving the effectiveness of project implementation. It draws upon lessons learned and experiences and lessons learned during the implementation of the proposed project.

UNEP's comparative advantage over other accredited entities lies in its ability to provide technical/technical advice and support to countries in formulating, implementing and monitoring climate change projects. These are supplemented by UNEP’s strong capacity in the field of climate change through implementation of climate change adaptation projects at global level. UNEP has a track record of providing technical assistance to governments for project formulation and effective delivery of the project's objectives. Lastly, UNEP is house policies and expertise on gender mainstreaming, social inclusion and environmental safeguards will contribute towards gender- and environment-sensitive project implementation.

### Executing Entities

The Government of Tanzania Vice President Office (VPO) will act as the Lead Executing Entity (EE) of the project underpinning the national policies and plans and acts as well as complementary with existing adaptation projects and Tanzania’s NAP process. As the lead EE, VPO will oversee overall project implementation and will be accountable for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with UNEP’s guidelines and procedures will follow applicable UN rules, regulations, and policies, which include provisions for financial management and reporting as well as other relevant requirements. The VPO will also ensure compliance with the Subsidiary Agreement to regulate the obligations of the parties. Compliance with applicable UN rules, regulations, and policies will be monitored by UNEP. The VPO will provide the necessary resources and technical assistance and resources for the implementation of the designated project activities for the completion of the required information from UNEP. The VPO will also ensure that all expenditures are in accordance with UNEP’s guidelines and procedures and expenditure reports to the AE.

VPO will engage partners through different mechanisms including direct execution (DE), PMU, cooperation agreements with District Government and TSS as well as contracts with service providers and consultants. The District governments underpinning the VPO will execute designated activities as the district-level directly and may be authorized to contract NGOs, SADCs, etc.

The selection of such partners will be guided by UNEP to ensure that UN requirements are met and with the aim of ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with UNEP’s guidelines and procedures (personnel, agents, staff, contracts) as well as in the selection of implementing NGOs and the Technical Partner following UNEP partnership policy and procedures, including due diligence process. UNEP will review terms of

UNHCR’s comparative advantage is in its expertise and mandate in the refugee context. As the vast majority of 465 million people displaced by conflict are concentrated in “climate change hotspots” around the world, UNHCR has a unique opportunity to work on addressing climate change issues in refugee hosting contexts—especially through promoting the inclusion of environmental considerations into national policies and strategies. UNHCR works with partners (e.g., in Kigoma town, Kipondo, and Kasauli) which works with the Ministry of Home Affairs (MoHA); and local government bodies such as the District Councils and Water Boards to promote the inclusion of environmental considerations into relevant projects including in forestry, water conservation and sustainable energy. Environmental considerations are built into UNHCR’s Global Strategy for Sustainable Energy (GSSE), which was launched in 2015. In order to increase engagement in this domain, the Global Compact on Refugees calls for strengthened partnership, including with development partners, civil society organizations, and private sector actors. As a result of its work under the GSSE project with the GCF is recognized as a major partnership opportunity to operationalize the GCF. In line with the principles outlined by the GCF, UNHCR will continue to engage in partnerships with governments, international organizations, UN agencies and the private sector to address energy and environment challenges in the camps and facing the host communities. To support this work, UNHCR has established a dedicated team of experts on environmental issues; dedicated teams have been set up to strengthen the operationalization of the GSSE as well as support UNHCR’s work under the GCF. The technical and financial support will be provided by UNHCR’s Global Technical Environment Teams located in UNHQ in Geneva.

UNHCR has a long-standing commitment to reducing the environmental impact of refugee operations, for example by locating camps away from environmentally sensitive areas; promoting sustainable land use practices such as reforestation activities, and enhancing refugees’ access to clean modern fuels and technology for cooking to reduce dependence on firewood. UNHCR’s Global Strategy for Sustainable Energy (GSSE) was launched in 2015 with the aim of minimizing environmental impact, in a way that includes host communities and other stakeholders, while improving refugee livelihoods and reducing costs. The GSSE aims at reducing the environmental footprint of UNHCR operations impacts across its operations worldwide. UNHCR launched a five year Global Strategy for Sustainable Energy (GSSE) to reduce the environmental footprint of its operations by 2030. This will be achieved through resource efficiency by reducing use of fossil fuels, purchasing green energy from reliable suppliers and other sustainable energy sources, and promoting renewable energy technologies in refugee camps. UNHCR’s catalytic role to promote sustainability in displacement settings includes this developing joint program between the GCF and UNHCR. The GSSE also aims at increasing refugees’ access to clean modern fuels and technology for cooking engagement with private sector and advocating for innovative financial mechanisms. For example, in 2019 UNHCR launched a pilot project in Bangladesh which seeks to provide renewable energy access and refrigeration services in refugee camps through partnerships with the Energy Development Authority (EDA) and other partners such as the Senior Development Officer, Environment Officer and Energy Officer, among others. The partnership with EDA will leverage the EDA’s experience in providing clean modern fuels and technology for cooking.

UNHCR also engages to support climate change adaptation for refugees and host communities, working with partners to promote sustainable land use practices such as reforestation activities, and enhancing refugees’ access to clean modern fuels and technology for cooking. Examples of UNHCR’s global initiatives for climate compatibility in refugee hosting areas include: the establishment of the Global Strategy for Sustainable Energy (GSSE) which aims at reducing environmental footprint of its operations impacts across its operations worldwide; the GSSE also aims at increasing refugees’ access to clean modern fuels and technology for cooking. Examples of UNHCR’s global initiatives for climate compatibility in refugee hosting areas include: the establishment of the Global Strategy for Sustainable Energy (GSSE) which aims at reducing environmental footprint of its operations impacts across its operations worldwide; the GSSE also aims at increasing refugees’ access to clean modern fuels and technology for cooking.

UNHCR is also working with partners such as EDA, REESO, and others to promote renewable energy in refugee camps. For example, in 2019 UNHCR launched a pilot project in Bangladesh which seeks to provide renewable energy access and refrigeration services in refugee camps through partnerships with the Energy Development Authority (EDA) and other partners such as the Senior Development Officer, Environment Officer and Energy Officer, among others. The partnership with EDA will leverage the EDA’s experience in providing clean modern fuels and technology for cooking.

Project compliance with the UNHCR policy on Age, Gender and Diversity (AGD) is mandatory. UNHCR applies an age, gender, and diversity (AGD) approach to all aspects of its work, applying participatory methodologies to promote inclusion and ensure that the needs of all groups are met. This includes ensuring that women and girls have access to education, health services, and other social services; ensuring that children have access to safe and secure environments; and ensuring that older persons have access to age-appropriate services.

These interventions were done by partners outside of this project.

## Implementation Partners

The Government of Tanzania, acting through its Vice-President's Office (VPO), will act as VEO and oversee and manage project implementation, with and through appropriate implementing partner organisations including local government authorities, District Councils, NGOs, and other relevant stakeholders such as private sector entities and UN agencies. Selection of the technical experts and national and international NGOs will be undertaken with UNEP guidance.

### Government agencies and UN agencies

For the implementation of project activities, the Government of Tanzania Vice-President's Office (VPO) will establish cooperation agreements with local government agencies and institutions selected on the basis of their mandate and their capacity to implement the project activities in a given area.

The District Councils are the mandated authorities in land use planning and development at the district level and will therefore play a key role in convening all technical officers including agriculture to inform the project annual workplan and to provide technical advice and capacity building services. The implementation of the project activities is anchored in local governance processes and that results are sustainable beyond the project duration. VPO will sign cooperation agreements with District Councils, as well as with other implementing partners such as NGOs (Annex 2.3.1, 2.3.2 & 3.3) under the supervision of VPO. This type of project implementation arrangement between VPO and District Councils is a common practice in implementing large-scale projects such as the GEF-funded project "Ecosystem Based Adaptation for Rural Resilience in Tanzania".

VPO will also sign one cooperation agreement with the Tanzania Forest Conservation Fund (TFCF), sufficient to cover for forestry activities, including afforestation and reforestation, within the areas covered by the respective District Councils.

Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism (MNRT) will play a key role in overseeing the implementation of activities in the refugee camps. Coordination with UNHCR on the planning and monitoring of camp activities. This will include consultation on the location and timing of project activities, as well as the identification of appropriate partners such as local government agencies or NGOs and service providers executing activities in the camps.

National Environmental Management Council (NEMC) and NEMA office in Kigoma, will be responsible for undertaking compliance monitoring ensuring land use regulations as well as providing technical advisory and compliance guidance on all environmental aspects of the project to the two executing entities and the implementing partners.

The Kigoma Regional Authority and host the PMI), will provide support to the delivery of activities in the region and oversee implementation at the regional level. The Project Management Unit (PMU) for the project implementation, which will be located, will convene all technical officers including land use planning officers, agriculture officers (responsible for agricultural extension district level), engineering officers, etc., as regular members, to inform the project annual workplan and to discuss project implementation progress and adaptive management approaches.

TFS officers and relevant communities in the implementation of Chapter 1 and Output 2. Specific tasks of the Technical Partner include:

- Research necessary on the establishment of Village Land Forest Reserves and Community-based Forest Management.
- Technical advisory on the design and monitoring of agroforestry interventions.

#### Project Steering Committee

The Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be formed during the inception of the project and is responsible for high-level project direction. It will also address concerns raised by any of its members (for example, transparency in procurement processes). The PSC will meet at least once a year to review progress against the project plan and discuss amongst stakeholder groups on the best way to manage these concerns. The key responsibility of the PSC will be to:

- Ensure high-level direction for the project
- Provide strategic guidance to the Project Management Unit
- Review annual work plans, and project reports;
- Approve budgets, including any adjustments to budgets or timelines
- Support the Project Management Unit in managing emerging risks; and
- Monitor progress against the project plan and address any issues identified.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 89

## Key institutional actors

In Tanzania, national arrangements and decision-making processes are concentrated at the national level: The primary institution is the leadership of the Government of Tanzania Vice President's Office (VPO) as main steering entity. VPO’s Division of Environment (DoE) has the mandate to implement climate change actions in the country and to lead the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF). A National Climate Change Secretariat (NCCLS) is led by the National Climate Change Focal Point (NCCFP). A National Climate Change Technical Committee (NCTCIT) is chaired by the NCCLS. The NCCFP, NCCLS, and NCTCIT are supported by a secretariat that provides administrative support to oversee all technical issues related to the implementation of climate change actions and ensure coordination initiatives and activities.

Climate change issues are currently addressed using this same institutional framework, following the mandate or by-laws of the Environmental Management Act (2004). At the sub-national level, the Regional Administrative Secretaries that represent the President in each region are responsible for coordinating the implementation of climate change policies. Local Government Authorities (LGAs) provide activities serving supervision and administrative instructions. LGAs are responsible for the implementation of climate change actions at local levels. The Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism (MNRT), the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Forestry (MECCF), the Ministry of Finance and Planning (MFP), District councils are mandated to provide essential services and infrastructure support for local community’s needs and welfare. Other key institutional actors relate to land-use planning and forestry.

Land administration in Tanzania is done jointly by the Ministry of Lands, Housing and Urban Settlements Development, and the National Land Commission (NLC).

The Tanzania Forest Service (TFS), a semi-autonomous organisation under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism (MNRT) has been designated as the lead agency for forest biodiversity conservation and management. The Forest Biodiversity Information System (FBIS), its remit includes improving the protection capacity of local (village) forest managers under community-based forest management.

Engagement with local institutions will be through coordination at the local level and signing of MOUs for the direct implementation of project activities.

## B. Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

GCF investment is critical for the project as projected and existing climate impacts are evident in the Kigoma region. The region has experienced a significant increase in rainfall variability and frequency, leading to increased dependence on rainfed agriculture. The districts of Kibondo, Kakumio, and Kasulu are particularly vulnerable as they share resources with a large refugee population. The project will support the implementation of climate change adaptation activities that will enhance adaptation activities. The majority of the activities and funding will benefit host communities in adapting their livelihoods and infrastructure to climate change.

Refugees are also vulnerable to climate change as, by circumstance, they are often living in already climate vulnerable areas. They have limited access to land, water resources, and access to financial services. This makes them more hamper their capacity to adapt to climate impacts. They are also a vulnerable group that is unlikely to receive adequate finance as, for the proponents’ knowledge, this is the first climate project at the scale to include refugees as beneficiaries.

The Government of Tanzania has been providing voluntary repatriation and resettlement services in response to the Government’s preference for voluntary repatriation as the durable solution. The protected refugee situation is not likely to stop, considering that fewer refugees are opting for return to their country of origin. Given the current situation in neighbouring countries, it can be expected that more refugees will continue to arrive at Tanzania. Therefore, mass repatriation for many refugees from this country is not a feasible solution. While resettlement, mostly to the US, provides a durable solution, it is also costly and time-consuming. The Government of Tanzania has been providing voluntary repatriation and resettlement services in response to the Government’s preference for voluntary repatriation as the durable solution.

Tanzania's location is in a region surrounded by countries that are experiencing insecurity and extreme insurgency, such as Burundi, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Rwanda, and South Sudan. The current situation in these countries is expected that the country will remain an important refugee hosting country in the coming years. Hence, the project’s approach to address climate change impacts on the refugee landscape that contain refugee presence are those anchored in the host communities and local institutions.

### References

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 90

The project provides an opportunity to address the climate change adaptation needs of both host communities and refugees in a holistic manner, by demonstrating complementary gains in climate, development, and humanitarian finance. The project leverages the roles of UNEP, the Government of Tanzania Vice President's Office and UNHCR in maximizing the impact of existing resources and funding mechanisms, while building on each organization’s comparative strengths, while promoting collaboration with other non-humanitarian partners. Unless supported by activities such as those proposed here, climate change will continue to exacerbate existing vulnerabilities and undermine the resilience of agro-ecosystems—land displacement can further reduce the resilience of host communities and refugees to climate impacts. The project is designed to address these challenges in a manner that complements and enhances the refugee response and explains why they do not currently represent viable sources of funding.

## Alternatives to GCF funding for maintaining climate change resilience

### Funding by the Government of Tanzania
The Government of Tanzania is unable to address fully the adaptation needs of the Tanzanian population, host communities and refugees in the Kigoma region. The Government has been involved with UNHCR camps and host communities is freely discussed. International commitments to burden and share responsibility for climate change are not yet reflected in national policies or budgets. In addition, the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment teams would be unable to provide sufficient technical expertise if they were to host.

### Funding from UNHCR core budget
The funding is largely comprised of annual core contributions. Funding priorities focus on basic human needs (e.g., security, food, water and shelter). The Government of Tanzania has been involved with UNHCR camps and host communities is freely discussed. International commitments to burden and share responsibility for climate change are not yet reflected in national policies or budgets.

### Funding from the Government of Tanzania
The Government of Tanzania is unable to address fully the adaptation needs of the Tanzanian population, host communities and refugees in the Kigoma region. The Government has been involved with UNHCR camps and host communities is freely discussed. International commitments to burden and share responsibility for climate change are not yet reflected in national policies or budgets.

### Funding from other multilateral and bilateral donor sources
The majority of bilateral donors have historically contributed to UNHCR through the lens of supporting humanitarian relief efforts. Over the longer term, we would expect to see a move towards more sustainable development approaches that reflect the principles of the Global Compact on Refugees, through the mechanism of the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The Government of Tanzania has been involved with UNHCR camps and host communities is freely discussed.

### Funding from the private sector
The private sector activity in the region is mostly limited to small scale livelihoods and agriculture. Private sector activity in the camps is even more limited than that in the surrounding areas. There are no formal agreements between UNHCR and the private sector regarding land use, water access or agricultural production. The Government of Tanzania has been involved with UNHCR camps and host communities is freely discussed.

While a significant amount of development activity has taken place in the Kigoma region, projects that have taken place to date:
- not considered the long-term climate change impacts on water, agriculture and livelihoods
- so have not been framed as climate-resilient interventions
- have been dispersed throughout the Kigoma region and so have not concentrated on, or been designed for, the unique problems of refugees in this area
- tended to look at water, livelihoods or agricultural systems in isolation, rather than considering the ecological context within which they operate. This has led to a fragmented approach that does not consider the need for interventions with a landscape-level approach.

Hence, there is no current rationale for funding a project that will address climate change impacts in a holistic manner, with specific attention given to those most vulnerable, including the refugees and their camps and neighbours.
