**Page 95**
---

# Justification for level of concessionality

Tanzania is currently classified by the UN as a Least Developed Country (LDC) with a per capita GDP of US$903 in 2018. Consequently, government budgets do not provide any allocation for addressing the impacts of climate change at Tanzanian refugee camps and the lands surrounding them. Furthermore, Tanzania has one of the highest poverty rates in Africa and the second-highest poverty rates in the country. The project requires a high degree of concessionality as it focuses on delivering long-term solutions.

## Majority of the project interventions do not have significant revenue streams

While some project activities such as resident livelihoods are expected to supplement food and contribute to income, these are not expected to provide sufficient revenue for the project. The average annual per capita income in the region is very low. The project aims to promote sustainability through the use of Savings Associations but loan repayments will be difficult due to the high poverty levels and lack of access to financial services.

Livelihood activities are already threatened by climate change and since these are mostly for subsistence-based needs such as food security, they cannot provide sufficient income to pay back loans. Furthermore, there is only one bank across the districts of Kibondo and Kakoma serving over 450,000 people. Most of them do not have access to financial services or savings accounts. This makes them unable to access finance to scale up their businesses and by new, climate-resilient practices.

## Refugees do not form part of the formal economy

Refugees are required to remain in the camps and are not able to engage in any economic activities outside these areas. They have no access to financial services or savings accounts (informal) (for example, barter or in-kind payments) or are fully reliant on humanitarian assistance. Despite these limitations, refugees are expected to contribute significantly towards the local economy through remittances and complex economic context. Since almost half of the project's direct beneficiaries are refugees who cannot take part in the project activities, they will not be able to generate income from their participation and repay loans or make loan repayments.

## Refugees are not appropriate to use as a financial instrument for the project

A limited amount of the GCF funding requested is spent on equipment with the potential to directly generate revenue and even then, the returns would be minimal. The project will also have to rely heavily on in-kind contributions from local authorities districts that host and share resources with refugees. Any increases in income and revenue will be channeled back to the government or other stakeholders who can mitigate its impacts such as low yields and damage to property; savings offer an important safety net for very poor households.

## B.6 Exit strategy (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project has been designed so that interventions will translate into long-term solutions which outlast the GCF's funding.

At the intervention level, long-term sustainability is ensured because of the inclusion of mutually supportive interventions in a broader framework and the use of innovative financing mechanisms. The project also includes an integrated social and sustainable financing mechanism. The basis for project sustainability and a successful exit strategy for GCF is underpinned by these factors:

### Anchoring interventions at the landscape level

While refugees are temporary residents in the Kigoma region, the region will most likely continue to host refugees in the long term. The project strategy is to ensure that the landscape and ecosystem can provide sufficient resources to support both refugees and local communities. This will be achieved through agro-pastoralist groups that pass through the area as it faces challenges of climate change. The interventions will also include a focus on strengthening the capacity of the local government authorities in the region, including strengthening the ecosystem's capacity to respond to climate shocks and continue to provide services such as provision of water and sanitation facilities for both refugees and host communities. In addition, the project will ensure that all project interventions will be implemented in the host community, that will be less vulnerable to population movements.

### Capacity building and empowerment of local government authorities

The project aims to involve local government authorities in the implementation of the project through capacity building activities such as training workshops for the Water Service, Lake Tanganyika River Basin Authority, and other relevant actors. Training and equipment will be provided by


---
**Page 96**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 92

The project seeks to build their mandate in land use planning, forest monitoring and conservation, and expanding experience with land-resilient agricultural practices. The expert consultants and technical advisory organizations engaged in the project will provide training for the host communities on land-use planning, forest management, climate change knowledge and build capacity on-the-job and through training sessions. These include the CTA expert in land use management, the UNDP expert in forest monitoring and conservation, and the GIZ expert in climate change and the Safeguards expert consultant. UNHCR's field presence and its partnerships with other agencies and NGOs in the Ngorongoro region will provide support and building-up on project implementation.

Introduction of a cross-cutting approach to land use planning: The Tanga Land Use Plan process has been the main tool for the integrated planning and management of natural resources in Tanzania since the 1990s. Its practice is embedded within the national policy framework, and it is recognized as an effective tool for sustainable development. This project will introduce climate resilience considerations into the traditional planning process but work within this established methodology.

Self-sustaining interventions: Many of the interventions introduced can be maintained or expanded by the host communities without further support after the initial five-year project period since they require minimal funding and input requirements, are based on locally available resources, and have a high potential for replication. The project will assess the viability and impact of its activities, setting the context for the continuation of these interventions beyond the project lifespan.

Financial mechanisms: The majority of the physical inputs for project interventions will be funded through Savings Accounts (SAs) established by the host communities in their respective villages. These SAs are managed by the host communities will have control over and be personally invested in these SAs. Profits from activities made possible by the project will also be deposited into these SAs.

Capacity building of the communities and inclusion: Project interventions include extensive training and capacity building, which will allow knowledge of the benefits of activities such as community-based forest management, resilient land use planning, and gender equality to spread throughout the host communities. This will ensure that women are not only beneficiaries but also active participants in these activities.

Gender sensitive approach: The project's gender-sensitive approach seeks to achieve practical and tangible benefits for women as well as bring about strategic changes in gender relations. The gender action plan (Annex 8) outlines concrete actions to address the needs of women, including their participation in decision-making processes.

Through enhanced capacities, skills, access to assets transferred to refugee and host community women by the project, they will be able to participate more effectively in development at the household and the community level. This effect is expected to extend beyond the duration of the project.

Revised institutional and regulatory systems: The institutionalization of climate change adaptation into regional, local and UNHCR plans, policies and programmes through Outputs 1-4 and it will contribute to the sustainability and replicability of these interventions in the long term.

Beyond the direct engagement of UNHCR with refugees regarding environmental considerations, the Global Compact on Migration (GCM) is a key mechanism for addressing climate change adaptation. The GCM provides an opportunity for the sector and other stakeholders to address challenges related to energy and the environment. The proposed project is responsive to this opportunity by engaging with relevant actors in the refugee sector and beyond. The project implementation will be documented and shared to guide UNHCR's global engagement with environment and climate change issues.

Collaboration: The project will work closely with other organizations, including NGOs such as CTA, GIZ, and UNHCR encourage the institutionalization of the approach proposed by the project.

Operations and maintenance (O&M): For assets outside the camps, ownership and management responsibility will be transferred to the host communities at the end of the project. The project will provide training on how to manage these assets effectively after the project ends. Prior to the project's completion, a budget will be identified to finance these O&M activities. Assets within the camps will be managed by MWA with monitoring and evaluation conducted in collaboration with UNHCR. This will ensure that the assets are maintained for future use.

Additionally, further insight might be given by UNHCR or other partner NGOs who have well established operations and maintenance procedures.

The participatory land-use planning intervention in Output 1 is designed to be both sustainable in itself and to ensure the sustainability of other interventions. The main part of this intervention will conclude with the villages having a registered land use plan, which will be signed by all relevant stakeholders. This will be achieved through the form of a Customary Certificate of Rights of Occupation. Achieving these goals will allow the host communities to

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 93

B

assert that we can actually ground land use planning (including by resolving any potential competing claims to land use) to adapt to be more climate resilient and in doing so, will ensure sustainability in the long term. In addition, through the cost-sharing mechanism of the project, it is expected that farmers will have a greater incentive to adopt new practices to future changes in circumstances. The land use plan forms the basis of the other interventions of this project and indeed of the entire portfolio of projects under the GCF. It is expected that the land use planning process and designing these interventions in a participatory and consensus-based process, their long-term success is more likely.

With respect to the community-based forest management (Activity 2.1), experience from elsewhere in Tanzania and further research will be used to develop a model for sustainable forest management that can be replicated across the country with minimal external support. The project will promote and support local leadership by establishing strong community- level institutions, which will ensure that the benefits of forest conservation are shared equitably among all stakeholders and also become self-sustaining – as farmers realize the benefits of including trees in their farming systems. It is expected that this model can be replicated across Tanzania with minimal external support.

The water harvesting, storage, distribution and irrigation interventions in Output 3 are designed to provide additional water for agricultural production by increasing the availability of water during dry seasons and reducing evaporation on the land. This intervention is anticipated to help the host communities maintain, and even increase, agricultural productivity over time. The project will ensure that the community has the capacity to manage these systems and that this need be maintained in order to ensure their effectiveness. Much of this will require only a basic level of skills (e.g., simple maintenance). However, for more complex systems such as irrigation channels, it is expected that some maintenance training will be delivered at village level, with more advanced maintenance training delivered to one or two individuals per village. This will enable the community to maintain and repair these systems in future. Any major maintenance/repairs that may need to be carried out by this equipment suppliers. It is expected that the cost of such repairs will be covered through the project’s co-financing mechanism.

Output 5 will contribute to building strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate response planning and development. These strengthened systems, and the evidence base that underpins them, will further contribute to the achievement of the project’s outputs. The evidence base will be developed by using a participatory knowledge base – with economic evidence, policy briefs, guidelines – and by institutionalisation adaptation into policy frameworks. This will ensure that the lessons learned from this project are captured in national policy and copied and once implemented (within the project cycle), will not require further updating in the medium-term, as the conditions for climate change adaptation development will be needed.

Savings Associations as a Key Part of Sustainability

Savings Associations (SA) are proposed as a key delivery mechanism for the project. SAs allow individuals to pool finances to save and borrow money based on consensus between members. SA – which typically have 20-25 members – are established in villages, with each member contributing an agreed amount per month. Villages, including the Kigoma region. Most residents of the host communities and the refugees in the Kilombero, Katambo and Kibondo regions are currently not able to save money due to their poverty levels. However, these communities have a general focus on saving rather than promoting climate-resilient activities specifically. This project aims to function as an enabling environment for SAs by providing training and support to SA members in the form of capacity building assessments by agencies like UNCDF. This could demonstrate early results and pave the way for less concessional financing from other development partners.

To promote sustainability of financing for climate-resilient activities, the proposed project features an innovative approach which involves the channelling of funds to be generated through the SA. The project will enable such savings to be channelled into a revolving fund that will allow farmers to purchase seeds, fertilizers, tools and equipment, and tools to foster greater resilience in climate change. This approach will generate new livelihoods and increase food security for the communities involved. In addition this approach will allow for the financing of immediate needs to be overcome and will provide an alternative to accessing formal banking services. For clarity, GCF funds will be used to establish seed, tools, and basic goods to promote climate-resilient livelihood but will not be used to provide co-ops grants.

111 UNEP is expected to be accredited for grant awards by the time the proposal is brought forward for GCF Board consideration.

The project aims to develop the capacities, tools, and systems for the local and regional stakeholders, thereby sustaining a model that can continue beyond the project cycle. By developing diversified livelihoods and strengthening the role of women in decision-making processes, the project will ensure long-term sustainability.

This also applies to village savings associations (VSA), which will continue to function with funds revolving. The host communities will be involved in the selection of tree species for agroforestry systems and establish small-scale nurseries for agroforestry seedlings. Moreover, the host communities will have control over and benefit from the products generated by these activities.

Sustainability of the mechanism will also be ensured through the production of a lessons-learned and best practices manual that will be shared with other stakeholders in the region. The manual will be disseminated to key stakeholders for review and feedback.

The guideline papers will be provided to key stakeholders to inform users, decision-makers, and development partners on the use of VSA as an instrument for financing climate adaptation projects. These guidelines are based on a review of how VSAs in different countries/contexts are being used for financing climate adaptation. Further details on the project’s approach can be found in the Financial Management Study (Annex 2.1) as well as in the operational manual outline for SAMOs that is appended to the Feasibility Study.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The table below shows the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D1. IMPACT POTENTIAL (MAX. 500 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

### Impact Potential

The primary impact potential of the proposed project can be described as follows:

- **270,000 direct beneficiaries (928,800 men and 277,500 men)**
- **77,100 indirect beneficiaries (366,635 women and 344,850 men)**

This includes:

- At least 50% of participants practicing climate smart agriculture, mushroom growing activities and beekeeping are women.
- At least 20% of the total forest area in the project area is under community-based forest management (CBFM).
- Approximately 363,745 people covered by village land use plans which improve incentives for climate resilience options.

### Numbers of Beneficiaries

Direct beneficiaries will be drawn from both the host community and refugee camps — those who are directly engaged with the project's activities, such as recipients of training. Although some interventions are targeted at specific groups of beneficiaries (e.g., women), the overall impact is expected to benefit all direct and indirect beneficiaries.

### Core Indicators

Core indicator 2: Direct and indirect beneficiaries reached

Core indicator 4: Hectares of natural resource areas brought about improved low-emission and/or climate-resilient land use

Core indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low-emission climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner

Core indicator 6: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards.

## D

### D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

Under the proposed project, a new innovative model will be implemented which brings together host communities and refugee camps through a mutually beneficial, ecosystem-based adaptation landscape planning approach that will address the climate vulnerability of underserved populations in climate finance — refugees and their host communities.

The sustainability of adaptation actions in the region requires addressing the needs of both groups as critical populations impacting the landscape. Addressing only host community needs and impacts on the landscape would not address the root causes of conflict over resources, while addressing only refugee needs would not change the paradigm to date of adaptation policies in the refugee-host community setting, yet that involve the two communities, local government, and other stakeholders in a participatory process.

Current investments are limited to sectoral and fragmented solutions such as food security, water supply, health care, education, and others. The landscape approach proposed by this project could be catalytic and bring together different actors and implementing partners in the region for activities to implement adaptation actions at the landscape level with refugees and host communities and Oxfam will support policy dialogue on how to scale up and apply UNHCR in other operations.

In Kigoma, after the exit of the GCF investment, it is expected that local government and other stakeholders will continue to work together for the implementation of adaptation actions. The long term will have the capacity to plan and implement adaptation investments at the landscape level based on the project’s approach and lessons learned from this pilot.

This project contributes to broader strategic benefits bringing equitable benefits for host communities and refugees as well as increases the protection space for refugees. Kigoma has been home to refugees for at least five decades in various locations, and the refugee camps are located within the same landscape as the host community. The landscape, social relations, and economy in Kigoma.

The project recognizes their presence in the region and the need to address their needs and impacts on the landscape. The project will be implemented through a participatory way resulting in minimal conflict over resources in the region, supporting self-reliance among refugees, and improving livelihoods for both groups. This is an innovative approach that brings together different stakeholders.

At the project level, the inclusive landscape-level adaptation will be achieved through a comprehensive assessment of climate vulnerability, including the identification of key challenges and opportunities to provide refugee services and provision of sustainable energy for cooking; complementing development projects in the region that are addressing non-climate change related issues such as water supply, health care, education, etc. The project will also address biomass energy demand by promoting distribution of improved stoves, promoting a cooking good, cooking practices, and other measures to reduce wood fuel consumption.

The landscape approach proposed by this project could be catalytic and bring together different actors and implementing partners in the region for activities to implement adaptation actions at the landscape level with refugees and host communities. The long term will have the capacity to plan and implement adaptation investments at the landscape level based on the project’s approach and lessons learned from this pilot.

The project will also address biomass energy demand by promoting distribution of improved stoves, promoting a cooking good, cooking practices, and other measures to reduce wood fuel consumption.

### Benefits

- **Savings can support household expenses for food, health care, education, and other needs.**
- **Improved food security and nutrition outcomes.**
- **Improved financial resilience.**
- **Contributions to empowerment of women and girls, including women and girls with disabilities.**

## D

Through this, a paradigm shift will be achieved whereby land use planning and activities move away from unsustainable practices towards more sustainable livelihoods towards approaches that promote climate-resilient sustainable development. This will be achieved by:

- Activities 1.1 and 2.1: Land-use planning (Activities 1.1 and 2.1), technologies and practices (Activities 2.1–2.3 and 3.1–3.3): to build lasting local resilience to climate change by reducing the rates of land degradation, ensuring sustainable livelihoods and promoting adaptation.
- Activity 3.1: Improving access to sustainable financing mechanisms (i.e., Savings Cooperatives) (Activities 3.1–3.3): and building capacity for decision-makers in Tanzania and beyond.
- Activity 4.1: Supporting mainstreaming of climate change adaptation into policies, plans, and strategies in Tanzania and broader humanitarian programs, with capacity building for policy- and decision-makers.

### To our knowledge, this project is the first of its kind that addresses adaptation needs of host communities and refugees in an integrated way by institutionalizing climate-resilience and EDA practices. This approach has the potential to lead global change in how we address climate change adaptation at scale. The project will be implemented in 10 refugee host regions, among them being some of the most vulnerable to climate change (Bangladesh, Jordan). The project will shift the focus from a narrow view of climate change adaptation as an add-on to development projects and policies towards approaches that do not consider climate change impacts, to integrated solutions that build resilience to climate change in the face of increasing variability. It is well known that land degradation compounds climate change effects to produce vulnerability for communities in Kilombero, Lwese, the Kagera region, and other parts of Tanzania. The project will address this by promoting sustainable livelihoods practices of village camp planners and governing authorities — as well as refugees and local communities — through integrated approaches.

Output 4 action support replication and scaling up of the project results through sharing of lessons learned and mainstreaming more broadly in the refugee and host community context within Tanzania and beyond. Activities in the project will be scaled-up to other regions in Tanzania, with a focus on climate change adaptation policy mainstreaming within district, regional, and national governance arrangements in Tanzania will leverage the project contributions to inform and influence policy development at scale. The model has been tested through successful interventions. On a global level, the ecosystem management and adaptation model developed in the project will be replicated by other countries with similar ecosystems and climate change challenges.

The model can be scaled-up and replicated in other refugee hosting regions by UNHCR and other humanitarian partners using lessons learned from this project, including national economic valuation tools, policy briefs, guidelines, and other knowledge and communication products.

UNHCR and UNDP support approaches that focus on the integration of effective practices by States and sub-regional organizations to address climate change adaptation challenges. The project will provide specific advice on these challenges. As Tanzania is a frontrunner in applying the One UN working model, UN Agencies, including UNICEF and UNDP, are committed to supporting this project through their respective country offices and through the Development Partners Group on environment. As a result, UNDP as UN and UNICEF as one of the EEs of the project will provide technical support for the implementation of the project.

The lessons from this project will be used to inform UNHCR and UNDP contributions and inputs to these policy processes. The project will also contribute to the development of a climate change adaptation action plan from the project, which would produce policy recommendations and briefs. Through this, the project will provide an actionable model that can be replicated so that the world’s most vulnerable populations benefit from climate finance.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 104

### Environment

- Improved ecosystem services. Studies have shown that in Tanzania an increase is community-based land use planning and forest management practices, as well as a shift from slash-and-burn agriculture to agroforestry, has led to increased reforestation of habitats and an increase in ecological interaction diversity, which is critical for ensuring climate resilience.
- Sequestration of 762,073 million tCO2e and emission reductions of 3.2 million tCO2e over 20 years through improved forest management practices, agroforestry, and afforestation (estimated using the FAO Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool®). Evaluation of the potential for carbon sequestration and emissions reduction in Tanzania is ongoing.
- In 2019, UNCDF's consultations with UNFCCC and FAO confirmed that this is an important mitigation co-benefit through forest-based interventions, which can be scaled up to other countries and markets.

### Social and health benefits

- The forestry process of land use change is an open, equal system for involving the public in planning and decision-making and increases social cohesion and peaceful co-existence.
- Increase in quality of life from reduced risk of water- and vector-borne illnesses during flood incidents. Reduced fire risks through improved forest management practices.
- Diversification and woodland provision reducing the need of local communities to enter forested areas for medicinal plants, food, and other resources.
- Reduction of risk conflicts and competition for resource use - improved socio-economic among host communities and refugees.

104 FAO (2019). Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-Act), Version 8.5.4. Accessed: 13 November 2019 URL: https://www.fao.org/forestry/ex-act/en/

The guidelines are based on lessons learned from the experience of managing forest resources in other forested areas in Tanzania and elsewhere. The content is broadly comparable to that in the Kigoma region of Tanzania.

108 In 2016, the FAO conducted a study in Kigoma region of Tanzania to assess opportunities for scaling up forest-based interventions for supplying wood fuel to refugees. The study was commissioned by UNHCR and funded through consultations with the UNFCCC on the potential to support afforestation and reforestation through the clean development mechanism (CDM) and other carbon markets. In addition, the study also assessed the potential of the CDM for monitoring CDM projects and the current and expected mid-term global carbon prices. The UNFCCC did not advise UNHCR to pursue the CDM option as it was considered too risky at that time.
109 FAO study in Kigoma showed that forestry in refugee areas will contribute measurably to carbon sequestration (FAO, 2018) and would be a valuable co-benefit of forest-based interventions for supplying wood fuel to refugees.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 105

## Gender
- Reduction of risk for gender-based violence, particularly those that typically occur during firewood collection.
- Training for women to take leading roles in Savings Associations, livelihoods activities and participatory land-use planning processes.

### Community Savings Associations
- Community Savings Associations will be set up with the project by becoming members of Savings Associations. The objective is that at least 50% will be female. The Savings Associations will be designed to ensure that women are able to save money for their families.
- A special savings account will be established in each Savings Association, and women will be instructed to save a balance of members' genders (in line with existing Savings Association practices).
- An opportunity will also be provided for women to receive training on financial literacy. This will benefit beneficiaries in the neighbouring communities.

## D.A. Needs of refugees and host communities (Kigoma Region, Tanzania)
As described above, Kigoma is one of the poorest and most vulnerable regions in Tanzania. The adaptation requirements of the country's rural poor, including refugees, focus on increasing water availability and quality, sustainable land management practices, and improved livelihoods. These are also key components for implementing climate-resilient agriculture. Furthermore, these requirements are strongly aligned to the self-identified needs of host communities and refugees.

### Key challenges
- The rapid urbanization and environmental degradation in Kigoma.
- Improving planning and protection of natural resources, including forest resources, is needed to halt the negative patterns of land and ecosystem degradation on which many rural households depend for their livelihoods. This will also help to meet the country's economic needs over time.

### Key opportunities
- The availability of water resources that host communities and refugees depend on.
- Climate-smart agriculture practices can be introduced, including improved crop varieties and better water management techniques.
- Improved access to financial services within the Kigoma region. Within the Kigoma region, the availability of financial services is limited.

### Disbursement funds
Disbursing funds through Savings Associations to deliver climate-resilient interventions is a critical tool of the project. The proposed project will also use the funds to support women's groups and other community-based organizations in the project area.
The proposed project will scale up financial inclusion activities to improve access to finance for the host community and climate-smart agriculture practices, including improved crop varieties and better water management techniques.

### The need to address refugees' unique vulnerabilities
Refugees are newly university educated from natural or social sciences. They have a strong desire to contribute to their communities by meeting the adaptation needs of both host communities and refugees, which can be applied locally and globally.

Another aim of the proposed project is to address critical institutional capacity needs in Kigoma. Many of the project's interventions will require the involvement of local government officials, including district officers, sub-district officers, and village chiefs. The project will also involve other key stakeholders such as police officers. However, local government and civil society capacity to deliver such complex, multi-stakeholder interventions is considerably limited.

For example, district-level staff are not trained in the UNFCCC District Environmental Management System (DEMS).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 106

Committees will have the capacity necessary to support the Ward and Village Environmental Management Committees. A critical intervention of the project is to address the overarching institutional capacity deficit in climate change adaptation, which is a major barrier to effective implementation of climate change adaptation strategies. Once representatives, will receive capacity building and training to develop climate informed land use plans that are owned by communities and that take into account the impacts of climate change on natural resources as well as risks in regional planning processes. Additionally, the policy review process will support the institutionalisation of the impacts and lessons learned from this project so as to demonstrate benefits.

## D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximate 1 page)

The Tanzania Development Vision 2035 aspires to have the country transformed into a middle income and semi-industrialized economy by 2035. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) is one of the key strategies in the Second Tanzania National Five-Year Development Plan 2016–2021, currently under implementation, defines specific national priorities for adaptation to climate change. The NAP was developed with input from the Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism, the Ministry of Environment, Climate Change and Forestry, the President’s Office and aligns with their plans and strategies, including: i) the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA); ii) the National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSSD); iii) the Intergovernmental Climate Change Adaptation into National Sectoral Policies, Plans and Programmes of Tanzania (2012).

Several of the project activities are listed in the proposed “topmost urgent and immediate projects” in the NAPA:

* increase irrigation by using appropriate water efficient technologies to boost crop production;
* diversify agricultural production through the introduction of drought tolerant and fast growing tree species; and
* establish good land tenure systems and facilitate sustainable human settlements.

The project activities are also well aligned with relevant national sector-level priorities in water, agriculture and land use. The project will contribute towards the achievement of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and targets: i) SDG 2: Zero Hunger; ii) SDG 6: Clean Water and Sanitation; iii) SDG 7: Affordable and Clean Energy; iv) SDG 11: Sustainable Cities and Communities; v) SDG 13: Climate Action. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the following national sector-level priorities in water, agriculture and land use development and management practices; i) enhanced protection and conservation of water catchments; ii) enhancing agricultural productivity through improved irrigation systems; iii) promoting sustainable land use and soil fertility (2014); and the Climate-Smart Agriculture Programme for Tanzania (2015-2025) have the objective of an “agricultural sector that is climate-resilient, productive, efficient, competitive and environmentally sustainable for rural development”. The Tanzania climate-smart agriculture (CSA) guideline – developed in accordance with numerous international CSA guidelines – aims to provide a framework for the implementation of CSA. It covers 95% of the nations’ rainfall needs. As a large proportion of the sector is rainfed crop production, and most households have limited access to irrigation systems, it is important that the CSA guidelines are designed to help farmers develop the guidelines are closely aligned with the government’s commitment to creating a resilient, climate smart agriculture sector in Tanzania. The CSA guidelines aim to address the challenges posed by climate change and changes and a behavioural shift of the population. The CSA guidelines identify several key areas where capacity building is required for the successful implementation of the project. These include: i) improved agricultural services; and improved agricultural risk management mechanisms[17]. Several projects in Kigoma have had a specific focus on improving access to agricultural extension services, which has contributed significantly to poverty reduction in the population[20]. These projects also demonstrate the need to improve dialogue between stakeholders[20].

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 3 pages)

This project has been designed to use GCF funds as efficiently and effectively as possible, while recognizing the unique challenges faced by refugee hosting regions. This is achieved through consideration of the points below:

* **Community-based Natural Resource Management (CBNRM):** CBNRM is a key component in addressing the environmental impacts of climate change on ecosystems, ecosystem services, and agro-ecological systems.
* **Water harvesting technologies:** The project will support water harvesting technologies to improve access to clean drinking water for refugees and host communities. This will be achieved through the implementation of Integrated Water Resource Management (IWRM) in key catchments within the refugee camps.

The regulatory framework in respect of refugees in Tanzania is set out in the Refugees Act, 1998 (the 1998 Act) and the 2015 Regulations made under that Act. The 1998 Act was amended by the Refugee Act, 2015 (the 2015 Act). The Tanzania Constitution of 2001 provides for the protection of refugees in accordance with international law. The 1998 Act and the 2015 Regulations are based on the 1967 Protocol Relating to the Status of Refugees and the 1967 Protocol Relating to the Status of Refugees. The Tanzanian Government has adopted a policy approach to addressing the needs of both host communities and refugees follows principles in the Global Compact for Migration, which calls for countries of origin, transit, and destination to work together to ensure that migration policies and development responses support both groups as effective management of refugee situations are based on the recognition that refugees and host communities live side by side. The project will contribute towards ensuring that refugees and host communities are embedded in the same landscape and benefit from the same ecosystem services and resources that they share.

In discussions on the project with the Tanzania NDC (Vice President’s Office: Division of Environment) throughout 2017-2021, the NDC expressed continued support and prioritization of the project to UNEP, UNHCR and GCF and invited the Vice President’s Office to continue to engage in discussions on the project. In a meeting held with the Tanzania NDC on September 28th, 2021, it was agreed that the Government of Tanzania, acting through its Vice-President’s Office, and UNHCR will co-execute the project. The Vice President’s Office will be responsible for providing overall guidance to the project team in terms of planning processes and promote the sustainability of project outcomes at the local, regional and national level. As described above, the NDC has also agreed that the Vice-President’s Office will act as the lead executing entity of this project, with the Vice-President’s Office, DcII acting as the lead executing entity. Additional consultations were held between the Tanzania NDC and the Vice President’s Office on September 28th, 2021 and again on September 30th, 2021 to inform the revision of the funding proposal and the new execution arrangement.

In addition to the executive entities, the Government of Tanzania (at ministerial, regional and district level) will be responsible for providing overall guidance to the project team in terms of planning processes and promote the sustainability of project outcomes at the local, regional and national level. The Vice President’s Office will act as the lead executing entity of this project.

A series of stakeholder consultations have been conducted throughout project preparation to gauge interest, solicit feedback and encourage ownership; the outcomes of which have been incorporated into the project design. The stakeholders consulted included representatives from government at all levels (national, regional and district), representatives of host communities and refugees. These engagements were aimed at gathering information about existing policies, laws and regulations that may impact on the implementation of the project as well as identifying and providing opportunities to discuss needs and desires of the targeted population. Details of the stakeholders consulted are provided in Annex 1.

**dual purpose** being demand-led whilst also rooted in best practice will ensure that GCF funds are efficiently utilized when they are needed most and on proven interventions.

* **Irrigation:** The project area has a high potential for irrigation, with water availability being the limiting factor. For example, the irrigation needs for agriculture of the project are met by a combination of rainwater-harvesting systems, solar-powered pumps and hand-pumps, as well as gravity-fed channels and small-scale irrigation schemes (e.g., flood irrigation). The project will also support the development of water harvesting structures described above (although a limited number may draw directly from natural water courses).

This approach is more efficient than using GCF funds to build large dams or other infrastructure projects. It will be implemented through village land use planning process. Treadle pumps represent a more-efficient use of GCF capital to meet irrigation requirements, as they are less expensive and can be installed in small groups. The project area has a high potential for treadle pump irrigation, with the village land use planning process.

## Considered indicators for groundwater recharge and flood attenuation may be considerable. The economic analysis planned under Activity 4.1 is expected to reveal the value of a broader spectrum of ecosystem services associated with these activities.

### Climate-resilient agriculture is shown to provide an IRR of 541%, with an associated NPV of US$1,815 million over the 20 year period under the "with project scenario" relative to the "without project scenario". The benefits measured under the "with project scenario" include increased yields and reduced risk of crop failure. These benefits are estimated for those as those associated with a higher consistency production relative to traditional agriculture, have not been captured as part of this analysis.

The water harvesting and irrigation interventions under Activity 3.2 have been found to generate an IRR of 197%. The NPV was estimated to be US$13 billion over the one year period, which is equivalent to ~US$75 billion per year.

The IRR for promotion of beehives under Sub-Activity 3.3.1 is undefined, given that there are no years in which net benefits are greater than zero. The NPV was estimated at US$20 million over the 20-year period, which equates to ~US$1 billion per year.

The IRR for promotion of mushroom cultivation was estimated to generate an IRR of 787%, and an NPV of US$436 million over the 20-year period. The NPV is equivalent to ~US$219 million per year, which equates to ~US$5 billion per year.

All NPV figures presented in Table 12 have been estimated using a discount rate of 9%, in accordance with the rate used in FAO & UNCTAD's 2018 Cost-benefit analysis of forestry interventions for supplying wood fuel in a refugee context. The economic analysis uses the social rate of time preference as the discount rate.

### Table 12: Internal rate of return and net financial present value for each of the listed activities and sub-activities. IRR for activity 3.3.1 is undefined, given that there are no years in which net benefits are less than zero for this activity.

| --- | --- | --- |
| 2.1. Irrigation and manure management systems, and 30 horticulturalists | 541% | US$9,288,096 |
| 2.2. Undertake afforestation on village land to e.g. reduce cereal production pressure | 50% | US$2,953,973 |
| 2.3. Provide inputs for climate-resilient agriculture | 197% | US$1,577,457 |
| 3.2.1 Water harvesting and irrigation interventions | 197% | US$806,447 |
| 3.2.2 Promote beekeeping for forest conservation in the village | Undefined | N/A |
| 3.2.3 Promote mushroom cultivation for marginalised groups with access to land | 787% | US$533,763 |

## Including mitigation costs

29 % | 25,862,214 | 50

The results of financial and economic analyses were subjected to sensitivity analysis to assess their level of robustness to changes in key parameters, and to ascertain switching values. The results, outlined in Table 14, show that Sub-Activity 3.1.2 (inputs for climate-resilient agriculture) is most sensitive to changes in key cost and benefit streams. Under a 50% increase in costs, the IRR switches from positive to negative (a moderate IRR for Sub-Activity 2.2.2 [afforestation]). Also highlighted in the table, a 35% reduction in benefits results in a negative IRR for Sub-Activity 2.2.1 (agroforestry). The analysis also shows that the sensitivity of costs is greater than the magnitude of benefits. When mitigation benefits are excluded from the analysis, a 61% reduction in project benefits results in an IRR switching to negative.

In contrast to changes in project benefits, the outcomes of the analysis appear to be relatively robust to changes in costs. Of note is that when costs incurred by beneficiaries in the "with project" scenario are increased by 30%, the IRR for Sub-Activity 2.2.1 (agroforestry) increases from a moderate positive to a high positive, while the IRRs associated with Sub-Activity 2.2.1 (agroforestry) are increased by 55%. The IRR switches to negative. The project as a whole, however, remains robust in terms of its financial viability even when mitigation benefits are excluded and a 230% increase in costs when mitigation benefits are included.

As mentioned previously, the full range of project benefits has not been included in the cost-benefit analysis. By considering only the direct and indirect benefits, a significant proportion of the total costs has been omitted from the analysis. These findings should therefore be taken as representing a cautious outlook.

| Increase in activity implementation costs | 2.2 | 3.2 | 5.2 | 10.2 | 16.2 | 33.2 | 49.2 | 78.2 |
| Decrease in magnitude of benefits | 35% | 30% | 13% | 61% | 91% | 71% | 61% | 71% |

E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

This section refers to the project programme’s logical framework in accordance with the GCF’s Integrated Results Management Framework to which the project programme contributes as a whole, including in respect of any co-financing.

# E1. Project/Programme Focus

Please indicate whether this proposal is for a mitigation or adaptation programme. For cross-cutting proposals, select both.
- Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- Increased resilience (adaptation)

# E2.C GC Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1 to 2 pages)

This section of the logical framework is meant to help a project/programme monitor and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in Section D.2 above by addressing the following assessment dimensions.

Accordingly, for each assessment dimension (as defined per assessment in the accompanying guidance note), describe the current state (baseline) and the potential impact of the project/programme on this dimension. The description should be concise but sufficiently detailed to demonstrate how the project/programme will contribute to that shift transformation under respective assessment dimensions (cost, replicability and sustainability). In doing so, please refer to specific outputs and/or types of change.

## Assessment Dimension
### Current state (baseline)
- **Scale**: Kigoma region is part of the Lake Tanganyika Basin, a globally important biodiversity hotspot. Forests in the Kigoma region are among the most biodiverse woodlands.
- **Description**: Tanzania ranks 8th in the GDP ranking and 26 in the HDI ranking of African countries. Currently developing, Kigoma region is one of 30 regions in Tanzania with a population of ~2.3 million people.

### Rating
**High**

### Potential target scenario (Description)
The project landscape-based approach offers an opportunity to address climate adaptation that consider resource use, land management, biodiversity ecology and complex development challenges. The project will provide a platform for ecosystem-based interventions which include solutions to reduce vulnerability of displaced refugees through adaptation solutions at the landscape level. These interventions across sectors that are likely to be affected by climate change will ensure implementation of activities that are

### How the project/programme will contribute (Description)
Outputs 1-3 will implement adaptation measures in the context of land use and refugee communities. Outputs 4-5 will support the replication of the project results through capacity building, policy development and advocacy for ecosystem-based solutions. Output 4 activities including ecosystem-based interventions will provide guidelines and policy briefs, and policy recommendations within district, regional and national governance arrangements

Tanzania as whole. Approximately 10 million ha of forest was lost in Tanzania between 1990 and 2015, the majority of which occurred in miombo woodlands. Miombo woodlands cover approximately 34% of Botswana, Burundi, Democratic Republic of Congo, Eswatini, Malawi, Mozambique, Namibia, Tanzania, Zambia and Zimbabwe, covering around 2.4 million km².

Today, more than 100 refugee camps are present today in Tanzania, with most refugees being in the two camps in Kigoma.

There are now more than 100 refugee camps in Tanzania, around 75% of whom are hosted by one or two families living in protected circumstances. Approximately 240,000 Burundian refugees (24% of the refugees under UNHCR protection) and 16 million IDPs (30% of concern to UNHCR) live in Tanzania. The climate change could contribute to the migration of people within their own countries by 2050, unless inclusive development actions are taken now. According to WFP’s RCP 4.5 mid-report, SRF and refugee settlements are concentrated in regions that are exposed to extreme weather levels and specific climate hazards such as temperature extremes and drought.

Experience from elsewhere in Tanzania shows that the structures are in place villages are able to manage their own land use management with minimal external intervention. The project will expand widespread effort, with the villages taking responsibility for managing their village land should be used to improve the livelihoods of refugees and achieve status in the form of a Customary Community Forest (CCF). Achieving these goals will allow the host communities to continue expanding their land-use planning (including the development of CCFs) and to land use to adapt to more complex climate change. This, in turn, will ensure sustainability in the long term.

The Tanzania Forest Service will be able to provide technical assistance extending the area under plantation forest management within the region after the project has ended. We expect that the project will also contribute to the District Council and the host communities to plan to adapt to future changes in climate.

To our knowledge, this project is the first of its kind in Tanzania to host communities and refugees in an integrated approach for climate-resilience and EBA practices. This

Tanzania will leverage the project outputs to achieve the desired adaptation outcomes.

Lake Tanganyika is a key biodiversity area for forest conservation actions. Building capacity will be provided to support district offices and Tanzania Forest Service staff in the implementation of the project. ## Replicability

Though the districts of Kilondo, Kigoma and Mwanga in the Western region are among the poorest, and the least developed (GDP per capita -USD 503 – is particularly low compared to other regions), many of the development challenges faced by these areas are common across the country. Approximately one-third of the population of ~47 million live below the poverty line, with subsistence agriculture being the primary livelihood for most households in the country — including the majority of women and their families. Agriculture is a dominant risk factor in this region, accounting for 25% of GDP. Despite fertile soils, good water resources, the agricultural potential, and high rainfall, productivity and persistent poverty are major challenges.

The project will overcome key policy and institutional barriers to scaling up climate resilience at the landscape level such as:

- Limited capacity in local institutions and communities to plan and implement interventions that address long-term climate vulnerabilities.
- Refugees and the special needs of internally displaced persons (IDPs) articulated in adaptation policies targeting vulnerable groups, including agriculture, forestry or land use change.
- Lack of knowledge to improve climate resilience.

### High

- Low purchasing power among smallholder farmers limits access to inputs for resilient agricultural investments.
- Limited capacity in Kigoma region to plan and implement interventions that address long-term climate risks.

During the implementation period, policy environment remains favourable to the scaling up of climate-resilient agriculture policies, plans and programs as identified by the National Determined Contributions (NDCs) identifies.

The project will use the results of the proposed interventions implemented to demonstrate the effectiveness, replicability, cost-effectiveness, and scalability of the outputs and fund-level outcomes at the national level. The project will promote 1) inclusive solutions to adaptation needs of host communities and refugees through mainstreaming climate change considerations in sector policies and plans and 2) implementation of activities that are co-designed with local communities where the people and landscape would be most vulnerable to the impacts of climate shocks.

Interventions culminating in the issuance of a Certificate of Customary Right (CCR) will empower communities to protect their land, improve livelihoods through agroforestry, pastureland, strengthening community forest management institutions and ensuring conservation benefits to the users. 

Community Based Forest Managements (CBFMs) will be established for sustainable forest systems and enhance ecosystem services. This will reduce pressure on the agrigulture and afforestation of natural forests and promote alternative sources for energy needs through improved agroforestry, silvopastoral forests and allowing them to regenerate naturally. The project will also support indigenous communities in establishing conservation plantations that will be established on degraded lands.

Agrisettlement interventions by providing livelihoods opportunities such as livestock for crops, and sustainable fuelwood production systems will reduce the behavior that degrade the natural forest.

food in camps, and a strict encampment policy which has led to limited livelihood activities within the camp area. Figure 1 shows the most affected region by wildfires, with at least four fires recorded annually. The risk of uncontrolled fires is expected to increase under projected climate change conditions. These conditions include more frequent and higher intensity rainfall events, which will reduce the stability of soils on agricultural lands and increase runoff and nutrient loss through erosion.

A degraded landscape with low soil productivity, reduced water supply availability, and reduced soil moisture retention capacity makes these sites susceptible to the impacts of climate change. This is a threat to both agricultural and other livelihood activities as well as the limited resources required for these vulnerable communities in times of flooding.

## Outcome 1: Strengthened policy and institutional arrangements to promote climate-resilient land-use planning and management at the landscape level.

### Capacity of communities and local authorities to conduct ecosystem-based adaptation planning and implement sustainable land-use practices in response to climate change and variability

#### Capacity assessment scored measurements

| Participants to the C-LUP process and supporting operational structures (Village Councils, WUGAs, EMGs, District government) | Participants to the C-LUP process and supporting operational structures (Village Councils, WUGAs, EMGs, District government) | Participants to the C-LUP process and supporting operational structures (Village Councils, WUGAs, EMGs, District government) |
|---|---|---|
| Capacity to conduct ecosystem-based adaptation planning and resource allocation. As a result of land-use planning and activities in large-scale ecosystems will occur unsustainable unsuitable unsustainable landscape degradation towards climate-resilient | Capacity to conduct ecosystem-based adaptation planning and resource allocation. As a result of land-use planning and activities in large-scale ecosystems will occur unsustainable unsuitable unsustainable landscape degradation towards climate-resilient | Capacity to conduct ecosystem-based adaptation planning and resource allocation. As a result of land-use planning and activities in large-scale ecosystems will occur unsustainable unsuitable unsustainable landscape degradation towards climate-resilient |
| Different groups are able to use the C-LUP process for ongoing allocation of village forest resources. | Different groups are able to use the C-LUP process for ongoing allocation of village forest resources. | Different groups are able to use the C-LUP process for ongoing allocation of village forest resources. |

**Note:** Level 1: communities and local authorities are aware of climate risk but have insufficient awareness on how landscape degradation and unsustainable practices contribute to increased climate risk or how ecosystem-based adaptation can enhance resilience. Level 2: communities and local authorities understand that landscape degradation affects the ecological services provided by large-scale ecosystems and show support to shift from unsustainable practices leading to landscape degradation, towards climate-resilient landscapes and livelihoods. Level 3: communities and local authorities are aware of the benefits of different ecosystem-based adaptation interventions and how these interventions are implemented by communities and local authorities resulting in a shift from unsustainable practices leading to landscape degradation, towards climate-resilient landscapes and livelihoods.

### Notes:
1. Although not enumerated here (because of the difficulty of attribution) the flood management and rainwater harvesting interventions will also have indirect impacts on the range of ecosystem services provided by forests.
2. Improved agricultural practices, such as crop rotation, intercropping, and agroforestry can help to reduce soil erosion and improve soil fertility.
3. Additionally improved agricultural outputs may relieve pressure of natural ecosystems by reducing land use change and the need for biogas (more efficient cooking stoves) and firewood.
4. Improved water harvesting interventions will also help relieve pressure of the natural forests, that are intended to be protected under CBFM. The economic valuation in Output 6 will be quantifying the benefits.
5. The LVI-POC Index® data comes from a (least vulnerable to climate) 1 model (upgrading the 2005) LVI methodology and incorporating the POC vulnerability definition. Group 1 is least vulnerable to climate change, Group 2 moderately vulnerable (e.g., livelihoods, adaptive capacity socio-demographic profile, livelihood strategies, social networks), Group 2 mortality (health, access to food, access to water) and Group 3 extreme exposure (e.g., drought, floods, landslides).

## 2.1. Implement Community-Based Forest Management (CBFM) for resilient ecosystems

The principal objective of this intervention is to establish and manage 30,000 ha of Village Land Forest Reserves.

### 2.1.1. Establish and manage 30,000 ha of Village Land Forest Reserves
- Approved management plan for each village land forest reserve approved by the District Council
- Active FMCs in ~20 villages (TBC)
- Forestry and natural resources extension officers trained and in training in implementing CBFM
- Staff trained and in training in the use of drone and GIS-based monitoring system

This is an indicative number, averaging the populations of the host communities in the three districts, since the exact number and sites of villages will only be determined during project implementation.

## 2.2. Implement 12,000 hectares of agroforestry and image land management to increase resilience and land use

### 2.2.1. To establish and maintain 10,000 ha of agroforestry ecosystems that are critical to meet the project’s objectives.

- **Establishment**: 10,000 ha of agroforestry established
- **Afforestation**: 2,000 ha afforested land established

### 2.2.2. To undertake afforestation on 2,000 ha to establish nurseries for tree seedlings and plant suitable tree species which will help in the regeneration of degraded mimo forest and allow it to regenerate and provide livelihood support functions.

- **Establishment**: 15 representatives from TFS and District Forest Department trained in agroforestry and capacity building services in the establishment, maintenance, and monitoring of agroforestry and afforestation

### 2.3. Implement flood and erosion control in demarcated areas to reduce the exposure of people living on the riverbank sides which have little or no vegetation cover.

- **The refugee camps are exposed to a high rate of flooding and erosion, owing to the lack of vegetation cover. The interventions described above will help to reduce the level of runoff and erosion in the project area, thereby reducing the number of camp-boundaries because of their density.**

### 2.3.1. To establish sustainable Drainage System (SDS) in the project area by constructing trenches and basins, the number and size of which are determined during project implementation.

- **Drainage channels (-10.16m)**: Gabions (-16)


---
**Page 141**
---

The principal objective of this intervention is to provide engineering solutions for areas most susceptible to erosion from high winds and heavy rainfall. The solutions themselves will be designed and implemented in response to changing weather extremes such as droughts and floods. The final design and distribution of the interventions will be subject to a technical assessment and stakeholder consultations.

The interventions proposed under this intervention include: (1) promotion of modern technologies and management practices to strengthen capacity of farmers, district officials and agricultural extension workers in climate-resilient agriculture; (2) building capacity of 60 farmer per Farmer Field School (FFS) on climate-smart agriculture through the use of traditional knowledge, though FFS training with the farmers, district officials and extension services. (3) provision of 50 resilient seedcuttings and 1 agricultural tool kit set to each SAWO for implementation through sampling associations to each of the 20 Farmer Field Schools established.

This project seeks to strengthen and scale up Savings Associations. The funds will be disbursed through a market-based mechanism, with farmers’ cooperatives in the region to have access to credit facilities at collectively negotiated prices, facilitate tax

## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

An M&E plan will be developed in the PIM for the duration of the project to conduct and supervise the monitoring and reporting on the project's activities. The M&E office will be supported by an M&E expert appointed as part of the Project Team. The M&E plan will include a baseline survey, mid-term surveys and assessments (part 30-35 days/year), and end-of-project surveys and assessments to monitor the results framework (see Annex 11 for further details). The M&E plan will be reviewed by the Evaluation Office and approved by the Project Steering Committee. Stakeholders at the district and regional level and with relevant ministries particularly the Regional Administrative Secretariat, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Health, Ministry of Education, Ministry of Lands, Housing and Human Settlement Development. Monitoring and evaluation reports, drafts of project progress reports, and other related documents will be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner.

An independent ex-ante evaluation (baseline evaluation) will be circulated to the relevant ministries and the Project Steering Committee for comments.

A UNDP Task Manager will be responsible for monitoring progress against budget and outcome indicators during project implementation. At key points (i.e., baseline, mid-point and end of project) the project team will carry out a self-assessment (internal evaluation) which will include an assessment of progress towards achieving the intended outcomes by project completion in the most efficient and sustainable way. The Project Steering Committee will review these reports and provide feedback to the project team on their performance and recommendations along with an implementation plan. It is the responsibility of the UNDP Task Manager to monitor, report and evaluate the project against the approved baseline indicators.

An independent ex-post evaluation (terminal evaluation) TE) will take place once the project has reached operational completion. The Evaluation Office of UNDP will be responsible for the TE, which is a summative evaluation that provides an overall assessment of the project's performance in relation to its intended outcomes and impact against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, sustainability). The evaluation will include desk reviews, key informant interviews, stakeholder interviews etc. In most cases, a field mission. Each evaluation criterion will be rated using a six-point scale from 1 (poor) to 6 (excellent). The evaluation report will provide an overall assessment of the project as a whole. Where there are any differences in ratings between the evaluation team and the Evaluation Office, this issue will be discussed with the Project Steering Committee before the report is finalized.

The draft ex-post evaluation (terminal evaluation) report will be sent to project stakeholders during the finalization stage of the evaluation by the Evaluation Office. The final comments on the report will be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be made available for public scrutiny.

The total 5-year cost associated to project monitoring and evaluation is €1,421,283, including:

- Safeguards and gender specialist €228,000
- M&E Officer €246,000
- Sociologist (for international safeguards expert) (international consultant part-time 30-35 days/year) €99,000
- M&E officer (international consultant part-time 30-35 days/year) €99,000
- Implementation of Safeguards and gender mainstreaming Plan ESMS €75,000
- Monitoring and evaluation reports (Annexes Part 10) €246,000
- Implementation of M&E plan €222,783
- Baseline evaluation €99,000
- Interim evaluation: Mid-Term Review €114,500

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk Assessment and Mitigation measures (max.3 pages)

The main risk factors include political, technical and operational, social and environmental and financial risks.

Section G2 discusses the environmental and social risks identified in the Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) and Section G3 provides a list of mitigation measures for these risks. The section also includes monitoring frameworks for the identified risks, taking into account lessons learned from previous interventions in the area. Risks are coordinated by the Project Management Unit (PMU), with support from the UNHCR Regional Office and UNHCR as the Executive Entity, and UNEP as the Accredited Entity.

VPO established a risk management policy and framework in 2018; this was driven by the fact that managing risk has become an integral part of project implementation to contribute towards the achievement of the public objectives. Risk coordinators are charged with the overall responsibility of reporting risk management issues to the Project Management Unit (PMU) and the UNHCR Regional Office.

VPO will consolidate, analyse and translate risk information reported by different project partners including UNHCR, TFS, MoHA, KRG, UNEP, etc. into a consolidated risk register for the project.

The role of the Safeguard and Gender officer at PMU is to monitor risks as they arise, and facilitate avoidance, mitigation or response measures. The safeguard and gender officer will be supported by a Safeguard expert implementing cultural (part-time) to develop, implement and monitor the protection and gender inclusion strategy and the Gender and Social Inclusion Plan. The recruited person will work closely with the Project Management Unit, the UNHCR Regional Office and other relevant stakeholders.

In the event of significant risks to the project, VPO will engage in consultations with the relevant parties (District Councils, KRG Regional Administration, TFS, MoHA, UNHCR or others) to agree on adequate measures for conflict resolution and mitigation. The process will be guided by the Project Management Unit and the UNHCR Regional Office.

The Ministry of Tanzania Vice President's Office Project Management Unit is coordinating risk management with UNHCR who will apply its approach to management of risks, including political and safeguard risks. As of 2018, UNHCR has embarked on a process to strengthen risk management across the agency through the launch of the Risk Management Framework (RMF) which aims at improving the quality of risk management in UNHCR programmes and addressing the root causes of corruption and fraud, and other types of critical risks in UNHCR. The RMF is designed to provide a common framework for managing risks that are relevant across all levels of UNHCR's operations. It will also help UNHCR to better manage its resources by ensuring that it has the necessary tools and capacity to achieve its objective of providing protection and assistance to people forced to flee. Within the UNHCR local office in Tanzania, the RMF is currently being piloted at the Dar es Salaam office with a view to having an "established" level of maturity risk. Based on a request by the UNHCR High Commissioner, work is currently under way to expand this pilot project across all levels of UNHCR operations within Tanzania and to achieve full maturity by the end of 2023. This initiative has led to the expansion of protective resources across all levels of the UNHCR operation in Dar es Salaam.

UNHCR Tanzania staff, has specified the Senior Project Control Officer to support facilitation of risk assessments, updating and maintenance of the country risk register, undertaking risk reviews, following up on risk treatments, sharing information with relevant stakeholders and ensuring that the project is aligned with the RMF. This capacity in place is to support the entire scope of work undertaken by UNHCR Tanzania, including any future GCF projects.

As an Accountability Entity level, the Task Manager, with the support of the Portfolio Manager, the Safeguarding Advisor, and the GCF Coordination Office, will take the leading role in monitoring risks in close cooperation with the Project Management Unit and UNHCR.

## Description

Water-related infrastructure fall into disrepair, lack of spare parts or lack of skills in the host communities impedes maintenance of irrigation equipment. As a result, irrigated areas are abandoned and cultivation of arid/non-arid areas increases. The project will provide technical assistance to the communities to ensure that they have the capacity to maintain the contracted service costs during the project implementation period. Beyond the lifetime of the project, O&M costs will be covered by the communities through user fees (e.g., water harvesting infrastructure maintenance, irrigation equipment and chamoirs). Majority of the maintenance work required are inspection, cleaning, desilting, and weeding (through community participation) to ensure that the irrigation systems continue to function effectively in the local markets. These include stones, brushwood, wooden posts, sandbags, wire mesh. Costs of maintenance depend on the size of the system and the type of infrastructure. Maintenance costs for a 10 ha surface area will be estimated at $3 per ha surface, and irrigation basins at $0.1 /50 m² square meter of detention basin area. The total cost will be estimated during the implementation stage since the interventions and their extent have been plotted on the project area. It is highly unlikely that the communities will be able to cover these costs from their own resources (based on local market prices). Please See Annex 2.2 for references), based on local market prices.

UNHCR, as the EE of this project activity will monitor effectiveness of training provided to water engineeers and technicians and adapt as appropriate. The project will work with suppliers of irrigation equipment such as Kobilio/Kabilo/Kitale Irrigation Equipment (KIE) and Water Harvesting Infrastructure (WHI) in Kenya. Annex 21: Operations and Maintenance has already been prepared to address relevant issues. Within the camps, assets will be turned over to the Ministry of Home Affairs (MoHA) for maintenance and management. The MoHA will provide technical assistance to communities and local development agencies can support M&V in this role. Water harvesting infrastructure in the host communities will be maintained by the communities through user fees. The project will work with water users associations (WUAs) and their user associations (WUAu) and in cooperation with the Lake Tanganyika River Basin Authority. WUAs can leverage on services provided by the LTRA to collect water user fees to support O&M. This will be determined during project implementation for payment structures and viability.

| Category | Probability | Impact |

| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

### Description

Savings Associations are unable or unwilling to increase the amount of funds under their management to the degree needed in order to implement project interventions. As a result, project funds are not able to be spent or are spent ineffectively.

### Mitigation Measures

The project will support the substantial expansion foreseen in the funds management by SAs through the establishment of a Savings Association Management Office (SAMO), which will be supported by a dedicated member of the Project Management Team. The SAMO will provide technical assistance to SA members and will also help them to increase the amount of funds handled. To mitigate this risk, the proportion of agricultural inputs supplied to farmers through the SAs will be increased from 20% in Year 1 to 50% in Years 3 & 4. The proportion of agricultural inputs will be adjusted based on the SA performance in the delivery of agricultural inputs to farmers in Years 3 & 4.

Description: Refugees return to their countries of origin. As a result, camp reduce operations.


UNHCR will keep close attention to the situation; should major developments take place that would prompt large-scale voluntary repatriation. In the unlikely event of refugee numbers dramatically decreasing or other camps), (besides those in Tanzania) are closed down, UNHCR will ensure that refugees are not left behind and that they do not feel regret and not sensitive to the fluctuation or transience of the refugees and anchored on the landscape and host communities. The government has a policy framework for refugee repatriation which is designed to promote sustainable measures that result in adaptation benefits for host communities in the long term. Adequate management mechanisms are in place to ensure that refugees are not left behind when camps are closed down. If necessary, with camp-benefits and livelihood activities being absorbed by the host communities (who are receiving similar international assistance), UNHCR will provide support to the government to address any potential risks of natural flood events and erosion damages. The Tanzania's Refugee Act of 1986 and the National Refugee Policy of 2003 serve as the legal and policy framework in the country. The Government of Tanzania aims for regional cooperation to refugee protection and management, including with other African countries and the African Development Community and the government has expressed support Global Compact on Refugees. Government actions and support will be provided to ensure that refugees are not left behind when camps are closed down and to promote existing policy of voluntary repatriation. Any forced evictions of refugees would violate the government's commitments.
