| Selected Risk Factor 7 | Other | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Category | Low | Medium | High |

### Description

Risk of tension between refugees following relocation from Minkel to Mikela or after demobilizing host communities with regard to access to fuelwood. Refugees as well as displaced and successful returnees (including IDP women) are often related to the collection of fuelwood, an increase in camp population in Ntula may exacerbate these conflicts.

The population relocated from Minkel to Ndala are of the same profile as those in Ndala. Both populations are predominantly Burundian refugees who have been living for many years in camps and settlements around Ndala which have coexisted for years with these Burundian refugees in an overall peaceful manner and have built social cohesion. The relocation of refugees from Minkel to Mikela is expected to be similar, however there will be a greater competition over access to fuelwood. Other concerns from refugees related to the consolidation adding pressure to negotiate, the new housing situation and the lack of land for agriculture are also likely to increase tensions between refugees and host communities as well as an increased competition for fuelwood. Also, longer distances may need to be tracked by refugees to meet cooking energy needs which may lead to increased SBIW incidence.


| Migration Measures (Cont.) |
| --- |

UNHCR risk assessment using UNHCR risk register methodology was undertaken for Minkel consolidation exercise and mitigation measures identified. A Task Force has been established to plan and oversee the relocation consisting of MINUSCA, MONUSCO, UNHCR, FAO, WFP, IOM, CCF, DRC (camp manager) and NIRC (chief Sweash). Furthermore, Camp Coordination Coordination Teams (CCCT) will be established in each camp. The Task Force is also responsible for the coordination of the following agencies: WASH, Shelter, ARID Health, Education, Environment, Refugee leadership, CWT, Police and Host community liaison officers. Specific protection activities/strategies have been identified to address the relocation exercise. These include specific livelihoods activities (e.g. production of charcoal briquettes) and other complementary activities. Specific measures complementary to the GCF project, primarily through increasing production and use of biomass briquettes.

The afforestation of 20 hectares of land in villages (sub-act 2.2.2) aims for a stretch that has growing woodland on one side and a village on the other. The area is estimated at 13% of the total project area, which can be met per stated assumptions in Annex A - Detailed Budget Plan). Though this is a modest direct contribution, it is an important indirect contribution to the project.

UNHCR supports regular peace co-existence meetings which bring together community leaders from refugees and host communities. These meetings are also used as a platform for sensitization of refugees on demobilization and resettlement from Minkel. With regards to conflicts among refugees in Ndala, community structures are in place to resolve these conflicts. The Task Force will be responsible for the coordination of the relocation exercise and it is expected that the same structure will be undertaken. Refugees leaders play a key role in communicating with and disseminating information to the wider refugee population. They also act as mediators between refugees and host communities. In addition, training during physical meetings with refugee leaders in practicalities as well as risks and mitigating actions (for example as identified by the protection working group) the wider refugee community will be informed and sensitized.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 150

## Category: Other
### Probability: High
### Impact: Medium

**Description:** Political risks. Tanzania's policies towards refugees have been increasingly restrictive in recent years. UNHCR Tanzania has identified a number of risks related to government policy, which may relate to Tanzania’s implementation of its asylum policy in line with international standards, access to asylum seekers fleeing conflict from neighbouring countries, risks linked to the country’s border security and migration management.

### Measure (Action): UNHCR Tanzania develops a yearly risk register to support risk identification and treatment. The most recent Risk Register was updated on 1 December 2018. The most recent Risk Register includes specific proactive and reactive treatment measures to ensure that UNHCR can proactively mitigate them and, in the event they materialise, that UNHCR is able to respond effectively.

To address the political risks noted above, UNHCR has and will continue to put in place measures to ensure that government capacity to apply international refugee standards are continuously strengthened. Initiatives are underway to train relevant authorities on the application of international refugee protection standards and to jointly develop Standard Operating Procedures with government, particularly around voluntary repatriation and reintegration processes. UNHCR is also working closely with other UN agencies and partners in the country to ensure that refugees are able to live in an environment where their needs are met. Such advocacy is undertaken at the country level as well as through high-level meetings such as the High-Level Meeting on Refugees and Migrants held by the UN Secretary-General’s level. Where appropriate, joint advocacy is also conducted with other UN agencies and with the government. UNHCR will continue to engage with relevant authorities in Tanzania to ensure that refugees are able to live in an environment where their needs are met.

Furthermore, it should be noted that if the GCF project will, upon implementation, contribute to the recognition that refugees can contribute to host communities, therefore ensuring greater local acceptance of refugees. Evidence on the benefits of the presence of refugees in host communities is already available and has been used by UNHCR in Tanzania. The project also addresses a key government priority, namely environmental degradation in refugee hosting areas. In doing so, the project will contribute to broader national discussions on refugee issues and management, along with the movement of a wider range on government missions.

At the regional and district level, there remains overwhelming support for the project and its activities as it engages directly with local communities. The project is expected to provide opportunities for refugees to engage in income-generating projects and benefit projects to host communities. Through these people-to-people dialogues and benefit sharing arrangements in the project areas, refugees will be able to contribute positively towards their hosting communities. The implementation of this project are expected to contribute to a more positive outlook on refugee hosting and peaceful coexistence, which in turn could lead to improved relations between refugees and host communities.

The project is also expected to have an impact on the political environment – as the project is believed to be able to highlight the shared benefits and advantages of a more inclusive approach.

# G. OCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Project categorisation

The project is being proposed as Category B or moderate risk according to the GCF project classification. This is because the project has potential limited adverse environmental and/or social risks and impacts that are few, generally site-specific, and can be mitigated through appropriate measures described in the Environmental and Social Management System in Annex 6. The main risks anticipated are summarised below in Table 15.

### Table 15. Summary of risks and mitigation

| Summary of risks | Mitigation factors or measures and management strategies |
| --- | --- |
| **Risk that project has potential impact on area considered important by local communities** | Restoration and conservation in forests within a 10-km radius to the Moyene Game Reserve will be conducted. This is expected to have positive benefits. No afforestation activities are planned for this area, but if any were to occur, they would be subject to consultation with local communities. |
| **Low risk** | Hydrological connectivity to the Malagasy–Moyene wetland is present, but the drainage of water from the project site into the Moyene Game Reserve will have a negative impact on the wetland. The construction of rainwater harvesting structures would likely have a negligible impact on the wetland. There are no other significant risks associated with the project that could be identified at this stage. Modeling and discussion are available in more detail in the Feasibility Study. |
| **Risk associated with the use of pesticides, herbicides, but which are non-invasive** | The project team will monitor any potential impacts of rainwater harvesting structures on water quality and quantity in the Moyene Game Reserve. This will be included as part of Output (1), which will work with local communities to identify the specific areas of land where interventions can be carried out. |

This process will be used to identify any area of land that was locally important and ensure it is not considered in the land use planning process to reflect the wishes of the local community.

### This project is fully participatory, involving multiple stakeholder groups, and interventions are planned with their involvement at all stages.

## Risk of security concerns/conflicts between host communities and refugees related to project resource delivery

### Moderate risk

Where possible, dialogues and coordinated implementation between refugees and host communities will be facilitated. These serve as important opportunities for interaction that can help fill the vacuum left after the end of the current refugee camps. Coexistence meetings were highlighted as an important peacemaking and conflict resolution mechanism in the region.

Management arrangements for forest reserves and afforested woodlots for fuel will consider the needs of both refugees and host communities, with a view to ensuring that the two groups benefit from these schemes coexisting with the refuge and host communities. Management mechanisms such as joint patrols and regular meetings between the two groups were highlighted as a means of reducing conflict over resources.

The majority of the interventions are located in host communities. This will help to reinforce the message that refugees have equally benefited from support in comparison to the host communities.

The project will be delivered under the guidance of the Regional government, District Councils and other relevant agencies. NGOs experienced in working in the region are managing any dispute.

## The conflict sensitivity assessment planned under output 1 will contribute to further assesses the risk of displacement, repression or limitations on access to resources

### Low to moderate

The land use planning process will engage separately a range of potentially underrepresented groups (e.g., women, pastoralist representatives) to ensure full participation. The project will engage NGOs experienced in delivering the planning processes and will be supported by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. This will also include pilot testing of GIS applications to ensure best practice in this process.

There are plans to set aside land for joint money farming forests for sustainable harvest of fuelwood that will be accessible to both groups. Consultations among both host communities and refugees on the use of the land, including issues around responsibility and benefit sharing, which will be addressed by project through Activity 2.2.

The feasibility study recognizes that agricultural restoration in riparian areas is an issue of concern for both refugees and host communities. The proposed project area does not seek to actively restore riparian areas although restoration may be an outcome of providing additional benefits in terms of agriculture, livelihoods and water supply.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 3.0 | PAGE 154

## Approach to environmental and social management during project implementation

The project is not introducing activities that are expected to result in increased risk for SEAR women, girls, and children exposed to violence and sexual exploitation, modern slavery, and harassment.

### Potential increase of incidence of Gender-Based Violence and Sexual Exploitation, Modern Slavery, and Harassment: Moderate risk

The project may potentially increase the risks of GBV, particularly intimate partner violence, in carrying out activities targeted to women’s livelihoods. The beneficiary selection process will be designed to ensure that women are not disproportionately excluded from the project.

### Risks will be managed through:

- **Comprehensive environmental and social safeguards** specified within the specific planning, design and implementation processes.
- **Ensuring that all biodiversity (forestry, livestock, agriculture and water) resources on or near the project are sufficiently experienced in safeguard relevant to their specialist technical areas.**
- **Establishing a monitoring framework for each of the activities under the project.**
- **Ensuring the monitoring and evaluation (M&E) process includes adequate review of environmental and social risks (and opportunities) at all stages, including during planning, implementation, and closure phases (see specific considerations).**

### Additional roles and responsibilities for environmental and social management across the project team:

- Assessing and addressing issues related to the interventions.
- Establishing a reporting procedure for environmental and social concerns related to the interventions.

### Increasing capacity of staff through required considerations in the training, capacity building and technical assistance delivered through the project.

The environmental and social management processes will draw upon the following documents:
- Green Climate Fund Environmental and Social Management System;
- UNEP safeguards systems;
- UNHCR safeguarding policy;
- summaries of stakeholder consultations;
- ESIA (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment);
- Policy and regulatory environment, and
- Country context.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (3.0) | PAGE 155

## Gender Assessment and Project Analysis

During project preparation, a gender assessment was conducted to understand the differential vulnerability to, and impact of climate change on women and men in Kigoma. From the findings of this assessment, and an analysis of the proposed project solution, a gender action plan (GAP) was developed. The process to produce these components involved extensive consultations with key stakeholders including government officials, civil society organizations at country level, as well as interviews and consultations with project and beneficiary stakeholders.

The gender assessment and project analysis were conducted in order to:

- Increase awareness of knowledge about financial services, and broad participation in income-generating activities
- Increase women’s access to land tenure, participation in decision-making processes, and access to credit
- Increase women’s capacity for self-reliance through increased access to education, training, and skills development
- Reduce exclusion from access to core water-level. This will result in productivity and income generated from improved livelihoods, including increased agricultural production, better health outcomes, and reduced firewood and water use on non-agricultural facilities.
- Gender-blind interventions that are not sensitive to the social and cultural norms of communities and their impact on women’s lives
- Women’s participation in income-generating activities and involvement in farmers’ groups, as well as being underrepresented in forest management activities.

Women refugees and host communities are also susceptible to protection issues like sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) when they travel outside of their settlements, particularly to collect firewood. A 2015 field report from the UNHCR indicated that 37% of all SGBV cases reported in Kigoma refugee camps occurred during the month period occurring during firewood collection trips, and 90% of these incidents were rape or sexual assault. In addition to this, women refugees are also at risk of being trafficked for forced marriage into host communities, whereas male refugees collecting firewood are perceived to be ‘leaving’ and may be violently attacked.

Women’s participation in income-generating activities is further hampered by the fact that women are often excluded from decision-making processes. This was highlighted during September 2015 consultations noting that SGBV during firewood collection remained an ongoing challenge. Other challenges include lack of access to land, credit and other financial services, as well as limited access for refugees, including family conflict – particularly regarding returning to country of origin – firewood collection beyond the 4km buffer zone and restrictions of freedom of movement and livelihood activities.

The gender action plan outlines several interventions aimed at ensuring that the project does not unintentionally contribute to or exacerbate existing protection issues. The gender action plan also seeks to promote gender equality by including activities which will increase women’s representation and awareness, as well as increasing their access to land tenure, decision-making processes, and financial services. This is done through disaggregated data collection, gender-sensitive design features and quantifiable performance indicators to promote women’s inclusion in all aspects of the project.

The actions of the GAP include:

- Promoting women’s inclusion in all project aspects, including training and employment opportunities;
- Promoting women’s participation in decision-making processes and workshops/events;
- Advancing gender diversity and challenging negative stereotyping in public awareness activities; and
- Ensuring that all project staff receive training consistent with the current learning methods and training accessibility of men and women.

The full Gender Assessment and project level Gender Action Plan are included as Annex 8.6.

## Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximate | page)

The financial rules for UNFCCC projects will be guided by applicable UN Financial regulations, rules and practices. The financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS) are also relevant to UNFCCC projects. In addition, the project’s funding allocation mechanisms are managed in accordance with applicable UN rules and procedures, including ensuring that all payments are made in accordance with the approved budget.

UNEP is audited annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for Green Climate Fund (GCF) projects.

In line with UNEP reporting procedures outlined in section E.7, reports to summarise the disbursement and projected demands for Programme funding will be prepared and submitted to a UNEP Programme Officer who will conduct

## Proposal name: Building climate resilience in the landscapes of the Rungwa Region, Tanzania

### Accredited entity: United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Project programme size: Small

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

a. The project will focus on the remaining host communities and refugee camps of Nolsta and Nyarugusu, and the former Mtendeli camp/1 in the Kigoma Region of the United Republic of Tanzania. These communities are nature-dependent living in an increasingly degraded landscape with limited land and water resources.

b. To address these challenges and to support sustainable development, this project intends to complement the already existing development and humanitarian efforts in the area by focusing on climate change adaptation.

c. The Kigoma Region covers about 37,000 km² in the north-west of the country with a population of about 2.3 million people. Situated on a plateau, the region hosts about 280,000 refugees and internally displaced persons (IDPs) from Burundi, Rwanda, and Democratic Republic of Congo, surrounded by areas of global biodiversity importance, with a predominance of Miombo forests and patches of montane forest in the north-east. The Kigoma Region is characterized by high rainfall in the north of the region, agropastoralist Sukuma people pass through in search of grazing land.

d. Historical climate trends show more intense and erratic precipitation events leading to an increase in flood risk. This has been observed as a shift from the floodplain flow regime (the period between the pluvial flood flows (from extreme precipitation events) and the floodplain flow regime (from rainfall)) towards the pluvial flood flow regime, which increases the flood risk.

e. While the former Mtendeli camp and the Njuta camp are exposed to both pluvial and fluvial flooding, the Nolsta and Nyarugusu camps are only exposed to fluvial flooding. The average rainfall in the Kigoma Region is expected to rise from about 1 mm/hour (Representative Climate Pathway 4.5) to about 1.5 mm/hour (Representative Climate Pathway 6).

f. Fluvial flood risk is expected to increase by substantially more, because climate change has altered, and will continue to alter, vegetation. The roughness index of land surfaces will decrease as a result of the decline in tree cover and the expansion of grasslands. This means that hydrological processes are highly non-linear. For example, an increase in rainfall of 10 per cent can result in a 50 per cent increase in runoff.

g. These changes in the Kigoma Region will have significant impacts on water resources. The climate change is a major factor in increased flood risk, leading to secondary impacts such as gully erosion. The changes in the rainfall profile of the Kigoma Region show more prolonged dry spells. These are expected to lead to reduced soil moisture and increased surface runoff, decreased evapotranspiration and evaporation of surface water resources. These drying trends are affecting agricultural productivity and food security for communities living in the Nolsta and Nyarugusu Region.

h. This climate context, compounded with the landscape degradation threats and impacts (described above), makes the region highly vulnerable to climate change.

i. The project consists of two components with four outputs that will be implemented by two executing entities (EE): the Tanzania Vice-President Office (VPO) as lead EE and the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as co-lead EE. The project includes environmental and social safeguards (ESS) categorization provided by the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the World Bank Group (WBG) as part of its Environmental and Social Framework.

j. Component 1: Increased resilience of ecosystems and people to climate change threats:
Output 1: Participatory Climate-resilient Land Use Plans developed (C-LUP) to support ecosystem-based adaptation;

At the time of the Secretariat review, the Nolsta and Nyarugusu camps are in existence, and the Mtendeli camp was closed by the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR). The voluntary repatriation were relocated to Nolsta camp after the Mtendeli camp has been decommissioned.


---
**Page 169**
---

(b) Output 2: Degraded ecosystems restored to support climate change adaptation at host and refugee communities.

Output 3: Climate-resilient livelihoods practiced to increase the capacity of host communities and refugees to better adapt in changing climatic conditions.

Component 2: Strengthened policies for climate-responsive planning and development:

Output 4: Institutional arrangements are strengthened to ensure that climate-related mainstreaming into policies, plans and strategies in the United Republic of Tanzania and in humanitarian contexts.

All activities will be executed, overseen and managed by the Division of Environment of VPO. UNHCR will execute activity 2.3, and subactivities 3.2.1 and 3.2.2. Most activities in outputs 2 and 4 will be implemented directly by the district council and relevant national/local partners in coordination with VPO.

The project is seeking only grant funding from the GCF, with a mix of co-financing in the form of contributions from UNHCR, and other donors, at a ratio of 1:0.24. Considering the least developed country (LDC) status of the country and the extreme political, social, economic and climate vulnerability of the target beneficiaries, this can be considered satisfactory.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Increased resilience of agropastoral people to climate change threats (total cost: USD 20.85M)

The starting point for all project interventions will be the development of C-LUPs to support climate-resilient and ecosystem-based adaptation measures. This is proposed as a fundamental step in ensuring that the planned activities are relevant, appropriate, and can be planned and delivered. Key steps will include the identification of the specific adaptation measures required by each agropastoral community. The following three project activities (2.3 [food and erosion control] and 3.2 [rainwater harvesting and irrigation]) are clear illustrations of this. As each C-LUP identifies village forest areas, it is also an entry point for the preparation of land-use plans that will support ecosystem-based adaptation interventions (output 2.1).

#### Component 2: Community climate-resilient land use plans developed to support ecosystem-based adaptation (total cost: USD 160.85M; USD 1,602.85M, or 100 per cent)

Land-use planning activities will support overcoming challenges related to the relationship between agropastoral communities and their environment, host communities, and refugees. While the host communities in the project areas are composed of former refugees who have been living there for many years, they do not include displaced people who are generally not part of community planning and awareness-raising processes. This is due to the lack of land-use plans that would support the development of such plans, as well as the lack of land-use planning and management of customary land can lead to conflict between farmers and pastoralists. The project will address this by engaging with communities in the encroachment onto village and national forest reserves outside camp villages. The project will engage with local people through community groups and other stakeholders in projects, and its activities relate to environmental conservation and strengthening environmental management committees with overall processes reflected in the stakeholder engagement plan. Overall land-use planning will be supported by C-LUPs that identify appropriate land use with the participation of users and community members.

Another important group are the refugees, who have unique needs. Refugees living in the camp areas are not allowed to move beyond their designated area (or restricted movement beyond the camp). Although they practice agricultural activities to some


---
**Page 170**
---

**Green Climate Fund**

GCF.07/17/24/MAR/P

Page 159

entire, they are also often reliant on food aid. However, many refugees are now in protracted situations where the temporary camps are becoming increasingly more permanent places of settlement. Therefore, incorporating climate-resilient approaches and measures (i.e. measures to prevent land degradation and enhance ecosystem services) that allow access to alternative livelihoods) in camp management is key for sustainable camp management.

15. Under output 1, the planning processes for host villages and camps will be separate, because they are different types of settlements with different objectives and associated landscape-level benefits (e.g. stream protection along streams in camp and host community land). The planning process for host villages will focus on identifying areas that can be used to allocate grazing areas, as well as land for farmlands, crops, woodlots, natural forests, settlements and different land uses within the village boundaries. The planning processes serve as the mechanism through which the boundaries for land use, identify land tenure, achieve consensus, and negotiate across different and potentially competing interests. The stakeholder consultation process in host villages has been a key component of the planning process. Lessons learned under past initiatives suggest that increased clarity with land-use planning can (i) successfully contribute to reducing conflict over resources; (ii) increase community participation and enhance community capacity to share resources and resolve differences.

16. The long-term legal outcome of the village land-use planning process is the issuance of a certificate of customary rights to use land (CCRUL). This certificate confers rights according to a land occupier and user following tribal customs and traditions. The project will support this relatively new legal framework in order to provide security for land users, and lead to sustainability of project outputs and outcomes. The certificate will provide long-term security for the village land-use planning process and allow for borrowing money to conduct other future village-related climate-resilient investments.

17. A conflict sensitivity assessment has been introduced under output 1 and will be conducted in all villages where land use planning is being implemented (see annex 6 of the funding proposal). The assessment will cover: (i) potential conflicts related to land-use planning; (ii) potential conflicts related to land-use planning in host communities; (iii) tensions between indirect beneficiaries outside the project target area and direct beneficiaries within the project target area. These risks have been assessed by UNHCR during project formulation (see annex 6 of the funding proposal).

18. Output 2: Degraded ecosystems restored to support climate change adaptation in host and refugee communities (total cost USD 13,039,743 GCF USD 9,579,577, or 77 per cent). Activities under output 2 are expected to address the negative impacts that refugee communities place on the ecosystems and their services through CEMP. In order to reduce climate change-related impacts such as ecological range shifts and alteration of the phenological cycles, both forest protection and patrolling; (ii) allocating areas for CRFMP; (iii) establishing dedicated successful agroforestry systems in host villages and refugee camps; and (iv) providing training and services. Based on the feasibility assessment and economic analysis, the impacts of project activities will be monitored through a monitoring framework.

19. Activity 2.2 (forest monitoring) is expected to complement the CEMP interventions. Afforestation and agroforestry are expected to take place in host communities. The afforestation areas will be located on degraded land that has been identified by community members and will be mobilized by village leaders. Agroforestry systems are expected to mimic forest-like conditions, with trees planted at a density of 100-250 per hectare (the same as that which was initially lost when forests were converted to agricultural lands). The afforestation and agroforestry interventions are also expected to reduce the pressure on the native Mambu forests for firewood. This will help to protect these forests from degradation, thereby reducing climate change-related impacts such as ecological range shifts and alteration of the phenological cycles. Both


---
**Page 171**
---

### Activity 2.3 (Flood reduction infrastructure) is integral to protecting the people living and investing in the project area from flood-related risks and damage. The proposed flood risk reduction measures encompass: (i) micro-scale, stone-pitched drainage channels; and (ii) sustainable drainage systems. Flood and erosion control measures will also be implemented as part of the canal, water harvesting, and soil conservation components of the environmental restoration plan, in combination of other restoration interventions implemented by UNHCR. The post-consolidation restoration phase is expected to focus on flood risk reduction in terms of: (i) reducing flood and erosion risk for downstream host communities; and (ii) enabling direct engagement with local authorities and community-based organizations to improve host communities' access to basic services.

The beneficiaries of this activity are primarily the refugees of the former Bwendo camp, and the surrounding host communities, given the local impact of this intervention. This complements the large-scale agroforestry and village land afforestation interventions (activity 2.7) that will be implemented in the project area. The flood risk reduction infrastructure to be involved will be planning and constructing the flood reduction infrastructure. Building on previous experience with similar projects, the flood risk reduction infrastructure is expected to be enhanced by 16 stone-filled gabions. Further fine-tuning is essential, and this is foreseen during the implementation of the project using local expertise and knowledge. The proposed sustainable drainage systems (flood reduction infrastructure using natural soil and/or natural systems that: (i) tend to preserve existing open spaces; (ii) protect natural systems (including groundwater recharge); and (iii) are in harmony with the environment) will be integrated into an existing land-use plan and maintenance to manage stormwater flows.

Fostering ownership will be key for ensuring continued maintenance of the proposed interventions. An overall management and accountability mechanism (OAM) costs basically relate to camp-based small-scale flood mitigation infrastructure that will be turned over to the Ministry of Water, Energy, and Natural Resources and/or other relevant local or national authorities. The water and sanitation humanitarian cluster development agencies will support the Ministry of Water, Energy, and Natural Resources in the planning, implementation, and weeding (for instance, through community labour). Material for replacement is expected to be provided by the beneficiaries. Moreover, an OAM plan has been developed and incorporated into the project design.

Output 3: Climate-resilient livelihoods practiced to increase the capacity of host communities to adapt to climate change and reduce their vulnerability. Total cost: USD 7,994,532; GCF USD 6,827,324, or 80 per cent) (Climate-resilient agricultural practices under the umbrella of the Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) approach: (i) improved soil management; (ii) integrated soil management; (iii) integrated soil fertility management; (iv) intercropping; and (v) water harvesting and storage techniques. These practices are to be implemented as appropriate, according to the participants' needs and preferences, in collaboration with the beneficiaries. These activities will be implemented in alignment with the climate-smart agriculture principles and best practices for agroforestry and village land afforestation cultural practices. This also includes the use of traditional knowledge through training sessions co-designed by the project team and local communities. The project will also support an appointed capacity-building of the savings association management offices (SAMOs) to manage savings

### Impact potential

The project aims to reach 19 per cent of the total national population and approximately half of the population in the Kigoma Region. The project's direct beneficiaries are estimated at 370,141 people who will benefit from the adoption of diversified, climate-resilient livelihoods, including improved agricultural practices, agroforestry systems, and climate-smart agriculture practices as well as on climate-smart agriculture practices and CBFM in the Kigoma landscape. These interventions are expected to increase food security and improve access to markets for climate resilience for their effective implementation. An additional 711,516 people in the


---
**Page 173**
---

region are also expected to benefit from enhanced knowledge and learning in climate resilient activities as well as from the replication potential of local financial structures such as the SAs. The total expected beneficiaries amount to 1,281,857 people. Specific criteria for activities are to be used by the project to ensure that vulnerable populations (e.g., women, youth) are included in the CIFRM system (as well as for the afforestation and agroforestry under activity 2.1) will be targeted. The project will also consider other output 1 following important environmental- and social-related indicators. For activity 3.3 (following forest management and beekeeping activities), vulnerable populations from the host communities will be targeted (e.g., female households). The proposed adaptation measures are in line with the CIF investment criteria indicators.

The climate change vulnerability study has been shown that the Kigoma Region is experiencing rising temperatures as well as increased rainfall intensity and variability, which will contribute to an increase in the frequency and severity of floods and particularly late season flooding. These changes have already led to a shift in rainfall patterns have produced climate impacts that have compounded environmental degradation, as well as reduced agricultural productivity. The combination of these factors has resulted in limited resources and deforestation, surface- and groundwater availability, soil erosion and fertility, and the loss of biodiversity. These are all contributing to increased vulnerability non-climatic development of degradaion. These changes are threatening local rain-fed and ecosystem-based livelihoods and food security for vulnerable populations.

The proposal includes climate information based on the observational data that was used to validate the CORDEX regional climate models used for assessing projected climate changes. The validation results show a good agreement between the modelled and observed precipitation compared with the station data.

The analysis described in annex 2 notes that the current observational trends are projected to increase into the future. The most notable trends are the lowering of early season rainfall, and the freezing of rainfall into the mid and, particularly, late seasons. There is also a greater frequency of extreme weather events such as heavy downpours and flooding events. The meteorological drought index (the Standard Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index) shows that there has been an increase in the number of days with severe drought events. The average maximum and daily extreme temperatures are increasing in all seasons, particularly during the dry season.

The severe gully erosion problem in former Mendeli camp has significantly increased flood risk in the region. A recent analysis shows that flooding in Kizura, Kasakwa and Kandoko Districts occurs primarily a result of precipitation events. The communities and refugee camps reported the occurrence of flooding events and subsequent riverbank and other large-scale landslides. Current rainfall data (1980-2015) precipitation future show an average of 4% additional inundation area. Under the no adaptation scenario, this would result in 6.49 km² or 380 ha additional flooding (Annex 2, section 2.2.2).

The expected adaptation benefits for host population surrounding Mendeli include:

* Flood risk reduction in villages located downstream of the degraded Mendeli camp area;

(b) Protection of downstream agricultural lands and soils from further flooding, erosion and pollution, thereby improving food security;
(c) Reduced siltation in surface water resources in the catchment thus improving water quality;
(d) Protection of roads and services infrastructure in the decommissioned camp area and in downstream villages; and
(e) Opportunities for expansion of climate resilient productive activities in the restored forestlands.

35. Through this project, it is expected that the landscape and ecosystems of the Kigoma Region can continue to support their residents, including host communities, refugees and agriculturalists, by reducing the vulnerability of these populations to climate change impacts and environmental degradation. Overall, USD 14,191,000, which accounts for 83 per cent of all funding required, will be targeted interventions in the host communities within a 10-15 km radius surrounding the camps.

Project benefits for the host communities and the agricultural producers include: (i) improved water availability; (ii) increased food security through an increase in productivity of ecosystem services and land productivity; (iii) increased water availability for irrigation during dry periods, which will lead to increased yields from crops and livestock; and (iv) increased income and savings through climate-resilient agriculture.

Project benefits for the population within the refugee camps include: (i) increased food production of crops, settlements and assets; (ii) improved health and safety from flood prevention and control measures; (iii) increased water availability for irrigation during dry periods, which will lead to increased yields from crops and livestock; and (iv) increased income and savings through climate-resilient agriculture.

The project aims to enhance the protection of 42,000 hectares of natural ecosystems, including 30,000 hectares of forests (dominated by Montane forests) under CRFM. A further 2,000 hectares under other forest types are also included in the project area (see Annex 1 for details).

The project activities (Activities 2.3 and 3.2) also expect to have indirect positive impacts in terms of improved ecosystem health through enhanced soil fertility management and land use management and rainwater harvesting interventions. These benefits relate to improved ecosystem health for the provision of ecosystem services, including: (i) enhanced infiltration; (ii) increased water retention capacity; (iii) reduced runoff; (iv) reduced sedimentation off- and (v) reduced soil erosion and siltation. Valuation of these benefits and quantification of benefit streams are not included in this project framework.

The sustainable management and restoration of these ecosystems is deemed critical for enhancing the conservation status of these ecosystems, which are currently under fragmentation stress. As clearly shown in the baseline and supporting studies, the forests in the Kigoma Region are critical to the resilience of these ecosystems as they are critical for provision of non-timber forest products and ecosystem services for local populations.

The project will generate mitigation co-benefits because of the emission reductions generated through avoided deforestation, improved agricultural practices, afforestation and agroforestry. The estimated emission reduction is 120,783 tCO2e under the Emission Reductions Test (EX-ACT). Sequestration of 672,973 t CO2e and emission reductions of 3.2 million t CO2 eq are expected throughout the project’s lifetime. These are not included in the logical framework (Uniform Framework) as they are considered to be outside the scope of the project and project implementation. Given the complex setting for project implementation [i.e. refugee camps], prioritization of activities is required, with a focus on those that can be implemented when both sequestration and emission avoidance for reporting purposes may detract resources and focus from the core project activities, namely, delivering adaptation interventions to generate

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project presents a robust climate rationale for adaptation. The climate baseline information includes well-referenced scientific information and sources. It also demonstrates how the project area is vulnerable to climate change impacts, including droughts and floods, and how the climate hazards will affect the project's geographical area (the Kigoma Region, and three refugee camps) in terms of value of physical assets and livelihoods, and their mapped impacts to specific sectors such as agriculture. The project has considered the spatial and temporal scales have been used for planning the project activities; namely, observed historical climate data and projected future climate scenarios were used to develop a baseline with a timescale that matches the project's lifespan. The project also distinguishes between the drivers directly related to climate change (e.g., changes in precipitation) and indirect ones (e.g., land-use change).

In relation to the interventions, the paradigm shift is demonstrated through different project activities. The project intends to support the development of resilient livelihoods, especially in community benefit-sharing. Fostering local integration, the project is expected to augment the recognition of the value of natural resources and their conservation in the Kigoma Region and the Kigoma Region. Mindful of post-project sustainability, the project intends to incentivize shifts in the revenue streams from existing livelihoods towards more resilient livelihoods. The sustainability of the project's activities is supported by high financial profitability. It is expected that farmers will use the Savings Associations support to purchase seedlings from the existing tree nurseries and plant them on their farms. This will increase the productivity of their farms.

Furthermore, to promote livelihood activities that preserve forestry resources the project will support the development of a market for non-timber forest products (NTFPs) in the Kigoma Region with access to the existing high-value chains in the Kigoma Region. Such strong financial viability contributes to the sustainability of the project's activities and is expected to be supported by private sector sources of finance. Although the exit strategy is backed by the economic and financial feasibility, it is also recognized that a market-based approach alone may not be sufficient; therefore, other mechanisms can be developed to further incentivize communities and refugees to continue the resilient livelihoods activities after the project ends.

Agricultural value chains (AVCs) are another intervention of the use of SAs. These have the potential to improve access to sustainable funding mechanisms and to use financing in a more efficient manner by reducing transaction costs, increasing returns on investment, and user practices after the project in the host communities. The SAs operate the accounts or hold the money that is not lent or granted to members through physical cash boxes, mobile money, and, eventually, digital platforms. The SAs can also be used as a platform for engaging financial partners (e.g., Good Neighbors, Care International, or the United Nations Capital Development Fund). It is expected that the project will build on the experience of the existing microfinance institutions in the Kigoma Region to develop and implement the design and monitoring of SAs. Strengthening SAs through the project will build on the experience of the Union Microfinance Institution (UMI) in the Kigoma Region, which has been operating since 2013. The lack of penetration of private financial institutions in the project area can be addressed by working with existing microfinance institutions and other financial partners to develop a model that will ensure that communities have improved access to farm inputs, collectively negotiate prices improving marketing of products and have access to microfinance for the continuous implementation of their livelihood activities.

It is recommended that the potential role of SAs and SAMOs for forestry-related activities be explored because these are already locally legitimized models that allow for sectoral replication.

### Sustainable development potential

The project will create several economic, environmental, social and gender benefits. In terms of environmental benefits, the project will promote the conservation, restoration and sustainable management of forests and other natural resources in the Kigoma region. The project area is located within the Kigoma Forests Landscape (KFL), which covers 10,524 ha of forested areas. The project aims to restore degraded forest ecosystems through the implementation of agroforestry systems on around 60,000 ha of agricultural/crop land in the project area at a density of one tree per hectare. This will result in an increase in carbon sequestration and benefits related to the enhancement and maintenance of key hydrological services.

The project aims to reach 35 per cent of the agricultural/crop land in the project area at a density of one tree per hectare, which is expected to cover around 20,000 ha of agricultural/crop land. The selection of species to be planted will be made in discussion with farmers, in consideration of farmers' preference for certain non-native species.


---
**Page 177**
---

## The preservation of ecosystem services has wide benefits over the multiple dimensions of sustainability and resilience. The project will deliver positive impacts on food security and management practices, increased access to inputs and enhanced water use and soil management, improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers, and reduced vulnerability to climate shocks and stresses. By improving agricultural production, increasing market access, and diversifying income sources through livestock farming and agriculture, access to food will be improved by enabling these beneficiaries to purchase more diverse diets in the Musanze District. Through the development of value-adding activities, both host communities and refugees will have greater control over their local diet diversity. Project outputs such as strengthening farmer field schools, establishing market linkages, providing agricultural inputs and training, improving access to credit, promoting livestock farming, and secondary transformation, as well as access to microfinance, will contribute to increased food security.

### Social benefits for host communities include improved tenure arrangements and clear land-use demarcation. For refugees, they include improved health and safety through flood protection measures, reduced vulnerability to climate shocks and stresses, and better disaster events. Overall social benefits relate to improved social cohesion among host communities, refugees, and appropriate approaches.
3.4 Needs of the recipient

The project targets people living with high vulnerability to climate change, as evidenced in the findings from the feasibility study. The project will be implemented in the Rwigoma Region, which is one of the poorest regions in the United Republic of Tanzania, with more than 32 per cent of the population living below the poverty line and an unemployment rate of 40 per cent of the region's population unemployed. Moreover, 90 per cent of the region's population engaged in agriculture, and most smallholder farmers are dependent on rain-fed agriculture, which is highly vulnerable to climate change. The project will address water scarcity, soil erosion, water availability for irrigation, occurrence of pests and diseases, and crops being pushed beyond their climatic zones.

The protracted refugee situation began more than 20 years ago and is unlikely to change. Identified recipient needs include the need to address refugees' unique vulnerabilities, as they are often displaced from their homes and communities due to conflict or climate change interventions. Therefore, the project intends to develop a model for meeting the climate-related needs of refugees in the Rwigoma Region while also addressing the same landscape. It is worth noting that repatriation is encouraged but voluntary and remains regulated by the UNHCR. The project will be implemented with the support of the Government of Burundi and UNICEF. It is understood that the choice of Musanze camp was motivated by resource constraints, as well as the need to address the increasing burden considering the recent reduction in refugee population in both Mwandi and Ntuba camps.

There are several factors limiting the capacity of the United Republic of Tanzania to adapt to climate change. These include limited institutional capacity for adaptation, the need to address environmental degradation in rural areas such as the Rwigoma Region, and the need for financial resources. The project will contribute to addressing these challenges by improving access to financial services by strengthening SDA to deliver climate-resilient interventions and by scaling up financial inclusion. The project will address the overarching institutional capacity deficit in climate resilient land use planning through capacity building and transfer of best practices

3.5 Country ownership

The project is firmly anchored in government structures and ownership. The Government of the United Republic of Tanzania has actively participated in the project design, ensuring alignment with its relevant policies and plans. Exchanges and discussions between the Secretary for Environment and Natural Resources and the Project Director have been held.

The project will be aligned with the following policies and plans: the intended nationally determined contribution of the United Republic of Tanzania; the National Development Plan (2016–2021); the National Climate Change Policy (2013), the National Climate Change Strategy (2012), and the Guidelines for Integrating Climate Change Adaptation into National Sector Policies, Plans, and Programs. The project will also contribute to the intended nationally determined contribution – aimed at reducing climate change vulnerability in the country by enhancing resilience against droughts and floods.

Although the project does not include separate co-financing from the Government, the national designated authority (VPO) has expressed its continued support for and prioritization of the project. The VPO will continue to provide technical advice on environmental aspects as outlined in previous sections. Both VPO and UNHCR will work through local government agencies and institutions to ensure that the project is implemented effectively.

The Ministry of Lands, Housing and Urban Development (MoLHUD) will play a key role in overseeing the implementation of activities in the refugee camps. The Government of the United Republic of Tanzania will be represented at ministerial and district levels by the VPO and UNHCR, respectively, to ensure that the project is implemented effectively.

Furthermore, the National Environmental Management Council (NEMC) will ensure compliance with relevant environmental laws and regulations through providing technical advisory and compliance guidance on all environmental aspects of the project.

Recently, the United Republic of Tanzania has developed its own Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) strategy. The implementation of this strategy requires additional interventions. These areas are well aligned with the interventions proposed by the project. In turn, the guidelines will support the country's commitment to create a resilient, climate-smart agriculture sector by 2030.

The policies of the United Republic of Tanzania towards refugees have been more restrictive in recent years than those of other countries in East Africa. Despite this, over the last two decades, accepted a comparatively high number of refugees compared with the rest of the region and has provided them with access to education and health services. In line with the terms of the formal xenagia, the Kigoma Region will probably continue to host refugees in the long term. Given its international legal obligations, the Government is committed to protecting refugees and asylum seekers.

Risk mitigation measures are in place, particularly to ensure the Government's capacity to continue providing humanitarian assistance. The National Climate Change Action Response Plan (2019–2020) acknowledges the need for wider efforts within the environment and energy sectors to address climate change adaptation needs through integrated interventions. For this materialization, it is crucial to collect evidence on project benefits in terms of climate change adaptation and sustainable landscapes in the region.

The Government will continue to provide financial support to the project thanks to the enhanced capacity of local stakeholders built by the project as well as provisions to the District budgets

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The proposed level of concessionality (based on a GCF grant plus a minimum level of line-of-credit (LoC) from NDB(NBRC)) is based on three underlying characteristics of the proposed investment: (i) high poverty level of the recipient country; (ii) the targeted group, which is extremely vulnerable to climate change and its impacts; and (iii) the scale of the project. For this project, it is not appropriate to make use of reimbursable grants as a financial instrument. A limited amount of the GCF funding required is to be spent on equipment with the potential to directly and immediately reduce poverty and vulnerability in the recipient country. This will supplement the incomes of very poor vulnerable populations, particularly in districts that host and share the benefits of the project activities. The remaining funds are to be used to scale back to the operation of SAs in the host communities as well as to support maintenance costs of existing infrastructure and equipment. These investments are expected to reduce poverty impacts, such as crop losses and damage to property, saving offers an important safety net for very poor households. The level of concessionality is properly justified in the economic and financial analysis.

The project is primarily considered as one to create public goods. It is to be co-financed by private sector entities, which will provide a return on investment that is expected to be generated. It is to be noted that the GCF Co-financing Policy does not set any requirement from the perspective of the economic viability of the project. The proposed project will generate substantial positive externalities, including carbon dioxide sequestration and reduction in carbon dioxide emissions. However, these are not included in the economic analysis.

The economic analysis shows that the proposed project is expected to be high enough to justify the investment from both a financial and an economic perspective. The project is expected to generate a net present value (NPV) of USD 536,348 million and an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 17% at a discount rate of 20%. The EIRR will be increased by 9 percentage points when all the benefits are included (using the higher discount rate of 6.2 per cent in both financial and economic analysis). This is expected to result from the scale of activities and benefits. The economic analysis shows that the proposed agroforestry activities will be economically viable over the 20-year project lifetime, with a high IRR of 50 per cent.

The proposed project is expected to generate an NPV of USD 7,199,067, with an EIRR ranging from 16 to 78 per cent. The high returns are due to the scale and benefits of the activities as well as scalability potential. A sensitivity analysis has shown that the results of the financial analysis are robust.

While the project will be approximately 20 per cent co-financed, parallel financing is expected to be about double the project size. Letters of support from the Royal Danish Embassy in the GCF Secretariat and the Government of Denmark have been provided for this project. This support demonstrates the overall coherence and complementarity of the project with other investments.

In terms of cost-effectiveness, benchmarks are provided for water pumped using the proposed technology (treadle pumps), which are less expensive than solar-powered pumps (the alternative technology). The treadle pump is expected to be 20 per cent cheaper than every solar-powered pump. A comparison benchmark is not reported for other activities. This is due to the fact that the project will not be implemented in parallel with other investment activities.

The project exhibits a cost per beneficiary of USD 33, which is at the low end of projects in the GCF portfolio.

Environmental and social risk category:** The project primarily aims to improve the resilience of communities in Tanzania against climate change, particularly coastal communities against the threats of climate change through an integrated landscape ecosystem approach. The project will have two components: (output 1) increasing the capacity of local communities to cope with climate change by implementing CCFPs for villages to support economic and adaptive coping; (output 2) restoring ecosystems to support climate change adaptation by implementing CBFPs/afforestation and reforestation activities in degraded areas, particularly in densely populated areas; and (output 3) provision of and diversification of livelihoods for refugees and host communities through the development of sustainable land use management systems, infrastructure support such as water harvesting and micro-irrigation systems, and capacity building to improve agricultural productivity. In addition to mitigation benefits through GCF, substantial other potential environmental and social risks exist in relation to the project’s implementation and development. In addition to mitigation benefits through GCF, substantial other potential environmental and social risks exist in relation to the project’s implementation and development.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

The GCF standard on labour and working conditions (Annexure 1) will be applied to Section 2 such as:

The project will involve use of water resources for irrigation of land and nurseries. ### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS: 7 Indigenous Peoples.
The EMS provides an analysis of different groups that might be considered under the scope of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. The policy applies to all projects funded by the GCF, including those based in the Kigoma Region where project activities are to be undertaken, thus a separate Indigenous People’s Advisory Group (IPAG) has been established for this project to address relevant social issues. The EMS further highlights the presence of agropastoralists, mainly from a majority ethnic group, who have traditionally used communal grazing areas and land in conjunction with agropastoralists and other land-users in the land-use planning process, and in activities relating to environmental education and strengthening environmental management committees and overall community participation. The project team has developed an integrated land use planning process providing opportunity to allocate grazing areas and, by engaging in this process, will ensure that these communities are included in the decision-making processes. This mechanism allows for adjustments to boundaries for land uses, identify management regimes, achieve conservation of natural resources, and promote sustainable livelihoods. The EMS highlights that the different community stakeholders are identified, engaged and consulted throughout the process, including through the establishment of a Community Liaison Committee. In addition, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE.


### ESS: Cultural heritage.
The EMS provides for strict avoidance of activities that affect cultural heritage sites. The project will avoid areas where there are known cultural heritage sites and control infrastructure and small-scale irrigation infrastructure will be constructed by the project. This will ensure that no adverse impacts on cultural heritage sites occur. There is a section on Cultural Heritage and Chance Finds Procedures has been incorporated in the EMS to guide the project should it affect cultural heritage sites.


### SEAH: Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment.
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIM-2017/18 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH). The project team has developed a comprehensive SEAH safeguarding in its submission for this funding proposal. The EMS of the project identifies a number of SEAH issues including sexual and GBV that may occur in the project and put in measures to prevent them. These include the development of an inter-agency standard operating procedures and protocols will be applied in managing the potential risk of SEAH and to guide SEAH complaints handling and grievance mechanisms. In addition, the project team has developed a Gender Action Plan (GAP) as part of the Ministry of Community Development, Gender and Women as per the National Plan of Action to end

# Implementation arrangements

In addition to the project steering committees which provide overall guidance and oversight of the project, the AE will act as the secretariat at the operational level and will be staffed by technical experts including on safeguards. A Safeguard Specialist will be hired for this role. The AE will also hire a Gender Advisor who will be engaged and will be responsible for identifying emerging risks, developing adaptive management strategies to address these risks, and ensuring that gender is integrated into social and environment safeguard plans. The management plans will also be built into the contracts and agreements with contractors and implementing partners of the project.

### Stakeholder engagement plan

The AE has submitted a stakeholder engagement plan (annex 7 of the funding proposal) "Consultations Summary and Stakeholder Engagement Plan" which outlines the process for conducting consultations conducted on the project and provides a stakeholder mapping analysis as well as a detailed plan for continued engagement with stakeholders during the project implementation. For public consultation purposes, the AE will also provide information in accordance with the confidentiality disclosure of all project level and activity-level safeguard instruments and other relevant information to the public through the GCF website. The AE will also be guided by the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy; the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage.

### ESMS

The ESMS provides for the establishment of a grievance redress mechanism that will build on an existing UNHCR feedback and complaints mechanism. The AE has also its Stakeholder Engagement Plan which outlines how it will receive, record, investigate or report concerns, complaints or grievance issues about its project. The ESMS also relates to the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy; the GCF Indigenous Peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage including before a claim has been made.

## Gender policy

### AE's gender assessment and gender action plan

The AE has provided a gender assessment and a gender action plan and, therefore, complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF. The Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Development is responsible for creating an enabling environment conducive to promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment in the country. The Ministry has been working on several initiatives aimed at improving the implementation of policies and legislative acts have been passed such as the Land Act and the Village Land Act, as well as two key poverty reduction strategy documents, MURITAT II and III. Such development plans and strategies are aligned with the GCF Gender Policy and the AE’s gender action plan.

In addition, the National Plan of Action to end violence against Women and Children in Tanzania aims to combat all forms of violence against women and children, including sexual violence against women in the country and the refugee camps.

### Gender assessment

The gender assessment was based on a desk review and stakeholder consultation among the refugees and host community surrounding the refugee camps. The assessment covered the differentiated issues for women in the refugee camps and host communities as well as the differences between the two groups of women within the refugee camps as well as the differences in the host community between women

* Feedback and stakeholder responses can be sent through the following website https://www.unep.org/about-un-environment/why-does-un-environment-fund-its-environmental-projects-concepts.


---
**Page 185**
---

and men alike. While in both cases women are disproportionately affected, refugee women often have more challenges in coping with the climate change-induced challenges to their nearby environment. Similarly, despite a supportive policy environment, Tanzanian women continue to face gender-based violence and discrimination. The patriarchal norms tend to favour men and perpetuate views that men are the sole owners of the land, and have and control property rights. This often results in increased levels of conflict within the home if women attempt to control land. Such views and practices leave women with little or no incentive to invest and improve the land, while on the other hand women do not possess the resources required for such investments. Women are also responsible for household chores. Further in the refugee camps, economic activities, such as selling food aid rations, non-food items, tailoring clothes, repairing shoes and tents, and providing childcare services, cooking, cleaning, repairs, are mostly done by women while men are engaged mostly in carpentry or masonry in addition to other forms of manual labour.

ii. There are GBV risks within and outside of the refugee camps. Refugees in the camps are exposed to violence, as well as when they leave the camps to search for work, to trade, or to gather firewood. Women and girls face a high risk of sexual exploitation, harassment, and abuse, and early or forced marriage. Refugees are restricted and endure constricted living conditions within the camps, which increases their vulnerability to GBV. The lack of privacy also increases this risk. This forces some to engage in risky coping mechanisms or behaviours (transactional sex, illegally traveling outside the camp, etc.). Women and girls are responsible for collecting firewood, household chores, maintain food and energy security, and obtain necessary resources. For both the host and refugee communities, firewood collection is the responsibility of women and, for refugees, it is a daily task that can take up to 10 hours per day. Due to the sheer lack of fulfilling their needs as a result of depletion, women are forced to travel further beyond this boundary.

iii. The assessment indicates that gender issues in forestry, agriculture and land use are critical challenges and need to be addressed. Regarding land, issues of insecure land tenure and exclusionary practices are common. These practices are often based on patriarchal norms and discriminatory social and cultural norms. Further, challenges to women exist in relation to access to productive resources such as land, water, tools, seeds, and credit facilities. Women also have fewer skills and less access to information and knowledge, which limits their ability to engage in income-generating activities that are critical for improving livelihoods and productivity. Women bear the burden of unpaid care and domestic work, which puts greater demands on women’s time and energy. This reduces women's opportunities for increased productivity and income, rendering them economically weaker and dependent. This in turn forces some women to engage in behaviours and practices that they do not want to engage in. The assessment also indicates that there is a lack of gender-sensitive policies and programs that put in place mechanisms to address causes of gender inequalities within the refugee and host communities.

iv. The AIF has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender action plan includes activities addressing the challenges to women in forestry, agriculture and land use. These include setting clear objectives, targets (for host and refugees), timelines and budgets. Activities will ensure women engage in decision-making processes at all levels of project implementation, including training on new skills while creating opportunities for access to assets transferred to refugee and host community members. Women will be provided with training in sustainable agricultural practices, agriculture training, and inputs and irrigation investments; improved environmental management and conservation activities; and gender-sensitive livelihoods. Gender-related interventions will support women to better manage natural resources, and reduce their work burden and the time needed for collecting firewood; while providing creche facilities and morning care services for children of working mothers. Women’s participation is critical to the project. Moreover, for refugee women, exposure to the threat of GBV and other forms of

### 3. Risks

#### 3.1 Overall proposal assessment (high risk)

The project aims to address the need for climate change adaptation for both host communities and refugees in the Ngorongoro Region of the United Republic of Tanzania. The project targets four outputs: (i) participatory, climate-resilient land-use planning in refugee camps and surrounding areas; (ii) improved livelihoods through diversification of income sources and livestock diversification; and (iii) information on climate change adaptation disseminated and mainstreamed into policies, plans and strategies at the ministry and humanitarian programming levels. The project is expected to generate a total of USD 190.1 million in grants that GCF provides USD 190.1 million in grants. Co-financing will be provided by UNHCR (the EE) in the form of technical assistance, capacity building, and other support for the implementation of risks related to government policies towards refugees. The AE has identified a high probability of such risks due to Tanzania's climate impact and will therefore need active adaptive management from an early stage.

#### 3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (high risk)

The EE (UNEP) has an extensive track record of implementing climate change projects through, for example, the Least Developed Countries Fund, the Special Climate Change Fund, the Adaptation Fund, and bilateral funds such as those of the European Union. UNHCR has a regional presence in Tanzania with a country office in Dar es Salaam. The project will be managed by a programme manager, who will take the lead role in monitoring risks in cooperation with the EE. Accordingly, the Secretariat of the GCF has determined that the majority of the project's climate risk will be borne by the AE.

- VPO will act at the level of the project. VPO has experience with projects of a similar size and scope from bilateral and multilateral donors, including the Asian Development Bank and the International Fund for Agricultural Development, and as such, the AE. VPO will execute project activities in Tanzania through its country office in Dar es Salaam. The project will be managed by a programme unit (PMU), and subsidiary agreements with district governments and TFSs, as well as contracts with service providers, will be executed under the PMU's supervision. UNHCR has experience in overseeing and managing the implementing partners involved in the implementation of project interventions in Tanzania through its presence in the project area (in Rukiga County, Kigoma, and Kilobondo) that works with the MoLH and local authorities to ensure the successful delivery of its humanitarian activities. UNHCR has a track record of supporting local communities with climate change adaptation projects in Tanzania. The AE will provide technical assistance and guidance to the project through the AE UNHCR Tanzania has provision for a senior level, in situ risk advisor, whose work covers all aspects of the project's implementation and is aimed at mitigating potential risks.

#### 4.3 Project-specific execution risks (high risk)

## Political risk (AE and UNCR have identified a number of risks related to government policy)

These risks relate to implementation by the United Republic of Tanzania of its asylum policy in line with international standards; access for asylum seekers fleeing conflict from neighboring countries; and the overall restrictive protection environment. Through its own risk analysis, UNHCR notes that government policies and practices could create reputational risk for the AE, UNHCR, and GCF. Options for halting disbursements in the event of adverse circumstances could be limited because of the humanitarian imperative to continue providing assistance to refugees. The risks from the assessment by the AE that the project will, upon completion, contribute to the recognition that refugee status is a legal right and that refugees have rights under international law pertaining with local authorities. However, these points would not substantially reduce the political risk.

The project has been designed in such a way as to minimize the potential for adverse impacts pertaining to refugees before the project implementation has been completed could erode the project's reputation and create reputational risk for the AE, UNHCR, and GCF. This may reduce political risks but could also add complexity in case of political risks materializing.

The Secretariat has undertaken an interdepartmental analysis and acknowledges that the project needs an active engagement with the Government of Tanzania to ensure its success.
### O&M: post-implementation O&M costs are not included in project costs and are to be borne by the AE. The maintenance of water harvesting infrastructure in the camps will be turned over to MoWA. Water-harvesting infrastructure in the host communities will be turned over to district and regional authorities, with regular maintenance responsibilities assigned to them.

### Land use: Formalization of land tenure could lead to the disturbance of informal arrangements between refugees and host communities. However, the AE does not foresee this because it has been designed to ensure that all land-use activities are in accordance with agreements on diverse land-use practices in respect of village by-laws. In addition, there may be risks associated with the project's potential impact on national development objectives, adaptation and environmental objectives, such as farming along floodplains and within national and village reserves. The AE will ensure that all land use activities are carried out on communal land, and affected populations will have the option to be engaged in project activities under relevant laws and regulations. This will help to reduce any potential negative impacts.

The benefits received by host communities in the project could create a source of tension with other communities outside the project area. Close monitoring is advised on progress on land-use agreements.
