### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

123. The funding proposal is an adaptation project focusing on three result areas of the Integrated Results Management Framework: (i) most vulnerable people and communities, (ii) health and well-being, and (iii) food security and nutrition. Through application of integrated landscape approach in Ragona region of Tanzania, the project aims to achieve 10 million direct project beneficiaries, of which 9.27 million are direct and 671 million are indirect beneficiaries.

124. The theory of change (TOC) clearly defines the ultimate project goal and explicitly states the causal relationship between project activities and results. The TOC has been crafted in a manner that is closely supportive of meeting the ultimate project goal. The TOC diagram identifies on benefits, which can be achieved along the way towards climate results and captures the causal relationship between outcomes and/or benefits. The assumptions are based on the evidence from previous projects and other studies, including how the project's benefit will diffuse to indirect beneficiaries. The barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of the project goal have been identified in the project activities.

125. The logical framework is structured in alignment with the GCF integrated results management framework. It shows how the project can contribute to three GCF adaptation result areas with project outcomes and outputs, including paradigm shift and enabling environment. The indicators are aligned with the GCF integrated results management core and supplementary indicators have been chosen for monitoring and reporting the key performance of the project. These indicators will be used in conjunction with other indicators that measure aspects of project implementation along with project deliverables. A continuous feedback mechanism has enabled the identification of gaps, challenges, and opportunities to improve on result areas, indicators, means of verification, targets, assumptions, etc. They are found to be adequate and are expected to allow smooth monitoring and reporting of project results.

126. The indicators have been selected based on their relevance, reliability, and format. The milestones and deliverables that are envisaged at different stages of project implementation will be used as a basis for the development of these indicators. Adherence to the proposed implementation timetable will facilitate smooth implementation of the project as well as timely delivery of the results at planned intervals.

127. The monitoring and evaluation (M&E) reporting ( Annex 11 ) have been strengthened with the introduction of a monitoring and evaluation (M&E) specialist who will work with the lead EE to ensure that the M&E plan is implemented in line with the approved evaluation budget has been identified in the budget plan, and it is in the range of 2-5 per cent of the total programme budget as requested by the GCF Evaluation Policy. It is also confirmed that the costs of interim and final evaluations will be covered by the All-fre.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP218

## Proposal: Building Climate Resilience in the Landscapes of the Kigoma Region, United Republic of Tanzania

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)

### Country/ies):
United Republic of Tanzania

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential: Scale: N/A

Note: This project proposal was evaluated by the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (independent TAP) for the twenty-ninth meeting of the Board (R.29). However, the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania has requested that the evaluation be completed before the communications deadline for the Board meeting. Since then, the project changed some relevant aspects, including: (i) the inclusion of the Vice Presidential Task (VPT) as a co-financing entity; (ii) the change of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the implementing entity (IE), which will hire and supervise the project management unit (PMU); (iii) the change of the role of the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR) from a co-financing entity to an implementing entity; (iv) the inclusion of the technical partner to enhance project quality, and (v) the change in budget and overall indicators in different components. The effects of these changes are addressed below.

Kigoma is a region with a population of about 2.3 million people, out of a total of 56.3 million inhabitants in Tanzania. It covers an area of approximately 10,847 square kilometres (km²) in the north-west of the country. Refugees have been present in the region since the early 1960s. About 80 per cent of the refugee population in the country are accommodated in the three existing camps: Mweso, Nyarugusu and Morogoro. The total number of refugees is about 250,000 refugees from neighbouring countries, the majority of whom are living in the refugee camps of Mweso and Nyarugusu. These two camps are located on degraded land surrounding the already densely populated region on the surrounding degraded agricultural landscapes.

The Government of the United Republic of Tanzania has decided to close the Morogoro camp by 2018, with the option for voluntary repatriation or resettlement in other parts of the country for those opting for voluntary repatriation. Therefore, the final beneficiaries of the project will be defined as the refugees in the Mweso and Nyarugusu camps and host communities.


---
**Page 195**
---

4. About 70 per cent of the host community* population in the areas adjacent to the refugee camps and a substantial number of refugees are engaged in small-scale agriculture. As a result, they rely heavily on the goods and services provided by the region's ecosystems. Climate and natural resource management is therefore critical for both groups.

5. The project strategy is to ensure that the landscape and ecosystem of the Rungwa Region can continue to provide these goods and services into the future. The proposed project proposes responding to the climate change impacts affecting an already vulnerable and degraded landscape, while considering the complex needs of host communities and refugees in an environment where there are often competing interests between development priorities and social and solutions interconnect.

6. The project consists of four components: Component 1 aims to increase the resilience of ecosystems and people to climate change threats. It proposes the development of participatory, climate-resilient land-use plans and adapting the existing village land-use plans (VLUPs) by acknowledging and adding new uses for degraded lands, including livestock grazing areas and landscapes. The planning processes for host villages and camps will be separate, as these are different groups with different needs and interests in relation to their respective communities. However, the objective of developing them in a participatory manner is to ensure the most effective use of resources and land for both groups.

7. The project proposes to scale up the VLUPs to other villages and end up in the issuance of a certificate of title to the village land-use plan (VLUP) by the local government as a land occupier and user following tribal customs and traditions on land. It is also aiming to build the capacity of the regional government and support ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) and landscape management public private partnership district, ward and village-level environmental management committees (EMCs).

8. In Component 2, MoHh will be involved in developing a participatory, climate-resilient refugee camp land-use planning, but MoHh objected to the inclusion of land-use planning activities inside the camps. In the absence of land-use plans in the camps, the location and type of food production activities are not regulated by any law or regulation. The development of VLUPs will be informed by consultations with relevant stakeholders and the refugee population as well as the results from Component 1.

9. Component 2, which focuses on restoring degraded ecosystems to support climate change adaptation in host and refugee communities, aims to protect natural forest systems and enhance their resilience through a participatory approach. The project proposes that MoHh will establish and manage 30,000 hectares (ha) of village land forest reserves (VLFRs), including deforested areas, degraded lands, and other areas with high potential for reforestation by host communities. Pressure upon natural forest systems is predominantly driven by the local demand for firewood and charcoal in both refugee camps and host communities. In Rungwa Region, 80 per cent of households rely on firewood for cooking, with 81 per cent using it as a primary source.

10. This component will involve providing equipment and training for 10 forestry and natural resources officers for forest monitoring in national forest reserves. It also plans to implement 12,000 ha of agroforestry and village land afforestation to increase the resilience of

land use including the establishment of nurseries in target villages district councils in collaboration with the Tanzania Forest Service Agency TFS will lead the implementation of this activity with support from the technical partner and local non-governmental organizations NGOs) to ensure sustainability The project will be implemented in three phases Phase I involves undertaking afforestation on 2,000 ha of village land to (i) restore critical ecosystem services such as water control and (ii) establish a sustainable supply of fuelwood that will take care of the needs of households within the Mwero forest and allow it to regenerate so it can provide its ecological and livelihood support functions in the future Phase II involves implementing erosion and flood control measures on the same area TFS will conduct an assessment establish the necessary firebreak areas through casual labor and plant 10,000 trees in the first year of implementation

establish water pumping and irrigation through 50 microirrigation and 173 treadle pumps that will draw water from the rainwater harvesting locations and pump it to where it is needed.

This component will conduct training and awareness-raising for 400 members of water users associations (WUAs) and farmers on water management and efficient water use through FFS with a focus on climate resilience. It will also strengthen existing WUAs through organizing and conducting 20 meetings/trainings.

Furthermore, component 3 aims to promote climate-resilient livelihood diversification strategies in the three target districts of Tanzania. This will be achieved by establishing district-level councils, in coordination with the VPLs, who will implement the livelihood interventions at the best community level. The project team will provide technical assistance and training on the use of GCF resources. The project states that UNHCR, through the project co-funding, will implement livelihood diversification strategies for 100 refugee households in each of the three districts as possible, with the final livelihood activities to be coordinated and agreed with MoFA.

The livelihood diversification interventions include (i) agroforestry and tree conservation in the forests surrounding the camps and villages by providing 52 hiveshives, equipment and training for ESO people; and (ii) mushroom cultivation for marginalized groups with training and provision of materials. The project also includes a component on beekeeping and mushroom groups. However, the proposal is not explicit with regard to the number of livelihood interventions or the resources needed to support the refugee communities, all subject to the approval of MoFA.

Component 4 focuses on information and on mainstreaming climate change adaptation in policy and planning processes at national level. This will be achieved by supporting the planning processes and physical interventions implemented under output 1.3 to demonstrate the economic benefits of climate-resilient livelihood diversification strategies, which will contribute to the sustainability and scalability of the outputs and GCF fund-level outcomes at the national level. The project team will also support the mainstreaming of climate change into humanitarian programming through UNHCR, as the project has substantially supported the reform process.

The project beneficiaries (drawn from both the host communities and refugee camps) are estimated at 128,100 people, consisting of approximately 570,300 direct (292,000 women and 277,500 men) and indirect (288,000 children and 291,000 adults) beneficiaries in the camps. Of the direct beneficiaries, 36 per cent are refugees, with a population in November 2021 of 150,540 people living in three refugee camps (Machakos, Kigamboni and Mwanga), mostly from host communities) adopt, at the end of the project, diversified, climate-resilient livelihoods in agriculture, beekeeping and mushroom growing.

The project will also support the development of infrastructure, building small-scale infrastructure to support flood control, water harvesting and efficient irrigation interventions, and on the rehabilitation of existing infrastructure such as boreholes, wells and other water services. In terms of flood control interventions, as mentioned above, the development of the camps was done within the context of humanitarian pressures, with suboptimal land use planning and a lack of consideration for climate change impacts, including increased flooding and an increase in precipitation intensity due to climate change.

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

## 1.2.1 Innovation

The project acknowledges the knowledge of land use in the project site as well as the complex and dynamic relationship between the human settlements (refugees and host communities) and the ecosystems available, taking into consideration the increased challenges associated with climate change.

The integrated approach to project design and the challenges of both host communities and refugees to support their livelihoods while adapting to the changing climate are well analyzed in the project. However, as explained above, the current funding proposal has


---
**Page 199**
---

reduced impact on refugee communities and therefore the majority of the interventions in land use, agriculture, water and livelihoods will end up mostly benefiting the host communities. Moreover, there is a risk that the refugee camps will end up closing as was the case with the Mwamba camp in 2015. The current project proposal does not address this risk. The dominant humanitarian-climate nexus is not as promising or transformational as in the original FP assessed by IAP back in February 2021.

The proposed climate-related interventions need further refinement and thought. The project is relying on external technical support to deliver some of the interventions and is adding new ones that are not yet fully understood, such as agroforestry and livestock from GCF. Moreover, the project lacks viable marketing alternatives to support the communities with agroforestry interventions.

The Tanzanian government policy in the United Republic of Tanzania, refugees are legally required to remain in the camps, and they are not allowed to be engaged in formal employment either inside or outside the camps, thus having to rely on humanitarian assistance or the informal economy within the camp.


| 26 | The United Republic of Tanzania has been generous in accepting refugees, mainly from Burundi and Rwanda. However, the government is increasingly concerned about the refugees' right to open or close refugee camps based on its own policies towards refugees. It has also experienced a number of challenges related to the management of refugee camps due to political instability. On some occasions, it has closed camps due to the repatriation of refugees. For example, in 2012, some camps in Kigoma Region were closed, and then reopened in 2015. Between 2016 and 2018, the government decided to close three refugee camps, and it has decided to close one camp. The permanence of these camps in the future will depend on the regional refugee situation and the political stability in Burundi and Rwanda.


| 27 | Although the refugee camps are relatively well situated topographically, Tanzanian authorities and camp planners were probably unable to account for long-term development pressures such as urbanization, population growth, and climate change-related phenomena. Increasingly frequent floods are damaging the shelters, roads and communal facilities. These floods cause damage to infrastructure, which in turn leads to poor access to water supply, health and sanitation. Floods also lead to deep gully erosion, which places community safety at risk of landslides and soil loss, as well as increasing vulnerability to climate change and land erosion. To respond to this challenge, the project provided detailed plans and designs to implement flood mitigation measures in all three camps.


| 28 | This opens a question to the Board in terms of investing GCF resources in public goods for temporary humanitarian relief projects. In response to the question of the adequacy of the livelihoods interventions proposed by the stakeholders, when either closed or reallocated, the project proponents answered that livestock activities would be absorbed by host communities and that agroforestry would provide a source of income for displaced occupants against climate-driven food events. However, in the case of the Morondva camp, the future occupants are yet to be defined, and even if they end up in the hands of the district authorities, it is not clear how these activities will be implemented or what their ultimate purpose of the investments, which is to support the adaptive livelihood of refugee communities.


| 29 | The project proponents have proposed a number of interventions such as beekeeping and mushroom growing, are well thought out that they support healthier diets on reduced land area and ensure some income generation. Participants will receive training as well as technical assistance in these activities from the GCF-funded project. According to the proponents, in the stakeholder consultations, refugees showed a lot of interest in these activities because they can be done at home and are not time-consuming. Also, they return to their country of origin, thus positively contributing to their self-reliance. However, the current funding proposal has erased the GCF funds allocated to these activities in


---
**Page 200**
---

refugees, which now rely on co-financing at a more limited scale. Therefore, the beneficiaries of the GCF-funded livelihood interventions will mainly be host communities.

### In terms of land-use planning, the proposal has an innovative approach, as the long-term legal outcome of the VLUP is the issuance of a CCRO. This document specifies rights conferred to the community and provides security for their livelihoods. The CCRO will provide long-term security for the villagers and may be used as collateral for borrowing money from financial institutions.

### By adopting a landscape approach with coordinated land-use planning across the project area, the project could offer innovation and potential to generate new lessons learned in the practice of VLUPs in the country.

#### The agroforestry component is based on the need to ensure basic subsistence crops, such as maize and beans. These crops will be planted in a way that takes climate change into account. The project proponents argue that these crops were selected based on climatic suitability and availability of seeds and fertilizers.

### The farmers will have access to seeds and FFSs, aiming to bring better productive technologies as well as sustainable and conservation practices. This will be complemented with agroforestry, afforestation, and reforestation activities in the project area. These interventions aim at reducing the loss of topsoil rich in organic material.

#### However, the benefits for host communities from the landscape interventions would come in the form of common ecosystem services, such as restored critical forest ecosystem services to manage floods and a sustainable supply of firewood to relieve pressure on the native Montane forests. These will be provided by the project through agroforestry, afforestation, and reforestation functions.

#### The project will implement community-based forest management (CBFM) measures for resilient ecosystems. Under CBFM, villagers take full ownership and management responsibility for an area of forest as a VLUP. Following this legal transfer of rights and responsibilities from central to local government, the communities will be able to manage their forests and retain forest royalties and undertake patrols (including arresting and fining offenders). Ensuring that the community has the capacity to manage its resources effectively is key. Instead of there being a centralized “command and control” type of arrangement, it is one of the key paradigms for VLUPs.

#### The establishment of VLUPs is a provision of the Forest Act of 2002. However, most of the VLFPs in Kakololo, Kausa and Kilindo Districts are still in the stage of declaration by the village communities. Currently, there are only two villages with VLUPs covering an area of about 15% of total forest reserves over 30,000 ha.

#### In Kigoma Region, 90 per cent of households rely on firewood for cooking, with 81 per cent using charcoal and 24 per cent using kerosene or other charcoal. The project proposes establishing 2,000 ha of forest lots producing a sustainable supply of firewood. This will take some of the pressure off the Montane forests in the region by providing an alternative source of fuel for cooking and livelihood support functions, benefiting both refugee and host communities.

#### The project presents a good mix of alternatives (micro-rainwater harvesting systems, runoff water harvesting, and rainwater harvesting trenches) to reduce soil erosion; increase water availability and use water efficiently through water harvesting and efficient irrigation interventions. These are common practices in the region and have been used by communities. These water systems are common and do not constitute an innovation per se. However, if they are well

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment and regulatory framework

The project will conduct training and awareness-raising on climate change and good environmental practices for district governments. The training will include the understanding of projected climate changes, the importance of ecosystem-based adaptation strategies and the importance of local ecosystems and the services they provide.

#### Potential for knowledge and learning

The project will also develop a series of training modules that contribute to deforestation and ecosystem degradation in the project area but are not currently included in wider community conservation or land-use planning processes. The training that would be provided, including those on agroforestry systems, sustainable agriculture and alternative livelihoods, would contribute to having a better inclusion strategy to allow these groups to be part of the landscape restoration goals and solutions.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: N/A

#### 1.3.1 Environmental co-benefits

The benefits associated with the EDA approach will include the functionality of the overall ecosystem services. Some of these ecological services include the retention of water that will support the evapotranspiration regime, the limiting of the effects of floods, and the reduction in erosion. The establishment of forest and agroforestry systems would also increase biodiversity. It could also support natural habitats to allow forest species to regain their dominance over invasive alien species. Forests are also important as pollination, pest control and the provision of food and non-timber products.

The project will establish forestry and agroforestry systems that will supply wood and food to selected communities, thereby preventing the expansion of the agricultural frontier and land degradation.


---
**Page 203**
---

## Economic co-benefits

### 13.2.1

The project expects to create livelihood alternatives with agroforestry systems and trees resulting in a net increase of approximately 500 jobs per year, including direct and indirect green jobs, most of which are expected to persist even after the end of the project. However, these alternatives will mostly benefit the host communities. The refugees will have the opportunity to participate in the agroforestry systems through co-financing, but these will be limited and based on agreements with MoFA. Improved and diversified livelihoods will also provide greater opportunities for women and youth to connect to markets, thus generating revenues to achieve a better quality of life for the communities. In addition to increased productivity, the communities would be able to have more stable incomes throughout the year.

The project will result in ecosystem services that should be supporting (e.g., nutrient cycling and soil fertility; pest control; pollination and non-timber forest products); regulating (e.g. water regulation and flood control); and cultural (e.g. recreation and education).

### References

**Page 204**

All of these services will serve to support income generation and job creation and ensure long-term rural livelihood sustainability.

* The economic value of ensuring water availability through harvesting systems, which provide members of the host communities with access to a more reliable source of water, is potentially significant. This will enable them to diversify their crops and increase their crop production and revenues. The infrastructure put in place for these purposes will also have long-term benefits for soil health and conservation.

* The project will also support the SA in allowing individuals from the host communities to build together to save and borrow money and maintain their agroforestry systems. This will ensure the long-term sustainability of the initial investments by the project, allowing the communities to continue the activities beyond the end of the project.


### 1.3 Social co-benefits

* The project expects to ensure social cohesion between host and refugee communities, enabling a more peaceful and safer environment while creating a sense of "belonging" and respect for nature. However, the options for refugee communities are limited as the project provides few options to improve their quality of life and they are obliged to stay within the camp limits.

* The project will also support the SA in allowing individuals from the host communities to build together to save and borrow money and maintain their agroforestry systems, and they will have a better standard of living from increased income. Having village plans in place will allow the communities to work together and ensure long-term land use planning for sustainable development.

* Communities will have greater knowledge of climate change and be able to decide on their production strategies accordingly. When forest and agroforestry systems are restored, the effect on microclimates can improve community livelihoods around the selected areas.

* The project aims to improve the quality of life of host and refugee communities by helping to avoid the diseases related to water contamination and storage, thus reducing risks of water- and vector-borne illnesses during flood incidents. Moreover, the project will help reduce exposure to malaria vectors and contracting sleeping sickness.


#### 1.3.4 Gender-sensitive approaches

* In the United Republic of Tanzania, gender inequality is a salient issue. Although the overall labour-force participation rate (including the informal sector) of women in the country is 70%, women are underrepresented in decision-making positions.

* Women also participate in low-productivity sectors. Specifically, women account for most of the agricultural workforce, with about 90 per cent of women living in rural areas participating in agriculture.


#### 1.3.5 Five-year National Action Plan to End Violence Against Women and Children (2017/18-2021/22)

* A five-year National Action Plan to End Violence Against Women and Children (2017/18–2021/22) has been developed by consolidating eight different action plans addressing violence against women and children. The plan is a key component of the national plan of action to end violence against women and children in the country.

* The Village Land Committees are not only responsible for ensuring women's rights, with some sections of law still inviting any customary practices that may criminalize women. The act upholds the right of every woman to acquire, hold, use, and deal with land; voids the role of customary law in determining land tenure; and recognizes women's rights to land in the event


---
**Page 205**
---

of the death of their spouse or upon divorce. There are also requirements for female representation in key decision-making bodies. The project will make sure that the law is effectively implemented through the support of village plans.

Despite efforts by UNHCR and its partners, refugee women and children continue to be exposed to a range of risks including sexual violence, gender-based violence, child abuse and early and forced marriages. Some of these risks relate to existing negative cultural norms and practices in host communities and among refugees themselves.

The gender action plan has an overall impact statement to "improve land management and increased resilience of host communities and refugees, including poor and female-headed households". The project will support the development of a climate change adaptation strategy for resilient agriculture and livelihood diversification and improved water management and drainage.

The project involves the following outcomes: (1) women are represented, and participate meaningfully, in land-use planning at the village and camp levels; (2) CBFM and agroforestry practices are promoted to improve food security and income generation for refugees; (3) the adaptive capacity of women farmers is increased and women's resilience to climate change is increased. The project will support the development of a gender action plan that addresses these needs, interests and knowledge are meaningfully captured in mainstreaming climate change adaptation strategies at all levels. The gender action plan is well developed and has specific activities, indicators, and a budget to ensure its development.

Overall, the plan is more oriented to support women of the host communities. The project recognizes that women are disproportionately affected by climate change impacts with the negative impacts of densely populated camps on the security and livelihoods of women. Moreover, women in refugee settlements have limited access to land and water but have just a few livelihood alternatives facilitated through resources from UNFCCC or agencies (e.g., the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania). The plan includes refugee women in the agriculture component of the project activities; however it is not clear if they will be able to benefit from these trainings to support their livelihoods.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: N/A

#### 1.4 Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups

The United Republic of Tanzania is a country in East Africa with an area of about 945,000 km². Following two decades of sustained growth, the country reached an important milestone by becoming one of only four countries to have achieved lower-middle-income status. In 2019, it was one of the top three growth performers in East Africa, enjoying a GDP growth rate of 6.3 per cent in 2018 and 5.7 per cent in 2019. The growth rate is projected to slow down to an estimated 2.8 per cent in 2020, as shocks to export-oriented sectors such as tourism, manufacturing and related services diminished the country's performance. This has led to a decline in remittances, which adversely affects domestically oriented firms of all sizes across all sectors.

President Samia Suluhu Hassan was sworn in on 15 March 2021 to serve as the United Republic of Tanzania’s sixth and first woman president, following the death of President John


---
**Page 206**
---

# Magafuoli on 17 March 2021: The government is prioritizing the implementation of a new strategy to contain the COVID-19 pandemic and has a renewed approach to regional trade and cooperation, with commitments to domestic policy reforms designed to improve business environment.

## In this period 2011–2018, the agriculture sector's contribution to GDP grew much more slowly than those of the rest of the economy, averaging 4.4 per cent a year, or 1.4 per cent per capita. With about 25 per cent of the population of about 3.6 million living below the poverty line of US$1.90 per day (World Bank), and with limited access to social services, agriculture sector growth is crucial for poverty reduction. Despite having fertile land and water resources, the agricultural sector has not been able to meet its potential.

### Rural areas such as Région face unique development challenges, including changing climate, socioeconomic inequality and a fluctuating population of refugees from neighboring countries, particularly Burundi and the Democratic Republic of Congo. Moreover, infrastructure constraints, low agricultural production, a limited fiscal base, insufficient human capital investment, and weak institutions are major obstacles to Uganda's development.

#### The country has been one of the most generous to refugees not only in East Africa, but also globally. The country has received refugees mainly from Burundi and the Democratic Republic of Congo. The United Nations estimates that by 2023, 500,000 refugees were living in camps across Uganda, with an additional 184,000 refugees living outside camps. In 2019, 37,000 refugees living there returned to Burundi. In 2015, political instability again resulted in civil war and the displacement of hundreds of thousands of people from Burundi into Tanzania. To meet this increased demand, refugees were transferred to the Mwende and Nkuta camps, which are now home to more than 374,000 refugees.

#### About 25 per cent of the current refugees come from the Democratic Republic of Congo. The Nyaruguru camp hosts the largest proportion of refugees from the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). However, as the number of refugees from Burundi and the DRC remain present, there is no certainty as to the lifetime of the refugee camps in Uganda. It is estimated that 10 per cent of the refugees currently accommodated in the three camps will return to their own countries in the long run. However, the United Republic of Tanzania is actively encouraging the repatriation of refugees, and a significant number have already returned. The government has therefore launched this project to the refugee communities, that the refugees should not receive special support.

### Persistent poverty, high levels of unemployment, and limited access to education are major factors in the decision by many young people to migrate from their home countries. Most refugees live as asylum seekers or stateless persons in camps or settlements. The majority of refugees living there rely on food aid for a large extent, and there is no certainty about the lifetime of these camps.

#### The locations are officially considered camps. However, for many refugees, the camps are permanent places where they have lived for decades. They are not temporary transit centres.


---
**Page 207**
---

# Challenges of environmental drivers of conflict remain in the region due to the scarcity of land and the overexploitation of resources, which are mainly used for subsistence agriculture.

## In terms of livelihoods and economic activity, refugees are compelled by ever-decreasing funding to engage in activities to support themselves financially. Although there have been some improvements in the agricultural sector, particularly in the area of livestock and economic activity within the camps, the Government has been lenient in enforcing restrictions on the refugee population. This has led to overexploitation of resources, especially among the Burundian people.

### The project places a priority on supporting the host communities neighbouring the camp, namely those directly impacted by the establishment of the camp. Despite the encouragement from the Government and NGOs, there are still challenges in ensuring that host communities secure their livelihoods. These are informal employment arrangements and agricultural land-based livelihoods. This is further exacerbated by the lack of access to water resources and the closure of common markets. Refugees leave the camps frequently to collect firewood from the land of the host communities.

### The majority of the population in the project consists of farmers and livestock keepers. About 70 per cent of the population in the region are engaged in small-scale farming, with other income-generating activities such as fishing, hunting, and petty trading. These resources include food, medicines, fuelwood and other items from village forests, especially in Miondo woodlands. The current lack of land-use planning and management of customary land can lead to conflicts between refugees and host communities over access to these resources.

### In conclusion, the needs of the recipients are high, as both vulnerable host and refugee communities are competing for livelihood alternatives in the same landscape, sharing scarce resources under climate change.


---
**Page 208**
---

The grade of climate-smart agriculture: reducing impacts of climate-related shocks through risk management and strengthening knowledge and systems for targeted climate action. Moreover, the goal of the Tanzania Climate Smart Agriculture Programme 2015–2025 is to have an “agricultural sector that is more resilient to climate change, more productive, more competitive and food secure for national economic development”. The programme focuses on six core objectives, including increasing the resilience of the agricultural sector through (appropriate) climate-smart agriculture practices that consider gender and the needs of smallholder farmers of agricultural and food systems.

The country’s intended nationally determined contribution identifies adaptation as a national priority. The severity of climate-related disasters and the impacts associated with annual temporal variability of rainfall and droughts and floods and heatwaves are all recognized. The country has aligned its climate change priorities with those in the National Adaptation Programme of Action (2009), the National Climate Change Strategy 2012, the Guidelines for the Implementation of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and Programmes of Tanzania (2012), the country’s intended nationally determined contribution (2015) and the National Five-Year Development Plan 2016–2027.

The National Five-Year Development Plan 2016/17–2021 defines specific sectoral interventions in natural resources management and agricultural development. In particular, the document identifies climate change adaptation measures at catchment/WUA level including and adapting crop and livestock production systems to changing climatic conditions and increasing water harvesting technologies.

The Tanzania Country Refugee Response Plan (January 2019–December 2020) for refugees from Burundi and the Democratic Republic of Congo includes: “wider efforts within the environment sector, including climate change adaptation measures in the areas of disaster risk reduction and mitigation. This will be undertaken in the context of both the host community and the refugee communities”. The plan also identifies priority interventions at the catchment level such as forest landscape restoration, sustainable forest management, community-based natural resource management, integrated water resources management, watershed management, water harvesting technologies, Integrated Water Resource Management, river catchment conservation and the adoption of renewable energy technologies.

In 2018, the District Executive Secretary and the Regional Administrative Secretary of Rungwa have identified the region’s highest priorities for development. These priorities include climate change adaptation measures such as disaster risk reduction and community participation in forest management and reforestation, both of which are addressed through this project.

The Tanzania Independent TAP has noted that the project does not include any type of co-funding by the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania. This appears unusual for many rural development projects in Rungwa District. However, given that the independent TAP on this matter, the proposals have explained that the project is very cognizant of investments made by district and regional authorities, TFS and other government entities.

In the United Republic of Tanzania, all public funds are centrally controlled. District budgets are prepared by the District Executive Secretary and approved by the central government through the district executive director, and are then confirmed and allocated annually. The district, regional and TFS budget requirements for environment- and forestry


---
**Page 209**
---

related activities are reported to be quite modest in the three districts concerned. However, the actual amount allocated is believed to be even smaller due to the government’s competing priorities and limited financial resources. Due to the annual public budgeting cycles, the various governmental agencies involved have not been able to provide a clear picture of their contributions. In light of these complications of having these contributions reflected as co-financing in the project budget, it is suggested that the government contribution be considered as parallel financing.

It should also be noted that the TFS and other relevant institutions are not directly involved in the proposal, so it cannot be considered an off-finance. The project sustainability will greatly depend on the capacity of the TFS to gradually manage and scale up the project interventions without the financial support of GCF and technical assistance from UNDP. This is particularly important for the sustainable management and operation of flood and water harvest interventions, the GIS system and the forestry and agriculture interventions.

### 3.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

UNEP will be the accredited entity (AE). The VPO of the Government of the United Republic of Tanzania will act as the lead EE of the project, ensuring alignment with the national policies and strategies and providing overall guidance on the implementation of the project within the country’s NAP process. As the lead EE, VPO will oversee overall project implementation and be accountable to the AE for the effective use of resources during project implementation.

VPO will also ensure that the project is implemented in a participatory manner through direct execution by PMU, co-implementation agreements with district governments and TFS, as well as contractual arrangements with technical service providers. In addition, where capacity has been acquired, the engagement of technical experts and national/international NGOs. The district governments, under the supervision of VPO, will ensure delegated activities at the district level directly and may be contracted out to local government agencies or other entities that have the required service providers. The selection of the technical experts and national and international NGOs will be coordinated with the AE and the PMU in close consultation with the lead EE and the project team and with the aim of accelerating project implementation.

The new project proposal includes the engagement of a technical partner with expertise in livelihoods, food security, and nutrition to provide technical advisory and capacity-building services to district officials, TFS officials and participant communities in the implementation of the project. The technical partner will be responsible for ensuring that there is a careful technical and knowledge transfer plan in place to ensure that the national entities end up having the capacity to continue with the project activities after project completion.

UNICOR will be the EE for the project activity 2.3 (Implication of land use and land rotation within and outside the area of the de la Mole and Nyamagana refugee camps and the Mandelii decommissioned camp) and subactivities 12.1 and 12.2 (Construct micro-rainwater harvesting systems in the de la Mole and Nyamagana refugee camps and construct a water supply system at T37 treadle pump). Through committed co-financing, UNICOR will implement activities in the camps targeting refugees and displaced persons living there (de la Mole and Nyamagana) and T37. However, the livelihood support needs to be further explained in the current proposal, which has eliminated investments from GCF for these activities for the refugee camps.

More specifically, UNICOR will provide technical assistance to refugees living in the refugee camps by coordinating the planning and monitoring of camp activities with UNHCR. This will include consultation on the location and design of infrastructure in the camp. MoU’s will also

The goal of the climate change-related activities of UNHCR in Africa includes supporting countries to enhance their capacity to adapt to climate change through the implementation of the DRR/DRR+ framework. The goal is to ensure that the project contributes to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement. The UNFCCC knowledge base has been derived from completed and ongoing projects that support: (i) methods for assessing vulnerability, risk, and adaptation needs; (ii) planning and implementing adaptation interventions; (iii) enhancing climate resilience by restoring vulnerable ecosystems that underpin community livelihoods; and (iv) monitoring the socioeconomic and environmental impacts of climate change.

### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

The project design has been based on prior consultations, including with national and district governments, as well as with key stakeholders in the project area.

#### First round of consultation took place from March to April 2017 and involved key stakeholders in the project area, including the government, NGOs, implementers, and communities. The first round also included focus groups with potential beneficiaries as well as meetings with each camp commander and delivery partners currently responsible for implementing interventions in each camp.

The second round of consultation was carried out during the feasibility study visit in September 2017 to identify necessary arrangements and mechanisms for project interventions and gather information about necessary management and arrangements structures for the implementation of the project. The second round of consultations took place in November 2018. It involved a series of strategic meetings with stakeholders, including the Permanent Secretary and senior officials within the VPO. The consultation review process also included the formulation of a monitoring framework.

A detailed round of consultations on the project design was undertaken between 7 and 11 September 2020 to validate the project design. Consultations sought to: (i) identify the most appropriate mix of interventions (econometric consultation), and (ii) ensure the validity of the technical and environmental assumptions (technical consultation).

The third round of consultations took place on 9–10 September 2021, when representatives from the VPO, UNHCR, MoAID and UNDP met in person in Dar es Salaam to discuss the project execution plan and the proposed changes. The meeting focused specifically on discussing the changes in the proposal, as explained in this document. Consultations with TFS officers took place on 25 September 2021. These consultations were held to further refine the project execution plan and assess gaps in terms of equipment and information management systems, as well as to identify the appropriate investments in fire risk management. Between 15 September and 1 October 2021, TFS officers met with representatives from the VPO to discuss the funding proposal and annexes based on the agreements reached in Dar es Salaam and subsequent meetings.


---
**Page 212**
---

However, during 2021, no consultations were undertaken with the communities (both host and refugee communities), and the changes in the project were decided using a more top-down approach.

The project has presented socioeconomic questionnaires used during consultation, including interviews by relevant stakeholders. However, it has not presented numbers on the men and women consulted and involved in each focus group.

The objective of the stakeholder engagement and social inclusion plan is to engage all relevant stakeholders in the planning process for the project implementation phase and a stakeholder engagement table. However, based on the consultation that has already taken place, the project will need to design a more robust consultation process at the beginning of project implementation and continue the consultations on an ongoing basis while including women's organizations (e.g., the Kibera Women's Cooperative) and other civil society supporting organizations (e.g., the Kibera Beedhopping Cooperative) and district officials.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A

### 1.6.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

The total project cost is estimated at USD 23,618,646 of which GCF is expected to provide USD 17,996,032 as grant resources. UNHCR will be the only co-financer, providing USD 4,642,721.

Component 1 on VLP involves an investment of USD 1,062,805 from GCF representing a reduction in the costs of developing climate-resilient refugee camps and our planning for the future. Component 3 on conserving biodiversity and protecting ecosystems and delivering flood reduction infrastructure will require USD 13,038,743 (USD 9,797,657 from GCF) to conserve biodiversity and USD 3,240,288 (USD 2,239,086 from GCF) to deliver flood reduction infrastructure. Finally, Component 4 on mainstreaming climate change in plans and policies is estimated to cost USD 469,554 with GCF funding.

The project will require the following resources: (i) a technical partner to support 13,000 ha of agroforestry and 2,000 ha of afforestation; (ii) a consultant to provide technical assistance in developing the business case for the coverage of farmers targeted from 16 per cent to 25 per cent, resulting in the increase of agricultural input beneficiaries from 13,000 to 13,000 households and the provision of more improved seeds; (iii) a consultant to provide technical assistance in developing the business case for forest monitoring; (iv) a technical partner to support 13,000 ha of agroforestry and 2,000 ha of afforestation.

It is noted that the erosion and flood infrastructure for the three camps account for USD 547,896. It should be noted that the cost of these facilities is high because a big proportion of the project is, it is important to realize that if the refugee camps are closed, the use of these resources might not be justified on climate change grounds.

As per the TAP, the project has been identified as having potential for money. The independent TAP enquired about the low costs of the forestry and agroforestry systems. The proposed cost of USD 102,584 is based on actual local costs from the field benchmarked against existing projects.

The cost of protecting village forest reserves is estimated at USD 420,000 for 20 villages (estimated to cover an area of approximately 37 ha). This includes the costs of land acquisition and facilitation costs for establishing reserves and their management arrangements, and that no input and labour costs are included. Therefore, there is a need to understand whether the government will pay for labour costs, and how they are to be accounted for in its parallel budget.

The project proposal presents the internal rate of return for subactivities, with all of them having a positive internal rate of return as shown in the following table.

| Establish and maintain agroforestry systems (AFS) for production of tree crops | 98% | $8,028,099 | $43 |

| Increase water availability and use efficiency through water harvesting and efficient irrigation interventions | 19% | $3,059,447 | $158 |

The overall project's economic internal rate of return is estimated to be 13 per cent when the value of greenhouse gas mitigation benefits are excluded and 10 per cent when they are factored in.

## Project Arrangements

The project involves an operation and maintenance plan that is well developed, explaining the arrangements to maintain interventions during and after project completion. In terms of project sustainability, the project's most important strategies include:

- **SAAs**: The project proposes a set of small area activities (SAAs) as reproductive interventions in the project. The SAAs are proposed as a key delivery mechanism for the project. They allow individuals to pool finances together to save and borrow money based on their own terms, with interest rates that are lower than those offered by existing structures by channeling funds through SfG for the purchase of climate-resilient agricultural inputs and other activities in targeted refugee communities, losing out on an opportunity to give them some economic relief.
- **Long-term land use plans**: Landscaping interventions
- **Support for the development of Climate Change Adaptation policies, plans and strategies**

## Overall Remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP would like to stress that the interventions in the refugee camps will be implemented under a climate change adaptation lens and not with the Mwandi camp. Moreover, the restrictive policies of the Republic of Tanzania towards refugees, which are likely to continue after the project closure, may limit the capacity of hosts and refuge in an environment where climate, humanitarian and development approaches intersect. In light of this, the independent TAP considers that GCF investment in activities to be implemented within the camps during the project period might not be validated under a climate-humanitarian-development lens in the event of camp closure.

The independent TAP also regards that the host agreement is subject to the following conditions:

- Prior to the second disbursement of funds by GCF under the funded activity agreement, the AE shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat:
  - An updated stakeholder engagement plan including:
    * Detailed direct beneficiary mapping in the host communities and in the refugee camps
    * Results from the ongoing community consultations and participation of project beneficiaries – drawn from both the host communities and refugee camps – in the project activities.
- Prior to the third disbursement of funds by GCF under the funded activity agreement, the AE shall deliver, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat:
  - A sustainability plan including:
    * The human resources and financial arrangements with governmental bodies at national and local levels, to ensure the effective implementation of the project activities during and after project completion.
    * The benefit-sharing arrangements in host communities and refugee camps that evidence how the benefits derived from the project are accrued and shared

#### 7. The FP sets out the climate rationale for the camp areas and surrounding communities. The camps are at the top of the watershed. Hydraulic analysis shows that flooding in Kitara, Kasanda and Kakoloko Districts occurs primarily as a result of precipitation falling within the camp area itself. This is because the catchment area is small relative to the population. The occurrence of flooding events and subsequent riverbank and other land erosion. Current land use practices are not sustainable and will lead to further degradation of the landscape and inundation under. Under no adaptation scenario, this increase will be 4.9% or 300 ha of additional floodplain area (Figure 12.2). The expected adaptation benefits for host population surrounding Miondoli include:

- Flood risk reduction in villages located downstream of the degraded Miondoli camp area.
- Protection of downstream agricultural lands and soils from further flooding, erosion and siltation, thereby improving food security.
- Reduction of water-borne water resources in the catchment thus improving water quality.
- Provision of roads and services infrastructure in the decommissioned camp area and in downstream villages.
- Opportunities for expansion of climate resilient productive activities in the restored former camp areas.

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Introduction

### The context: gender issues in Tanzania

Gender inequality is a salient issue in Tanzania. Over the last decades, the government has enacted policies aimed at promoting women's participation in the economy and improving their equality. These policies have focused on greater representation of women in government and business, the importance of gender equality to the country’s development and land rights for women. However, the implementation of these policies has been challenging, due in large part to cultural norms and the application of customary law.

There is significant discrepancy in the economic roles of men and women. Although the overall labor force participation rate of the female population in Tanzania is 88.7%, only 29% are employed in the formal economy. Gender segregation persists with women accounting for the majority of the workforce in agriculture (52%) and the trading sector (50%). Women also dominate the informal sector, particularly in retail trade and finance sectors. Important gender differences persist in the remuneration of workers, whereby only 4% of employed women in Tanzania are in paid jobs in either the formal or informal sector and women earn on average 36% less than their male counterparts.

Women continue to be participating in low productivity activities, such as subsistence farming for the majority of the agricultural workforce, with approximately 90% of women living in rural areas participating in the sector. However, returns in the agriculture sector have declined over time, with the average yield per hectare falling from 15 tons in 2003 to 8 tons in 2014. Low productivity in the sector is largely a consequence of inadequate investment and lack of access to farm inputs, extension services, credit, modern technology application, trade and marketing facilities, as well as poor infrastructure and unsuitable use of natural resources also play a role in reducing productivity.

Presently in Tanzania, women are responsible for the majority of domestic work. Men have traditional expectations of women’s role in household chores. Women who have jobs outside of the home often return to their homes after work to complete household chores. The division of labour begins early, with young girls, even those in school, being expected to shoulder their share of household chores. This is not expected of their brothers.* Women in Tanzania spend 50.8% of these time on unpaid care work whereas men spend 9% of their time on such work.*

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

25% of Tanzanian households are headed by women, approximately half of whom have been widowed. In rural areas, 41% of all households are female-headed versus 27% in urban areas. Female headed households (FHHs) have a larger number of dependents than male headed households (MHHs) with an average of 1.6 dependents in rural areas as compared to 1.3 for MHHs. The percentage of FHHs is higher in both rural and urban areas, and women with higher levels of education correlate with better wellbeing conditions. This is critical for rural women, 49% of whom have no educational attainment at all, as compared to 40% of rural men. Because of their lower level of education, women are more likely than men to experience poorer wellbeing conditions. This condition continues for members of FHHs who, on average, fewer years of education than members of MHHs.

Women in Tanzania face additional prejudice and, in some cases, violence. According to UN Women, 15% of Tanzanian women have experienced physical violence by their partners and Girls (IWRG) are two out of 15 women between the age of 20-24 who have experienced physical violence and one out of ten women of the same age range has experienced rape.

In terms of needs, women farmers require increased access to capital and educational resources to increase their adaptive capacity. Land tenure is also a critical limitation on women's ability to become climate resilient.


## 1.1. Kigoma region

Kigoma is among the poorest regions in Tanzania, with a poverty rate of 43.4%. 76% of the population lives below the national poverty line. Many of the trends seen at the national level are evident in Kigoma. The percentage of FHHs in Kigoma is higher in both rural and urban areas as 34.8% and 30.5%, respectively. The average size of FHHs is 7.8 as compared to 4.3 for MHHs. The 2011/2012 Tanzania National Bureau of Statistics, Household Budget Survey shows that Kigoma has the highest poverty rates, suggesting that FHHs in Kigoma are likely to be poorer than MHHs. Literacy rate is higher amongst men (73%) than women (67%).

Nearly 25% of people in Kigoma have never attended school. There is a significant gender gap with regard to education. According to the 2012 census, 28% of females in Kigoma have never attended school versus 21.5% of males. 28.1% of girls were attending school as compared to 32.6% of males. National level trends suggest that educational attainment is

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Adherence to traditional gender roles results in women and girls assuming responsibility for domestic chores such as water collection, firewood, and food preparation. Women are recognized as breadwinners expected to provide for their families. Women and girls are responsible for the collection of water and firewood as well as farming activities. The dependency on these resources is high; the risk increases as these resources become scarcer, requiring women and girls to travel ever greater distances. Additionally, dependency on firewood for cooking fuel negatively impacts women's health due to exposure to smoke. Women are also responsible for the health of all household members, but especially women, who are responsible for food preparation and the related challenge of obtaining cooking fuel, often in the form of firewood.

## 1.2. Legal and Administrative Framework for Women and Gender Equality

At the policy level, Tanzania has demonstrated a significant commitment to achieving gender equality. In 1990, the Ministry of Community Development, Gender and Children (MCGC), formerly known as the Ministry of Community Development, Women Affairs and Children (MDLWAC) was established.

Over the following years, a number of policies and legislative acts have been passed to this effect, promoting equality and discrimination and the use of affirmative action. These include:

- The Land Act and Village Land Act of 1999 repealed customary and traditional practices with the aim of increasing gender equality in land tenure, use, and management.
- A year later, in 2000, legislation was passed to increase the number of women in government positions to 30% by 2005. At present, women hold 36% of parliamentary seats and 20% of ministerial positions.17 33% of local government seats are also reserved for women.
- The Small and Medium Enterprise (SME) Development Policy of 2003 highlights the significance of women's role in SMEs and includes provisions to support women entrepreneurs.
- Two key poverty reduction strategy papers, MKURUTA I and II (in 2005 and 2010, respectively), recognized the significance of gender equality and women's empowerment.

Gender Empowerment and Action Plan

The Five-Year National Development Plan emphasizes women’s economic empowerment as a key strategy for reducing poverty and achieving equality.

Tanzania Vision 2050 highlights gender equality between genders in reference to Tanzania's Constitutions. Additionally, equality between genders is recognized as a key strategy for addressing the country's development challenges.

The Tanzanian government has undertaken several additional activities to promote gender equality. Gender responsive budgeting was introduced with the aim of ensuring that public funds advance gender equality. In 2010, Tanzania became a signatory to the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development, which calls for at least 30% representation in all decision-making bodies.

The National Plan of Action to End Violence Against Women and Children in Tanzania (2017/18-2021/22), a five-year plan, was developed by considering eight different action plans addressing violence against women and children. The plan aims to create a single comprehensive National Plan of Action to eradicate violence against women and children by focusing on building preventive systems and responding to the needs of survivors.

The implementation of this Plan is the direct responsibility of the Ministry of Health, Community Development, Gender, Children and Social Services (MoHCDGCS).

### 1.3. Gender issues in land, agriculture and forestry

#### 1.3.1. Gender and land access

Access to land and productive resources are essential to empower women economically. However, in Tanzania, women continue to face discrimination in land rights, despite positive reforms. Though laws may not formally discriminate against women, societal norms interfere with their ability to own or control land.

In rural areas of the country, women hold only 10% of all land ownership amongst women; only 20% of Tanzanian women owned land in their own names. Women who do own land often have smaller plots and less livestock. They also have less access to credit for agricultural production.

The Village Land Act (VLA) was introduced in 1999 with the aim of negating customary laws that discriminated against women's land tenure. The VLA breaks new ground in women’s rights with Section 3(2) and Sections 3, 8, 14, 22, and 20(3), rendering as invalid any customary practices that discriminate against women. It also states (Section 3(2)): "The right of every


---
**Page 227**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Women to acquire, hold, use and deal with, the land shall be to the same extent and subject same conditions as men, treated as a right of any man." The Act also vests the role of customary law in determining land tenure and it recognized women's rights to land in the event of the death of her husband or upon divorce. There are also requirements for female representation in all decision-making bodies related to land use and management. According to the Land Act (No 20 of 2004), it is clearly stated under Section 5 that at least three of the seven members of a Village Land Council should be women. The Land Use Planning Act states that land adjudication committees must have at least one woman member. In addition, in all local government areas there should be at least 25% female representation on VCs (as in the Local Government Act).

Despite a supportive policy environment, Tanzanian women continue to struggle to access land rights. Cultural and religious institutions tend to favor men and may perpetuate the view that women are not entitled to land ownership or control. Women's voices are often generated from it. Should a woman attempt to claim her right to land, household and community members may discourage her from doing so. In some cases, cultural norms perpetuate the patriarchal view that men are the primary decision-makers and as such, should have disproportionate influence in land related decisions and practices related to ownership and control of land.

Insecure land rights offer women little incentive to improve the resilience of their agriculture. Without any assurances, women are hesitant to make investments that would enhance their land's productivity.

### 1.3.2 Gender and agricultural activities

The agricultural sector is a mainstay of Tanzania’s economy, employing nearly 67% of the labour force.50% of rural women are employed in agriculture and they are the main producers of food for their families and regions. Women earn lower wages than men and have lower earnings than rural men despite being more active within the sector.50% of women work as unpaid family labour in agriculture.5 Agriculture accounts for nearly a quarter of Tanzania’s GDP. The majority of women farmers lack access to productive inputs, secure land rights and in access to control of resources (assets, capital, extension services, etc.). Farming is often seen as a woman's job and they are not involved in the commercial nature of work; elderly people, and people with health problems do not typically participate.

Women in Tanzania are more vulnerable to climate change than men, especially within the agricultural sector. This is due to the gender roles and norms but also because of a dependency on rain-fed agriculture. Women's vulnerability to climate change is also exacerbated by their central role in water collection. Increased water scarcity will increase the


---
**Page 228**
---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

time women spend traveling to collect water and result in opportunity costs, including less time to attend school, vocational training, or engage in income generating activities.

The economy and livelihoods of the population in Rwigoma rely on natural resources, in particular agriculture. The camp houses about 90% of the region's population, including refugees, are engaged in rainfed, subsistence agriculture. Farmers groups in Kigoma region, especially in the barangati area, have been supported by the government and NGOs with extension services and microfinance support. The group leader is usually male, whereas supporting leadership (treasurer and some of these secretaries) are women. Some groups supported by NGOs have more female members; experienced women often take on leadership roles.

As noted, refugees also engage in agriculture activities. Within the camps, land plots allocated per household are small. Families engage in small gardening projects around their plots to supplement monthly food rations received. Home gardening in the camp has been restricted to kitchen gardens and vegetable gardens. Vegetable gardens have become a key source of income for women. Women grow vegetables such as tomatoes, onions, potatoes, and beans. The management of the gardens is also largely carried out by women at the household level. They are supporting creative innovative ways of producing vegetables through the use of sack/bins and kitchen gardens to improve dietary diversity.

Refugees also engage in agriculture activities outside the camps. Tanzania follows a strict encampment policy, where informal land-use/land arrangements which mainly involve renting a farm or farming for Tanzanians. Refugee agricultural labourers include both men and women and Congolese and Burundian.


## 1.3.3 Gender and forestry

Women tend to be underrepresented in forest management activities. Gender relations shape participation, and thus reinforce women's vulnerability to deforestation and forest degradation. In order to develop appropriate interventions such as Community Based Forestry (CBF) that address the location-specific vulnerabilities and are context specific, there is need to understand how gender shapes women's participation in CBF. The literature on this topic has largely focused on the accepted role that gender plays in determining a person's vulnerability to deforestation and forest degradation in Tanzania there has been limited emphasis specifically on the linkages between forestry and gender. The hardship brought by deforestation and forest degradation is often experienced more acutely by women, who are responsible for collecting firewood and water.

## Access to finance, information and decision-making

### Rural women in Tanzania have limited decision-making power and access to credit, thus face challenges investing in resilience measures.

#### Only 13% of FHIs have access to credit, as compared with 25% of men. Women also have less control over land than men, which limits their ability to secure finance at the village level.


---

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Rural women have limited access to educational and technological resources. Literacy rates are lower in rural areas of Tanzania, with 39% of women being literate and 13% of men.[2] 49% of rural women have no educational attainment at all, as compared to 40% of rural men.[3]

Though there is evidence to suggest that there are equal levels of participation in climate change adaptation activities between men and women, the lack of access to accurate information between genders suggests that "it is unlikely that there is equality in awareness." [4]

During community focus group discussions notable variations were evident in the level of participation in and the financial contributions and gains obtained from the savings associations. Women appear to be more active participants than men in a number of the numerous savings associations. Particularly women appeared to be well represented in these savings groups. Amounts obtained through the savings groups in some cases are quite substantial, with some women reporting that they have received up to 100,000 Tsh.

The finances obtained through the savings associations are frequently spent on expenses such as school fees, household expenditures, small business enterprises and agriculture inputs.


| The picture obtained in the camps differs slightly. For instance, in Ndara camp the men interviewed were more likely than women to have participated in the savings associations compared with women. There may be some cultural aspects at play whereby one hypothesis is that saving associations in Tanzania are seen as a woman's domain and therefore men may not participate or may not give money to or allow their wives to invest their money in savings associations, whereas refugees may prefer to be participating directly themselves. In the camps it was noted that in some cases, men were more likely than women to have participated in the savings associations, as noted in the host communities. Investments made included agricultural activities (e.g. land leasing) and livestock trading. Women in Mwendei remarked that the importance and strength of the women's group has increased since they started participating in the savings groups and local markets in early 2019. The women explained that food rations are sometimes monetized to be able to participate in the savings associations. In some cases, women report monetizing their ration cards by selling them for food thereby circumventing the cash flow cuts. WFP food rations are expected to further erode the functioning of these savings groups, without additional interventions to support livelihoods.


The community leadership structures within the camps are yet to give a gender active party in decision making positions. The role of the current refugee leader in Nyangwiro is a Congolese woman. At the household level, decision making power also tends to be skewed in favour of men. Community members reported that women were more likely than men to have experienced inequality between husbands and wives on how household money is being spent. Refugee women in Mwendei camp remarked on the impact of the skills training that the refugee community, both men and women, had received, stating that this improved women's position in the household and decision-making power. ## 13.5. Risks of Sexual and Gender-Based Violence

### Kigoma is home to three refugee camps: Nduta, Attendu and Nyarugusu.

As of September 2021, the camps house over 605,000 refugees, approximately two-thirds of whom are women or children and just over half (55 percent) are children. According to UNHCR, 54.3 percent of refugee households in Tanzania are female-headed households.

The major economic activity within the camps is the selling of food rations and non-food items received from WFP. Other income-generating skills-based economic activity in the camps which includes tailoring/embroidering, bartering and hawking, cooking, soap-making, repairs, carpentry or masonry (done primarily by men) and small trade (women). Refugees also undertake agricultural activities, which are largely centred around agriculture. Both women and men engage in farming.

Despite efforts by UNHCR and its partners, refugee women and children continue to be exposed to a range of risks including GBV, child abuse, early and forced marriages. Some of these risks are exacerbated by the social and economic inequalities that exist within refugee communities, which often perpetuate gender-based power imbalances that bring about gender inequality. Gender discrimination often sustains systems that perpetuate violence against women and girls. These include harmful traditional practices such as female genital mutilation (FGM), child marriage, and forced marriages among others. UNHCR GBV reports between January and September 2021 indicate that the majority of GBV cases were reported by women and girls with GBV including physical abuse (intimate partner violence), rape, emotional/psychological abuse, denial of resources and opportunities and forced/child marriages. The risk of GBV are faced both within and outside refugee camps. UNHCR has established GBV multistakeholder referral systems to support all GBV survivors based on their needs.

Refugees living in the camps are particularly exposed to violence, including GBV when they leave the camps to search for work, to trade, or to gather firewood in the villages near the camps. The lack of access to safe water and sanitation facilities within refugee camps also experience occasional violence, leading to conflict and mistrust, although this is more acute for the Burundians than for the more established Congolese. Refugee children inside the camps are particularly vulnerable to sexual abuse and exploitation due to their vulnerability and exposure to GBV. The lack of access to education and opportunities for income generation due to the congested living conditions especially in collective shelters, and the limited opportunities for self-reliance among refugees, consequently increasing their vulnerability to violence. The economic challenges faced by refugees, including restrictions on cash-based interventions and the limited livelihood opportunities have further complicated the search for self-reliance among refugees.

Climate resilient development in refugee camps and host communities in Kigoma, Tanzania
