# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

particular risky activity and exposes women to protection issues, especially GBV. Firewood collection is a gendered activity and is mostly undertaken by women. Although refugees are technically only permitted to travel within a 4km buffer around the camps to collect wood fuel, these areas cannot presently meet the supply needs for the camp populations, forcing women to travel further into host communities where they may be exposed to GBV. Women engaged in collection risk violence, torture, rape and murder perpetrated by locals. A 2015 field report from Refugees International found that 22% of DRC GBV incidents reported by Burundian refugees were related to firewood collection, with 73% of these women reporting trips, and 90% of these incidents were rape or sexual assault. Although culturally a gendered role, women are often forced into this activity due to the lack of wood fuel in host communities, whereas male refugees collecting firewood are perceived as 'stealing' and may be violently attacked. Men who sometimes accompany the women during firewood collection are also at risk of GBV, including sexual assault as culturally this would make them seem weak and incapable of defending themselves as well as their loved ones. As such, statistics in this regard are not readily available.

Within the camp setting, a number of other factors can be identified that exacerbate the risk of GBV among women, which include:

- Alcohol use and abuse
- Family conflict and intimate partner violence, particularly where there is dissent about voluntary return to country of origin
- Household economic insecurity
- Poor lighting in camps leading to insecurity
- Restrictions of freedom of movement and livelihood activities, which contribute to increased vulnerability to GBV, including work and transactional sex
- Empowerment projects where female household members begin to earn money, or save through VSLA, which can trigger inter-partner violence from men

Peaceful co-existence meetings are held on a quarterly basis between representatives from the refugee community and local law enforcement officers. These forums systematically address GBV issues and incidences, often related to firewood collection, and have contributed to sensitizing the host and refugee communities and mitigating any conflicts that could jeopardize the relationship between them. The presence of women in these meetings as well as the inclusion of perpetrators also feature in the peaceful coexistence meetings as the local law enforcement officers with jurisdiction outside the camps are also invited to these forums. Camp law enforcement officers are also present during these meetings to ensure cooperation with local authorities.

Preventive measures in place include promotion of lockable doors in shelters use of partitions to improve privacy, proper design of sanitary facilities, and engaging men and community leaders in camp committees to promote gender equality. Economic programs aimed at reducing food rations are reported to reduce engagement in risky coping strategies. While limited at


---
**Page 233**
---

# Gender Sensitivity and Action Plan

The moment, long-term investments in livelihood activities for income generation and alternative fuel sources are aimed at reducing SGBV risks.

Inter-agency standard operating procedures (SOPs) exist for managing risks of sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV), including sexual exploitation, abuse, and harassment (SEA)**. The SEA SOP protocol covers roles and responsibilities, task force membership, procedures for reporting incidents, confidentiality, data collection and analysis, confidentiality, reporting to national authorities, integrity code of conduct and other processes.

A community-based feedback mechanism has been set up in all the camps where help desks are staffed by different humanitarian partners. Refugees can report incidents as well as obtain information on SGBV services through these desks. The desks also provide a means for people to email depending on the preference of the complainant. Complaint boxes have been established in all three camps to receive feedback from persons of concern and are managed by UNHCR. In addition, complaints can be made directly to UNHCR. Complaints including feedback from complaint boxes have also been established and are operational.

Data is collected in an information management system. Incidents of sexual exploitation and abuse have been reported by persons of concern where the perpetrators are security personnel. These incidents are recorded as SGBV incidents, but the perpetrators are also treated as sexual exploiters and abusers. UNHCR is also starting to develop disability inclusion in SGBV programming, such as developing tools to support non-verbal communication with a view to make SGBV referral pathways more accessible to people with disability.

Moreover, UNHCR activities prioritized for 2021 include mobilizing the refugee community in awareness-raising campaigns on SGBV prevention and response. In addition, capacity building will be provided for staff and partners, including legal staff, to provide survivors of sexual violence with legal recourse including mobile courts. To address psycho-social needs GBV survivors will be provided with counseling. These are parallel activities that will be done by UNHCR, and that will be coordinated through the SGBV Working Group. The Working Group will also continue work on SGBV in training activities and increasing awareness on incident reporting mechanisms.

Outside the camps in host community settings, the Kigoma Joint Programme has undertaken and planned under the theme "Violence Against Women and Children (VAWC)", under the leadership of the United Nations High Commissioner for Migration (IOM), UN Population Fund (UNPFPA), UNICEF, and UNFPA. The VAWC theme obtained funding of $1,129,617 in 2019-2020. Under this funding, the main achievements include:

*The Inter-agency standard operating procedures for prevention and response to gender-based violence in Nyarugusu, Nduta and Morogoro refugee camps, Tanzania, was launched in April 2018 and is periodically reviewed.*

The Working Group on SGBV includes representatives from UNHCR, IOM, UNICEF, UNPFPA, the International Rescue Committee, Caritas Kigoma Diocese, ICRC, Norwegian Refugee Council, Danish Refugee Council, Save the Children, World Vision, CARE, Concern Worldwide, Oxfam, Mercy Corps, Catholic Relief Services, HelpAge International, African Initiatives for Relief and Development, Tanzania Ministry of Home Affairs, Women's Legal Aid Centre, REDDOS, Boma Mission Centre, and Tanganyika Christian Relief Services.

## Priorities

Priorities include strengthening of coordination structures, developing online training, improving the capacity of Protection Committees to identify GBV cases, and building up actors to prevent violence. In support of these activities, the project's gender action plan will include activities to involve SGBV modules for women and men participants of training activities, as well as sensitization campaigns in schools and communities. The plan also includes lessons learned from the community feedback mechanism and complaints boxes within the same year.

## Community Safety

In the local communities of Kigoma Region, the most commonly reported types of SGBV are physical violence, emotional violence, economic violence, human trafficking and child labour. Several factors contribute to this situation: SEARCA (Social and Economic Analysis) indicates that cultural norms perpetuate practices like early marriage and gender-based violence; high poverty levels and limited livelihood opportunities increase vulnerability; insufficient infrastructure for women's access to services; and low awareness of support service hinder survivors' ability to seek help, overburdening communities. These factors contribute to the risk.

## Government Efforts

The government of Tanzania has taken measures to prevent and address GBV and SEARH at all levels. The government adopted a National Plan of Action and established Women and Children Protection Committees at various levels, staffed by professionals from different backgrounds. These committees serve as primary points for collecting cases of violence against women and children. One Stop Centers, including four in the Kigoma region, act as referral points and provide services to survivors. However, more centers are needed throughout the country.

## Awareness Programs

To raise awareness, sensitization programs are conducted by various entities, including Women and Children Protection Committees, Police Gender Unit, Women and Children Desk, and civil society organizations. Reporting mechanisms are in place, allowing victims to report to the committees, social welfare officers, police, and health officers. There is also a toll-free number available for reporting SGBV cases, ensuring confidentiality and anonymity.

## Case Management

For case management and referral, victims are referred to receive clinical and psychosocial support through One Stop Centers, Police Gender Unit, Children Desks, Women and Children Protection Committees, and civil society organizations. Psychological counseling is provided by professionals in healthcare facilities or One Stop Centers.

## 1.4. Gender considerations in the project design

### 1.4.1. Approach and problem analysis

Without a thorough understanding of the specific roles, responsibilities and opportunities that are ascribed to women and men, the project’s activities may add to rather than reduce poverty (the opposite is true for women who have more time for income-generating activities or household work which could otherwise have been spent on economically remunerative activities). From the overview of gender issues above, it becomes clear that women, in particular, are disproportionately affected by climate change and variability for a substantial number of households tasks. This entails productive and reproductive tasks and includes access to land, water, agricultural inputs, seeds, tools, markets, credit, information, food, water, food preparation and child care. Through the implementation of the project activities, women are expected to adopt climate-resilient agricultural techniques, sustainable forest management practices and alternative livelihoods – altering in and some cases increasing their time-use.

The assessment of gender issues prevalent among host communities and refugees in Kigoma region, and the considerations on gender-related issues for forestry, agriculture and land use have been used to identify the following root causes, causes, immediate effects and effects. The table below provides a problem analysis with regards to gender issues, while the following sections describe how these are mainstreamed into the project design.

| Root causes | Causes | Immediate effects | Effects |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Insecure land tenure and exclusion from access and control over land | Gender blind policies that discriminate socially and culturally against women in relation to land use, agriculture, water and forestry | Limited adaptive capacity to climate change and insufficient resilience measures | Lower productivity and income generated from natural resources |
| Women's low level of participation in decision-making processes related to land use, agriculture, water and limited access for women farmers groups | Lack of knowledge on how to diversify livelihoods in a climate-resilient way | Limited access to income-generating opportunities | Women have limited adaptive capacity to engage in income-generating activities |
| Gender gaps in women's access to information and knowledge | Greater demand on women to collect firewood and fetch water for domestic use and irrigation | Lower productivity and income generated from natural resources | Women have less income and are economically compromised, food |

# Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## 1.4.2. Gender mainstreaming in the project design

### Gender mainstreaming means ensuring gender equality and support to both women's and men's participation, contribution and benefits, and its ultimate goal is to achieve gender equality.

The rationale for considering gender issues in the project is that men and women not only play different roles in the communities, with distinct levels of control over resources, but they also have different needs. Gender mainstreaming is a strategy used within an integrated development strategy intended to reverse natural resource depletion in general and address ecosystem services too. Thus, to address gender inequalities, it is of crucial importance to consider the particular needs of women in the framework of ecosystem-based adaptation activities.

It is also necessary to remain sensitive to not exposing women to greater risk as a result of their empowerment through project activities. Unless identified that, if managed poorly, empowerment processes within which women begin to participate and gain confidence can expose them to inter-partner violence, as male partners struggle to accept these changes. The project team must therefore be aware of the potential for change in these dynamics, to ensure women are sufficiently capacitated to exercise their agency and empowerment, and that male community members are not "left behind" but are engaged participants in women's empowerment.

The gender analysis and problem analysis identified gaps in access and control over resources and opportunities. Mainstreaming gender in the project design is achieved through the use of sex-disaggregated data, together with developing strategies and actions to close those gaps. The VLPD provides a framework for implementing equality strategies and monitoring results using gender-sensitive indicators.

The VLPD processes supported by the project in host communities under output 1 are participatory in nature and the national guidelines provide suggestions to ensure that women are represented on all committees and in all decision-making processes. These efforts are detailed in the gender action plan. The VLPD seeks to include agreements that change traditional land management practices, thus improving the position of women in controlling land and natural resources.

Mainstreaming gender considerations in forestry-related activities can be done at different levels[15] and the project's gender mainstreaming activities under output 2 will build on good practices that have proven effective. The project will ensure that staff who provide training (e.g., extension officers) are trained to address gender issues, including challenging socio-cultural norms that inhibit women’s participation in the project forestry activities. Also, project aims to set aside land for jointly managing forests for sustainable harvest of fuelwood

### 1.4.3 Project benefits promoting gender equality

The project's gender sensitive approach seeks to achieve practical and tangible benefits to women as well as bring about strategic changes in gender relations. For women to meaningfully benefit from the project intervention it is crucial they are included in the various decision-making processes and spaces. Additionally, women’s needs, interests and priorities will be captured through participatory gender analysis and monitoring adaptation interventions. To address this inequality, the project will work to ensure women are included in the activities of Output 4, their needs, knowledge and interests are captured, and communication products are generated with appropriate content, design and distribution.

Women rely heavily on natural resources to be able to fulfil their various productive and reproductive tasks. Women, like men, engage in agriculture activities and by ensuring they
